<meta>Continuing from the catalogue description of Manuscript 2231, this page marks the beginning of the primary text: Simeon Seth’s "On the Properties of Foods." The transcription begins with a Latin header, likely added by a later librarian, followed by the author's formal Greek introduction dedicated to the Emperor.</meta>

<insert>
<unclear>Peace and ...</unclear>
An alphabetical exposition on food and drink, written by Simeon, Master of Antioch <unclear>Antioch</unclear> <unclear>... </unclear> addressed to the Emperor Michael Doukas. <note>original Latin: "Expositio per alphabetum, de cibis et potibus scripta a Symeone magistro antiochiae... ad Michaelem imperatorem ducam." Michael VII Doukas reigned from 1071 to 1078.</note>
</insert>

<margin><unclear>...</unclear></margin>
<unclear>In these [writings] he also composed in sections regarding foods [not to be ignored], so that through a healing reminder of sorts</unclear> <note>The marginalia seems to be a summary or commentary on the purpose of the medical text.</note>

For the benefit of many others, and especially for my "sun," <note>A common Byzantine honorific for the Emperor.</note> my Lord and Master, I offer not only my lips but also my willing spirit. I have harmonized this composition—mine and yours—concerning the specific <term>properties</term> <note>original: "idiomaton"; referring to the inherent qualities or "powers" of different substances in Galenic medicine.</note> of foods for the sake of health, directed toward your Highness. For all those who are governed by the <term>regimen of the body</term>, <note>original: "diaite"; the origin of the word "diet," referring to a total lifestyle and nutritional balance.</note> I have gathered those things which pertain to beauty and truth. And I pray to the Ruler of all things for the strength of your kingdom, so that through your [patronage], all might know the power and the specific nature of each food. It is useful to set these things down so they may contribute to one’s own well-being. For such a presentation will serve as a sacred service and a benefit to the world, releasing to the world that which is pleasant and useful, for the delight of the King of men and for health and longevity. Since I believe that the <term>alterations</term> <note>The process by which food is transformed into bodily humor.</note> of foods have perhaps not yet been fully understood everywhere, I shall list the ancient names alongside all the more recent and familiar ones, so that the subject of one's search may be easily found. I have also added to this discourse matters concerning taste, smell, and aromatics, such as familiar perfumes. We must begin, therefore, from this point:

From **lamb meat**: it is a <term>moderately warm</term> <note>Galenic medicine classified foods by "degrees" of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness.</note> part...

<meta>Continuing from the introductory dedication to Emperor Michael VII Doukas, this page begins the alphabetical entries of the treatise, starting with foods beginning with the letter Alpha: Almonds (Amygdala), Pears (Apidia), Cucumbers (Angouria), and Apples (Mala).</meta>

Concerning almonds and the nature of their moisture <unclear>type</unclear>. 
They are harmful to those who have an [excessively hot] <term>temperament</term>; <note>The "temperament" or "krasis" refers to the balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—within an individual.</note> 
this is resolved by... and as much as 
the almond is more bitter, so much is it "sweeter" <note>original: "glykyteron"; likely meaning more beneficial or refined in its medicinal action rather than its taste</note> 
and more easily digested. The larger they are, 
the more digestible and the less moist they become. 
The same applies to other living things: the larger ones 
are more watery than the small ones. 
Almonds are moderate in heat. 
They are nourishing, especially the <term>skins</term> <note>original: "cheide"; referring to the thin brown skin of the nut</note> which have 
a thinning and <term>cutting</term> power. <note>original: "tmētikēs"; a medical term for substances that break down thick or viscous humors.</note> Through the internal organs 
and the chest, they act as cleansers. If they are cooked twice, 
they become hotter, finer in substance, 
and more "cutting." They clear away blockages 
in the fine parts, specifically the <unclear>wrinkled</unclear> <unclear>obstructions</unclear> 
in the liver; they also crush stones that occur in the kidneys. 
They also act as a remedy if one uses an "almond-wash" 
for someone who has been drinking wine heavily that day or is a 
habitual wine-drinker. Almond oil itself is also 
moderate in heat; for blockages in the liver, 
it is an <term>obstructive-cleanser</term> <note>original: "apophraktikon"; a substance that opens up (de-obstructs) passages.</note> and it provides relief for 
the lungs and the chest. The oil of bitter almonds 
is particularly effective at clearing blockages, is <term>diuretic</term>, <note>Promotes the production of urine.</note> 
crushes stones, and [reduces] swellings.

Pears are cold in the first degree 
and dry in the second. <note>Galenic medicine categorized foods and medicines by four degrees of intensity for the primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note> One must...

<column-break/>

When they are ripe, they partake of a certain sweetness and 
heat. Along with the sweet ones, they have a sweet and 
astringent quality. For this reason, they also 
constrict the belly if eaten before a meal, especially the 
astringent ones. They are cohesive and [satiating] 
likewise, but can cause colic because of their placement in the stomach. 
They provide relief for "hot" stomachs. The seeds 
themselves are beneficial for those suffering 
from lung ailments, but they are harmful to the kidneys.

Cucumbers are cold and moist in the second 
degree. For those with cold and moist temperaments 
[they are problematic]... One should 
choose the small ones... and their seeds. 
They are diuretic and [moderate] the heat 
in those with short-lived fevers. They make the heat 
more balanced, especially... 
Frequent use of these, however, reduces the <term>seed</term> <note>original: "gonēn"; referring to semen and reproductive vigor.</note> 
and the sexual impulse. But the seed of these, when dried, 
is then somewhat warmer and [more potent], 
and we find them to be more diuretic.

Apples are cold and moist in the second 
degree. They produce a sticky and cold 
<term>humor</term>. <note>original: "chymon"; the bodily fluids that determine health.</note> They are also hard to digest. Some of them 
are quite <unclear>unpleasant</unclear>. By their nature they [can cause] 
chills and sore throats. One should eat those that are ripe...

<summary>This page lists the dietary and medicinal properties of almonds, pears, cucumbers, and apples according to Galenic humorism. It details how these foods affect specific organs like the liver, kidneys, and lungs, and their impact on the body's internal heat and moisture.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>Continuing the alphabetical exploration of foods and their medicinal properties, this page concludes the discussion on almonds (amygdala) and transitions into the properties of a specific, though somewhat unidentified, bitter vegetable or herb, likely within the Alpha section of the herbarium.</meta>

...with very hot and pungent seasonings. While these provide pleasure to some, one must guard against over-indulgence, as the harm arising from such seasonings is especially prone to strike the body. This is particularly true in winter for those with a <term>mixed temperament</term> <note>The "krasis" or balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which determined a person's health and personality.</note>. Because of their heat, these seasonings are <term>cutting</term> <note>original: "tmētika"; medical substances that break down thick or viscous humors like phlegm</note> and their effects on the bowels are clearly visible. Even the hooves of horses, if they were ground down, would behave similarly; if they are softened and digested, they create blockages in the liver or the internal organs. The humors provide evidence of this cooling effect. If it should happen that someone eats an excessive amount of almonds, they should drink a <term>honey-mead</term> <note>original: "melikraton"; a common medicinal drink made of honey and water</note> made with lukewarm water, and the womb <note>original: "mētron"; though usually referring to the uterus, in some medical contexts it can refer to the core or "mother" of an organ system</note> will thus be healed. +

<margin>Benefit</margin>
The <term>uninitiated</term> <note>original: "amyēton"; likely referring here to the unpeeled or raw state of the nut</note> variety is warming to the limbs and easily digested; a poultice made from it works beyond expectation. It is beneficial against <term>flatulence</term> <note>original: "empneumatōseis"; the inflation of the body caused by "trapped winds" or gas</note> caused by thick humors. A honeyed preparation is used for difficulty in urinating; and the juice mixed with honey is a remedy against... performing a heating and digestive action. Wild varieties are gas-producing but <term>dispersive</term> <note>original: "diaphorētika"; aiding in the dispersal of harmful substances through the skin or breath</note> for the liver... whereas those that are thicker are harder to digest and more "shining" in appearance. The oily variety is both dispersive and acts as a sleep-aid. Raw bitter ones are not beneficial to eat, but when applied as an ointment, they are helpful. In the stomach, they disperse gas; and for a body that has been over-exerted, they are therapeutic.
Regarding thirst: it is warming and drying. A poultice used every three days benefits heat in the spleen or the body, or conditions of the liver.

<column-break/>

...dispersive of <term>phlegm</term> <note>The cold, moist humor associated with winter and sluggishness</note> and relaxing to the tissues; for it is also a stimulant and promotes the production of milk.
As for this kind of vegetable, which is not well known to us, or perhaps is brought to us in this form—part of it is bitter. What is called "black [smell]" is unstable in its fragrances. It is mostly harvested according to the season of the air, and its irregularities are well known. If there is much soil, the whole plant becomes bitter and nourishing. One should not describe its nature simply; when raw, it is very juicy because of its nutritive quality. It is said, and not far from the truth, that it turns into flesh. It is <term>diuretic</term> by nature; and if there is a blockage in the parts of the liver, it acts as a <term>de-obstruent</term> <note>original: "apophraktikon"; a medicine that clears obstructions in the vessels or organs</note>. This is evident from the fact that it does not suppress the discharge of urine. This is experienced by those who eat it in any quantity, just as with <term>silphium</term> <note>A legendary medicinal plant from Cyrene, highly prized for its contraceptive and digestive properties, believed by the Middle Ages to have been lost or become extremely rare.</note>. 
<margin>v</margin>
Let no one wonder if it immediately imparts its own quality to the urine. For behold, what is called <term>chemistry</term> <note>original: "chēmeia"; here referring to the natural transformation and interaction of substances within the body</note> occurs easily; even without being applied by the hands, it changes the urine or its specific color. This seems paradoxical—that external heat should warm the <unclear>pathway</unclear>—but these matters are outside our present purpose. 
Let us proceed to the next points. It dissolves certain blockages of the bowels and obstructive conditions caused by phlegm. "Cuttings" <note>incisions or perhaps "thinning" medications</note> are also applied, and these are often converted into blood quite quickly. They are also beneficial for <term>menstrual flow</term> and for palpitations of the heart. The juice is also helpful...

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal use of almonds and a bitter vegetable (likely chicory or a similar herb) to clear liver and spleen blockages and treat flatulence. It highlights the "cutting" nature of these substances and compares their diuretic effects to the famous ancient plant silphium.</summary>
<keywords>almonds, liver blockages, humors, flatulence, diuretic, silphium, honey-mead, digestive health, heart palpitations</keywords>

...and of a similar kind. The better variety is tawny and oily, which is provided in a certain Indian city called <term>silachito</term> <note>original: "silachito"; likely a transliterated local name for a specific resin or aromatic substance</note>. The second kind is harvested in a small coastal town in Arabia [called] <unclear>Adeisimopotar</unclear>; it is named <term>sichrion</term>. The inferior kind is black, composed of fish [remnants] and the waste of **ambergris** <note>original: "ampar"; a valuable substance from the digestive system of sperm whales, used in ancient medicine and perfumes</note>. It has a <term>warming</term> property and is <term>cutting</term> <note>original: "tmētikēn"; medical substances believed to thin or break down thick, sticky fluids (humors) in the body</note> to the humors. Because of this, some add it to stomach remedies. It affects the head and the heart; even just smelling it while drinking brings on drunkenness quickly; if it is mixed into the wine, even more so.

---

### Element B
<note>Refers to the start of the section of the text organized under the Greek letter Beta (B).</note>

->Beef<-

**Beef** is more nourishing [than other meats]; however, the blood produced from it is naturally thicker than what is balanced. For this reason, it produces various <term>melancholic</term> <note>original: "melancholikas"; diseases related to an excess of "black bile," one of the four humors, often associated with heaviness, skin conditions, or low spirits</note> diseases in those with a <term>melancholic temperament</term>. Even if it is hard to digest and hard to eliminate, it nourishes sufficiently. When compared to **mutton** <note>original: "probateion kreas"; the meat of sheep</note>, it is colder and more prone to producing black bile. Therefore, beef broth stops **diarrhea** <note>original: "gastrorrhoia"</note> caused by yellow bile. If someone desires this meat or is <unclear>compelled</unclear> to eat it, let them treat the expected harm from it with vinegar, garlic, and **rue** <note>original: "pēganō"; a bitter herb used in ancient times as a medicinal antidote to "heavy" foods</note>. Furthermore, in addition to what has been said, it either produces <term>bad juices</term> <note>original: "kakochymon"; poor quality humors that were thought to cause illness</note> alone, or <unclear>... ... ...</unclear> for those who exercise vigorously and throughout the day.

->Butter<-

**Butter** is moderately warm and nourishing.

<summary>This page covers the dietary and medicinal properties of lobsters, onions, and ambergris before beginning the alphabetical section for the letter 'B', detailing the "heavy" nature of beef and the warming qualities of butter.</summary>
<keywords>lobsters, onions, mustard, ambergris, beef, mutton, butter, melancholy, humors, ancient medicine</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical discourse from the previous discussion on almonds and oils, specifically detailing the respiratory and digestive benefits of certain oils before moving into the properties of apricots and the production and testing of balsam. This marks the transition into the "Beta" section of the alphabetical materia medica.</meta>

It benefits the lungs and the chest and works together to easily expel phlegm <note>original: "ptuelon"</note> from them; it is also useful against coughs <note>original: "bēchas"</note> arising from coldness and dryness. When used sufficiently, it moves the bowels; and the older it is, the more nourishing it is by nature than any other oil. It possesses both a digestive and a dispersive power, especially upon soft bodies. For this reason, it is also applied to hard swellings behind the ears <note>original: "parōtidas"; these are the parotid glands</note> or to <unclear>abscesses</unclear>. It also ceases the pain of teething in children who are cutting teeth <note>original: "odontophuountōn"</note>. When it is frequently applied with wool to the <unclear>parts</unclear>, it <unclear>relieves</unclear>... however, it causes a lack of appetite in warmer stomachs, as it often changes into yellow bile <note>original: "xanthēn cholēn"; one of the four humors of ancient medicine</note>. 

### Apricots and Armenian Plums
The power of **apricots** <note>original: "berikokkōn"</note> and **Armenian plums** <note>original: "armeniakōn"</note> is such that they are easily spoiled. The "blood" <note>likely referring to the juice or the humor produced</note> that flows from them is of poor quality. Because they are cold and moist <gloss>the second qualities</gloss>, they are more succulent than peaches <note>original: "erdakiōn," likely a variant of "rhodakinōn"</note>. They move the bowels and cease yellow bile, provided they are not completely ripe. If consumed too frequently, they produce fevers. One must consume them before other food; for if they are eaten on top of other food, they float above it and spoil the meals due to their easily-perishable nature.

<column-break/>

### Balsam
**Balsam** <note>original: "balsamon"</note> is a small plant, less than two cubits <note>a cubit is roughly the length of a forearm, about 18 inches</note> in height. it grows near its source in a region called the **City of the Sun** <note>Heliopolis, in Egypt</note> by the ancients. When it is harvested by making incisions during the autumn season, the final incision is made using iron <note>ancient sources often debated whether iron or stone tools should be used on balsam plants</note>... 

...on parts of such plants. If, because of this, a sap flows from it which is similar to oil, it is called **balsam-oil** <note>original: "balsamelaion"</note>. It is stored with its resin in vessels and kept pure. Indeed, that which is for the most part white, fine-grained, and very fragrant is, by itself, much more precious. The middle grade is yellowish and pure, though it remains <unclear>thick</unclear> for a long time. Some use it for <unclear>ailments</unclear> of the hands and readily for treatments when other remedies are lacking. 

They test the quality of this scent by whether a drop <note>original: "dakry," literally "a tear"</note> spreads out; for this reason, it is used for anointing and as an offering. This is also a trait of what is called pure oil. Others test it by seeing if it does not stay on top of the water but instead sinks to the bottom; however, this can also happen with other oily substances. If both tests are met—the drop spreading and <unclear>sinking</unclear>—it is snatched up from the water... furthermore, it is tested by dipping a cloth <note>original: "othomēs"</note> into it and hanging it up; if the color does not stay on the cloth itself, but only a <unclear>residue</unclear> is left behind. 

It is beneficial for the blood. Secondly, it is given for many ailments mixed with woman’s milk to those who have swallowed poisons <note>original: "dēlētēriois"</note> or have been bitten by venomous creatures, acting as a healing remedy. It is especially effective for scorpion stings. It is either drunk or applied as an ointment. The Persians say it even crushes stones <note>likely kidney or bladder stones</note>. It also aids those unable to conceive <note>original: "asyllēptois"</note> when it is applied or used as a fumigant over a fire.

<summary>This page describes the medicinal uses of almond oil for respiratory and teething pain, the dietary precautions for apricots, and the botanical origins, purity tests, and anti-venom applications of balsam.</summary>
<keywords>almond oil, phlegm, teething, apricots, Armenian plums, yellow bile, balsam, Heliopolis, balsam-oil, scorpion stings, infertility</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical manual's transition through the alphabetical catalog of remedies. Having finished the section on apricots and plums, it now addresses a variety of substances starting with the letter Beta—specifically "basilika" (basil) and "birds" (eggs and bile)—before moving into a significant discourse on the nature of milk and its derivatives like cheese and butter.</meta>

For hemorrhoids, they say the **sea fish** <note>original: "halion ichthyn"</note> is useful, as well as for epilepsy; it is very warming for cold ailments.

### Royal Seeds (Basil)
**Royal seeds** <note>original: "ōa basilika"; while literally "royal eggs," the description of smelling it for the heart and the use of its "seed" confirms this is Basil (Ocimum basilicum)</note> possess a balanced heat. It benefits those who smell it, aiding both the head and the heart. When soaked in water, it makes the constitution more moist and induces sleep. Its seed is a remedy for heart ailments and for the despondency <note>original: "dysthymias"</note> arising from **black bile** <term>one of the four humors, associated with melancholy</term>; it produces cheerfulness and good spirits.

### Partridge and Ostrich
**Partridge eggs** <note>original: "ōa perdikos"</note>, when sufficiently heated by fire, warm the constitution from the outside and help with <unclear>contusions</unclear>. If these are eaten by themselves daily—the size of a signet ring—they make the blood of the body thicker and melancholic. Therefore, their broth clears the voice; it is also used against certain <unclear>fatalities</unclear>. They say that the bile of the **ostrich** <note>original: "strouthiou"</note>, when mixed with black vine juice and applied to those suffering from irritation, will prevent a final demise. Some also use its bile for eye conditions. It is said that the oily substance floating on top after boiling it is suitable for those hard of hearing <term>tinnitus or deafness</term> so that it might heal the <unclear>eyes</unclear> <note>The text says "eyes" here, though the context suggests "ears"</note>. The fat found within them, taken in a reed with squill vinegar, acts as a simple and firm remedy for those suffering from [constriction].

### The Nature of Milk
Such a spirit <note>referring to the essence of milk</note> pervades the whole world, not just staying in the head as a migraine. It is composed of three substances:
1.  The **watery whey** <note>original: "orōdous"</note>, which is thin and cleansing, and is therefore sometimes naturally purgative.
2.  The **cheesy curd** <note>original: "pyrōdous," literally "fiery" or "pus-like," but used here for the solidifying element of milk</note>, which is naturally thick and stays in the stomach.
3.  The **buttery part** <note>original: "boutyrōdous"</note>, which is the middle state of the constitution and the mixture.

<column-break/>

The nature of the whey is found in the cheesy parts. Therefore, milks that abound in the watery whey are easily passed through the bowels. However, those that abound in the curd-like part cause <unclear>stealthy</unclear> obstructions in the internal organs <note>original: "splangchnois"</note>, resulting in the formation of stones <term>calculi or kidney stones</term>.

It is used for those with consumption <term>phthisis</term> and coughs, and for those overcome by poisonous drinks, bites, and the **viper** <note>original: "echidnēs"</note>. Such things are prepared in wine or beer, though it is not altogether the best for <unclear>fleshy growths</unclear>. Add also honey <unclear>without fat</unclear> from which one might <unclear>treat</unclear> the vision. For those who fall into sharp rheumatisms of the eye, this is persuasive and does the best work. It also serves for the breath and the chest.

It is not diuretic and is flatulent; but the <unclear>curds</unclear> are digestive and slow regarding the lungs and the chest. We use it more frequently for those suffering from the primary humor and obstructions.

...and causes the belly to flow. But you should not praise **melons** <note>original: "pepona"</note> generally, though they are encouraging to those who are exhausted. The same applies to **pomegranates** <note>original: "rhoidōn"</note>. 

### The Poppy
Regarding the **poppy** <note>original: "mēkōna"</note> itself and the fruit of the poppy: they stir one more toward sexual desire. It is said that this seed is also made into a drink, which makes those who use it bright and more beautiful. Likewise, some place it under their bedding to aid sleep, and they will not be overcome. 

Regarding the "bromic" fruit <note>likely referring to a food-grade or fodder-like variety</note>, its parts are used because they honor the poppy. It provides a hotter impulse than the first indications, and also the second. These poppies cut through **phlegm** <term>one of the four humors, associated with cold and moisture</term> and thick humors. It encourages the belly moderately when applied externally. Regarding the "poppy-struck" <note>possibly those suffering from poppy-induced stupor or heavy lethargy</note> sand-makers <note>those with kidney or bladder stones</note>: they should take it with water and the juice of a **rose**; this may also be used as an ointment for hardened external ulcers. <unclear>And</unclear> the <unclear>Arcadian</unclear> variety and those specifically called <unclear>apple-quinces</unclear>. 

The juice of their flesh is nutritious compared to other more harmful things; they are beneficial against ailments and help produce a healthy body. However, they are resistant to the body’s waste products; for they are used for **colic** <term>severe abdominal pain</term> and **epileptic** conditions. They use the transplanted and dried parts for the nerves in the same way.

<insert>P</insert> **Plums** <note>original: "Damaskēna"; Damson plums</note> are cold and moist in the second degree. They benefit those who have a hot and dry belly. They stimulate the stomach to move due to the moisture they introduce, especially if they are strong. This is particularly true of the larger ones. However, the **Kakkabia** <note>a specific variety of dark plum</note> varieties are drying and strong for movement or for those who are barren, because they affect the head, especially in **phlegmatic** people. Indeed, 

---

they are very delightful. **White plums** promote easy breathing and are effective at moving the bowels. Large amounts of their juices cut through thick humors. For this reason, they are used in other vessels; but regarding the rest, it is as I say. He says that those that are harsh, astringent, and not ripe will instead restrain the belly. 

But the broth of plums, when drunk, completely relieves **dysentery** <term>severe diarrhea with blood</term>. It also treats the inflammations of fever and the eruptions upon the gums when drunk or held in the mouth. It is also good for those with "hot flesh" and internal storms. The stone of the plum is dry and has a power that thins and pierces; because of this, they say it is not only taken as food, but it also crushes **stones** <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>. It benefits the bladder and the chest but harms the stomach. The drink made from aged plums is superior.

-> + White Orach + <-

<insert>P</insert> **White Orach** <note>original: "Laukia"; likely *Atriplex hortensis* or a similar leafy green</note> is hot in the second degree and moist in the first. They are less nourishing than other leafy vegetables. They are **diuretic** <term>promoting the passage of urine</term> because of their lightness, yet they are difficult to digest. This is especially true when eaten raw, because they are prone to producing waste, and they are no less effective at inducing the flow of menstrual blood from the loins. They are productive for the eyesight and warm the infant. The dug-up portions of the pale variety are stirred by the heat of their color; they should be used in moderation. They thin the thick humors in those who are "thick-fleshed" <note>overweight or possessing dense tissue</note>. Their seed, however, is more diuretic. Taking it may interfere with the movement of the menses. It is of no benefit in that it stands against head-ailments; but it is protected and shaded for this reason with honey, making it easy to manage.

<meta>This page continues the dietary and medical advice from the previous section. While the previous page discussed poppies and fruits, this page transitions into animal products—specifically honey, venison (deer), and kid (goat)—before moving into legumes, focusing on chickpeas.</meta>

...regarding the things mentioned in the section on **honey** <note>original: "meli"</note>: that sharp seasonings <note>original: "drimia artymata"</note> are added to it; and it is very warming, making it more digestible, <insert>for example</insert> beneficial for the **coldness of the liver** <term>in Byzantine medicine, a "cold" liver was thought to cause poor digestion and sluggishness</term>. It also dissipates gases and can be applied to the mouth.

### Venison
The **meat of deer** <note>original: "elaphōn krea"</note> produces good juices <term>chyme; the semi-fluid mass of partly digested food</term> and is easy to digest; it does not particularly generate **yellow bile** <term>one of the four humors, associated with heat and aggression</term>. However, one must be careful with those hunted during the summer; since they often feed on snakes and vipers, they become poisonous. The wild deer does not drink water to help digest these, and so they die; for this reason, they endure thirst. For if their **internal heat** <note>original: "kokkinos"</note> is inflamed during that season when they are taken, they are harmful and foul. Therefore, one must be cautious regarding a diet of them. It appears they are not eaten in winter; for at such a time, they flee and hide because of their internal heat. If **venison** <note>original: "elapheia krea"</note> is not well digested, it sends out a thick humor. It also creates blockages in both the liver and the spleen. If eaten in small amounts but constantly and without moderation, it makes the body tremulous and unstable. However, the marrow and the fat soften the hardness of **tumors** <note>original: "karkinōmatōn"; refers to hard, crab-like swellings</note> through their heat. Its **horn** <note>original: "keras"</note> is cooling; and when used during a state of "frenzy" <note>original: "thermothermosis"</note> or with vinegar, it drives away apparitions. It is also used for those who have lost their minds <note>suffering from "paraphronēsis"</note>, for blockages, and for **worms** <note>original: "skōlēkas"</note> or intestinal parasites. It is also beneficial for the lungs. They say it even assists in **difficult childbirth** <note>original: "dystokia"</note>. Similarly, deer blood, when dried and thickened, is used against the bites of venomous beasts.

<column-break/>

It is beneficial when consumed; the genitals, when dried and drunk with wine or simply swallowed, help those struck by a **viper** <note>original: "echidnēs"</note>.

### Goat (Kid) Meat
<margin>from kids</margin> The meat is very easy to digest and well-balanced.
<margin>produces</margin> It produces thin blood and aids sleep. It is beneficial for those with **hot and dry temperaments** <term>the "choleric" constitution</term>. Better and more flavorful are those that are neither too heavy nor too large; for those that exceed the weaning stage should not strictly be called **kids** <note>original: "allades"</note>. The red and white ones are better than the others. They are beneficial for **colic** <term>severe abdominal pain</term> conditions. They say that when eaten with sweet honey, they prevent **drunkenness** <note>original: "methēs"</note>; they have been found to work day after day. 

### Chickpeas
Know also the facts regarding **chickpeas** <note>original: "erebinthōn"</note>; they are both "ordysian" <note>likely a geographical variety</note> and more nourishing than **beans** <note>original: "kyamōn"</note>, helping to cleanse the womb and the menstrual flow. They are both salty and possess a sweet richness. Because of the saltiness, they turn the belly <note>act as a laxative</note>; because of the sweetness, they are naturally **diuretic** <term>substances that encourage urination</term>. They are gas-producing and difficult to digest in the lower leg <note>possibly referring to causing heaviness or swelling in the limbs</note>. The **black chickpeas** <note>original: "melanes erebinthoi"</note> possess a medicinal power because of their specific action; for they are the most diuretic. They are capable of breaking **stones in the kidneys and the bladder** <note>original: "nephrois kai tē kystei lithōn"</note>. No other legume can match the power of chickpeas in passing stones. For this use, the black ones are best, specifically those small in size, and especially their **broth** <note>original: "zōmos"</note>. Regarding the irritation, they do not only help those with sores <note>original: "phylaktas"</note> in the bladder, but chickpeas also—

<meta>This page continues the dietary and medical discourse, shifting from the animal products mentioned previously (honey and venison) to legumes, specifically focusing on the medicinal properties of chickpeas, before transitioning to olives and olive oil.</meta>

**Red chickpeas** <note>original: "erebinthoi erythroi"</note> are warmer than the white varieties and produce a better **juice** <term>chyme; the semi-fluid mass of food as it is processed by the body</term>. All varieties are **diuretic** <term>promoting the production of urine</term> and **purgative** <term>cleansing to the digestive system</term>. They are especially beneficial for treating ulcerations when prepared as medicated dressings or pessaries <note>original: "pessois"</note>. 

The user should be careful to chew them thoroughly. For inflammatory conditions, they are used in poultices for **parotid swellings** <term>swellings of the glands near the ears, typically associated with mumps or infections</term>, as they have a warming and softening effect. They are also used for blisters. The decoction extracted from them is helpful for chronic skin conditions <note>original: "ōriasismō"</note>. 

Furthermore, they are beneficial for those suffering from long-term **jaundice** <term>a yellowing of the skin caused by liver bile imbalances</term> and "white dropsy" <term>leukophlegmasia; a type of swelling or edema</term>. One should not consume these alongside other food, nor immediately after a meal, but rather in the intervals between meals. When boiled with figs and celery in a broth of chickpeas and **almonds** <note>original: "amygdaleiō"</note>, the mixture becomes a powerful purgative, helpful for bladder ailments and **arthritis** <note>original: "arthritisin"</note>. Most notably, it is said to prepare one for a "worthy union" <term>a common ancient euphemism for sexual intercourse, as chickpeas were considered aphrodisiacs</term>. 

Elsewhere, it is noted that for those suffering from **gout** <term>a painful joint condition</term>, black chickpeas should be used. One should boil them until they dissolve in clarified honey and take this for three consecutive days; the patient will be greatly relieved. Green chickpeas, however, are flatulent. Some varieties are specifically prepared to be "split" or "cut," and these have a cutting or thinning effect on the humors. They are variously warm and dry. They can also be used to check the bowels...

<column-break/>

...can be harmful. Specifically, the ripe black chickpeas are more likely to cause harm, as they create dark humors in the flesh and are flatulent, leading to a burning sensation. They can heat the head and the stomach and are problematic for those with **cholera-like symptoms** <term>referring here to violent purging of the humors rather than the modern bacterial disease</term>.

**Preserved olives** <note>original: "kolymbades"; olives kept "swimming" in brine</note> are for those who are [of a certain constitution], and they are dry. In the diet for the stomach, they can cause a disturbance. It appears that if one [wishes to store them], they should be kept in wooden [vessels]; it is particularly helpful if one mixes in a measure of cumin...

<margin>Olives</margin>
**Olives** <note>original: "elaiai"</note> grown more than three hundred stadia <term>roughly 35 miles or 55 kilometers</term> away from the sea are warmer in nature. Consequently, in regions further inland, the olive-bearing trees produce a different quality of fruit...

---

<margin>oil</margin>
### † Olive Oil

**Olive oil** <note>original: "elaion"</note> is moderately warming, especially when it is fresh. The dark, unrefined kind is preferred when one seeks a less "biting" or irritating effect. As for old oil: the older it is, the more warming and **dispersive** <term>diaphoretic; able to thin and dissipate thick humors through the skin</term> it becomes. This is especially true if the oil was thin and light from the start. 

It is best for the oil to be pure and clear. For those who are weak or "shaking," it is beneficial to rub most of the body with it. Taking it in broth is also recommended, as the sweetness of the broth helps the heat of the oil be better received by the body. 

Its value is not only in its heat but also in its **astringent** <term>contracting or drying</term> quality, which makes it an excellent medicine. The **dregs of the oil** <term>amurca; the watery, bitter sediment left after pressing</term> are very warming and drying. These are useful for treating flatulent distensions and localized pains...

### Savory
**Savory** <note>original: "thrymbos"</note> is quite hot and dry. When swallowed, they say it is a thinner of phlegm and a disperser of **flatulence** <term>pneumatōse; the presence of "wind" or gas in the body</term>; it is more than the shoulder-blade <unclear>can bear</unclear>, which creates a watery state; it acts upon the digestion of food, and often moves the phlegm, and after its evacuation, it again checks the belly. And because of this, some people entwine this into the **ripe fruits** <note>original: "pepois"</note> of the elders; for while it is dispersive of winds, it also prevents their generation, both in the belly and in the intestines. A **poultice** <term>kataplasma</term> of it [aids] the menstruation; and its essential quality <note>original: "aitheremon"</note> prevents **worms** <term>helminthōn</term>; and a ground-up portion of it is effective for the path of a joint-ailment caused by cold; but it harms young men and produces **yellow bile**; when used for a long time, it often causes concern and one feels it after the [flow] into the flux.

### All Fish
All fish are cold and moist in their **temperament** <term>krasis; the specific balance of the four humors</term>; those raised among **rocks** <note>original: "petraioi"</note> are better than the others, easier to digest and providing good humors; they moderately moisten because they do not have hard flesh; second to these are the **pelagic** <note>original: "pelagioi"</note> or open-sea fish; that is, those living in the stores of the sea; but those raised in **mud and lakes** <note>original: "ilyōdeis"</note> are wretched; the signs of good-humored fish are having soft flesh and being **scaly** <note>original: "pholidoston"</note>; they should not be entirely lean, nor pus-like, nor excessively fatty; and they should not rot quickly in the air. Furthermore, the taste must be pleasant, as should the skin; for such fish do good and produce a lineage of excellent blood. And the blood breathed out by all fish is thinner than that of land animals; therefore, **dried fish** <note>original: "xēroi"</note> benefit those who are cold-natured and those weak in power, and those recovering from disease; for they do not cause a critical alteration, being so useful. Fish raised in **clean rivers** are good-humored; whereas those in turbid and similar waters and sewers are useless, of alkaline humor, and harmful to the stomach; but also fish living in **marshy** places are useless; the sea-dwellers are better than these; while the lake and mud-dwellers have much moisture; wherefore in...

<meta>This page continues the dietary and medical instructions from the previous leaf, shifting from the properties of savory and lupins to a detailed analysis of fish (marine vs. river), dried figs, and the medicinal uses of nuts, almonds, and endive for treating the liver and respiratory system.</meta>

**Marine fish** are easier to digest and less earthy <note>original: "dysamadōteroi"; likely referring to the presence of gritty or muddy textures common in freshwater fish</note>; but **river fish** are harder to digest and more earthy. Fish are beneficial for those with **yellow bile** <term>xanthēcholoi; those with a choleric temperament, characterized by heat and dryness</term>; but they harm those who are **phlegmatic** <term>phlegmatikoi; those with an excess of cold, moist phlegm</term> and those whose nerves are relaxed or weak. This is especially true in winter and in cold regions. **Small fish**, the young fry found in clean and flowing **streams** <note>original: "orythras," likely a corruption of "rheithras"</note>, caught upon pebbles and sand, are very useful. However, **cetacean** <note>large sea creatures like whales or large sharks</note> fish are hard to transform, difficult to digest, and produce **thick humors** <term>pachychymoi; heavy, viscous bodily fluids that can cause blockages</term>. Those who do not normally eat fish as part of their diet are completely harmed by it; it produces thirst and is moist for those of a cold temperament, especially for those with **bilious irritation** <note>original: "cholōdē kapria"</note>. The broth of **rock-fish** <note>fish that live among coastal rocks, such as sea bass or red mullet</note> is good for the chest. **Salted fish** are hot and dry; they are "cutting" <note>meaning they break down or thin out</note> to thick humors and make them thinner. Those with "good juices" are better, while those with **hard flesh** <note>original: "sklērosarkoi"</note>, when salted, acquire even more wretched qualities. Some say one must be careful using salted fish after **bloodletting** <term>phlebotomia; the therapeutic practice of opening a vein</term>, for they often cause **white spots** <term>alphous; a skin condition similar to vitiligo or leprosy</term> and serous discharges.

**Dried figs** <note>original: "ischades"</note> are hot in the first degree. They benefit the chest and are good against **coughs**; they are also diuretic and nourishing. However, they produce bad humors in the body, and if taken before a meal, they upset the stomach—unless they pass through the stomach quickly, they produce "wretched blood." Because of this, those who eat them to excess suffer from **scabies** <term>psōriōsi; a contagious, itchy skin disease</term>; an intense itching is brought upon them. Dried figs are useful...

<column-break/>

...and they act against **poisonous drugs** if **almonds** and nuts are taken with them. They cleanse and deobstruct the **liver** and the **viscera** <term>splachna; the internal organs, especially the heart, lungs, and liver</term> when they are full of blood. Indeed, they act as a medicine, and for those with hot temperaments, they ensure **yellow bile** is not sent out <note>i.e., they regulate its secretion</note>. Because of this, those with such a temperament should take **oxysaccharum** <term>oxysacchar; a medicinal syrup made of vinegar and sugar or honey</term> with them. If worked into a medicine after this, they are beneficial for chronic coughs and ailments of the chest. Regarding **parotid swellings** <note>swellings of the salivary glands behind the ears</note> and other abscesses, they soften them, for they aid in "cooking" <note>maturing or bringing to a head</note> the inflammation, especially in softer flesh. When mixed with **fenugreek** <note>original: "tilleōs"</note> in vinegar and applied to the feet, they mimic the treatment for **gout** <term>podagrikō</term> through the use of **endive** <note>original: "ētybou," likely a variant of "intybos"</note>. **Endive** <note>original: "psychome," likely referring to chicory/endive</note> is cooling in the first degree. The **wild** variety is dry in the first degree, while the **garden** variety is moist in the same rank. They are good for the stomach when used with vinegar. They especially guard and support the stomach; they also have a deobstructing power. It checks the flow of blood and disperses inflammations throughout the body, and mimics the cure for **jaundice** <term>ikteriōn</term>. By its specific quality, it strengthens the liver. It is also a provider of moderate **sleep**. Some say that after bloodletting, the use of **chicory** <note>original: "sykiasin," likely referring to the plant's juices</note> with vinegar preserves the health of the liver. When prepared correctly, it reduces **sexual desire** and diminishes the **seed** <term>gonēn; semen or reproductive potential</term>. It is beneficial for suffocations of the womb. In a broth of wine, it is entirely helpful for the liver. Its **seed** is beneficial against fevers caused by yellow bile, for it [protects] the viscera. +

<summary>This page discusses the dietary and medicinal properties of various fish, dried figs, and endive. It categorizes foods by their effect on the four humors, specifically detailing how to treat liver issues, coughs, and inflammatory conditions like gout.</summary>
<keywords>dietetics, fish, dried figs, endive, liver health, yellow bile, phlegm, bloodletting, oxysaccharum, gout, medical botany</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical and dietary discourse from the previous page, moving from the properties of fish and dried fruits to the medicinal uses of mulberries, figs, quinces, and cherries, focusing on their effects on the humors and specific ailments like fevers and stomach distress.</meta>

... are cold according to the first degree; but [moist] according to the second <note>original: "apophasin"; in Galenic medicine, this refers to the 'degree' or intensity of a substance's primary qualities—heat, cold, moisture, or dryness.</note>. They are beneficial against feverish pains and soothe the heart. They stop headaches caused by **yellow bile** <term>xanthē cholē; the humor associated with fire, heat, and a choleric temperament</term>. One may benefit from smelling **mulberries** <note>original: "morai"; this can refer to either mulberries or blackberries, both used in Byzantine medicine for their cooling properties.</note>, or using the gum from the mulberry tree. These are excellent for those with a bilious temperament. 

And this is most true, as I the scribe bear witness; I have noted this about these **figs** <note>original: "syka"; the text here seems to conflate the discussion of mulberries with figs, or perhaps refers to a specific variety of "mulberry-fig."</note> from the fainting spells I suffered whenever I was gripped by fevers in the stomach. When fried and drunk with **sugar** <term>zachari; unlike honey, sugar was often treated as a potent medicine in this period</term>, I felt the bile was cut away, unless I had already fallen into the sleep of the tragedy <note>original: "tragōdias"; likely a poetic or idiomatic way of describing a deep, death-like stupor caused by illness.</note>. By providing this to me, you cool my coldness. These are dry according to the degree of the head, and they cool the heat of the sense of smell, bringing rest. However, they cause a "flux" in those with moist heads. 

Their **decoction** <term>apozema; a liquid medicine made by boiling plant matter</term> heals children with stomach ailments, **quinsy** <term>kynanchē; a severe, suffocating throat infection or abscess</term>, and epilepsy. Fresh green mulberries, remarkably, impart their own scent to the urine. The oil made from them strengthens the body. They say it balances the cold. If one rubs this on together with **deer-grease** <note>original: "alapheiō"; animal fats were common carriers for herbal medicines.</note>, it helps with sweating or if one suffers from stinging eruptions. It is also effective against confusion <note>original: "agnoia"; can refer to delirium or loss of senses during a fever.</note>, all fevers, and headaches, whether smelled or applied as an ointment. From this is made the sugar-syrup called **julep** <term>zoulapon; from the Persian "gol-ab" (rose water), a sweet medicinal drink</term>, which is used for ailments of the lungs and the chest...

<column-break/>

<header>13</header>

# On the Quince.

**Quinces** are cold according to the first degree and dry according to the second. By nature, they are astringent and beneficial to the stomach. If eaten before a meal, they bind the bowels, but they also stimulate the flow of urine. If eaten in excess, they cause heaviness. Because of this, they are prepared for those with **colic** <term>kōlikas; severe abdominal pain caused by gas or obstruction</term>. In a specific preparation, they are more easily digested. Those prepared with honey are sweeter than those with dates. The sour ones are better for those with a "cold-gentle" temperament. Used as food, they can change the state of the stomach; if taken after food, they cool the belly and sharpen the hair <note>original: "oxynei tas trichas"; this may refer to preventing hair loss or improving hair texture through the balance of humors.</note>. In all cases, after food, they settle the stomach. But let us boil them, as they are more astringent and produce raw humors in the stomach when eaten raw. They have a strengthening power. They prevent the vapors of wine from rising to the head. It is said that if women eat these continuously, they will bear children that are industrious and clever. The preparation made from them called **quince-paste** <term>kydonaton; a thick, honeyed fruit preserve, the ancestor of modern marmalade</term> is excellent for the stomach and is a Roman <note>original: "rhōmaikon"; likely referring to the Byzantine (East Roman) culinary or medical tradition.</note> specialty.

---

# On Cherries.

**Cherries** are cold and moist. They are laxative, but they produce "bad juices" <term>kakochyma; poor quality humors that do not nourish the body well</term>, and they cause the moist stomach to rumble, especially when they are unripe. They are beneficial for those with hot and dry temperaments. The gum from these trees, when taken with wine, is said to break down **stones** <note>likely referring to kidney or bladder stones</note>. On the other hand, fresh **walnuts** are hot according to the first degree; when dried, they are hot according to the second degree.

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s pharmaceutical survey of foods and plants. Having discussed fruits like mulberries and figs on the previous page, he now transitions into nuts (almonds), gourds, chestnuts, and cabbage, categorizing them by their "degrees" of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness according to Galenic medical theory.</meta>

...but if they are fresh; for these are stimulating. They produce a healthy color in the face. Bitter **dried figs** <note>original: "ischades"; dried figs were a staple of the Byzantine diet and frequently used in medicine for their warming properties.</note> are more effective than **almonds** <note>original: "amygdalōn"</note>. They cause inflammations in the body—indeed, many of them—but they are more beneficial for settling the stomach. Regarding the heat of men; dried figs and **walnuts** <note>original: "karyōn"</note> and **terebinth** <note>original: "pitanou" (likely terebinth or pistachio)</note>—concerning such things, one is not greatly harmed even by lethal drugs. And yet, not with cold stomachs, for they digest walnuts easily; nor with hot ones, but for those with **bilious humors** <note>original: "cholōdesi chymon"; refers to an excess of yellow bile, which Psellos suggests these foods can balance.</note>. <gloss>14</gloss> And medicines produce most of all headaches; and if they attack, they are harmful. It is softened in other ways when eaten as a garnish. For vomiting it is not well-suited. For those using it excessively, it expels **intestinal worms** <note>original: "helmithas"</note>; and for those suffering from stings or bites, it is effective for many.

**The Gourd** <note>original: "Kolokynthē"; likely refers to a bottle gourd or a type of squash.</note>, is cold and moist according to the second **degree** <term>apophasin; in Galenic medicine, the intensity of a substance's primary qualities—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—was measured in four degrees.</term>. It is easy to digest, produces good humors, and is all-beneficial. It quenches thirst and is a diuretic. For when it is stimulating, it serves those with hot constitutions and temperaments. It also settles inflammations of the stomach and the liver. However, it harms those who are **phlegmatic** <term>phlegmatikous; people with an excess of the "cold and moist" humor of phlegm</term>, and is poorly suited for colicky conditions. But if it encounters bad humors in the stomach, it is corrupted and becomes a producer of bad humor, so that the food is spoiled. It also diminishes the impulse for **sexual acts** <note>original: "aphrodisiōn"; gourds were often considered "anaphrodisiacs" in medieval medicine because of their cooling effect.</note>. The blood produced from it is thin in its nature. It benefits the lung, the chest, and the bladder. The juice expressed from it...

<column-break/>

...is suitable for inflammations of the face, for any heat when it is applied. And when the whole thing is applied as a plaster, it cools hot inflammations.

**Chestnuts** <note>original: "kastana"</note> heat and dry according to the first <insert>11</insert> degree. They are nourishing, unless one provides the body with the whole fruit; yet they are slow to pass and hard to digest. They produce a thick humor and a sweaty headache. They are restrictive to the stomach; <gloss>for</gloss> they are effective at cutting through colicky conditions. <gloss>how, how</gloss> Yet mostly, they set aside much of the harm <insert>dry juice</insert>.

**Cabbage** <note>original: "Krambē"</note> heats and dries according to the first degree. It produces bad humor and generates **melancholic humor** <term>melancholikou chymou; the humor associated with "black bile," cold and dry, linked to depression and heaviness.</term>. It dulls the sight and disturbs sleep with troublesome dreams. But its juice is both restrictive and purgative. It supports the stomach and the body; and because of this, it suddenly dries the parts of the body. If we wish to boil it moderately, we should pour off the first water and immediately throw it into other hot, boiling water. For it should not touch <unclear>nor</unclear> a breeze <unclear>nor</unclear> cold water while it is being boiled. Summer cabbage produces worse humors than winter cabbage. It is a diuretic. By the **moon** <note>original: "selēnē"; agricultural and medical timing was often linked to lunar phases in Byzantine thought.</note>... it provides a solution for those who are failing. It is also said to heal **blurred vision** <note>original: "amblyōpian"</note> caused by moisture. When boiled with fatty meat, much of its harm is removed. But its seed, by some specific property, destroys the **offspring** <note>original: "gonēn"; can refer to semen or fertility.</note>. When placed upon the womb, it prevents women from conceiving. However, it harms the lung. They say that before any other...

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s pharmaceutical survey of foods and plants. Having discussed gourds and cucumbers on the previous page, the text now transitions into the medicinal properties of wild fruits like the strawberry tree, chestnuts, and a detailed analysis of the citron (Median apple), classified by its distinct parts: rind, flesh, juice, and seeds.</meta>

...taken as food, it prevents drunkenness. And the juice of this, taken as a drink with honey, greatly benefits the **sensitization of the teeth** <note>original: "haimōdias"; refers to that "on edge" feeling or numbness in the teeth, here associated with physical collapse or fainting.</note> caused by exhaustion. For hot wounds, these knit them together. It also produces healthy scars and heals hardened inflammations.

The fruit of the **Strawberry tree** <note>original: "Komarōn"; the *Arbutus unedo*, a common Mediterranean shrub with edible red berries.</note> or also of the **Wild Arbutus**, is hot in the second **degree** <term>apophasin; in Galenic medicine, the intensity of a substance's primary qualities—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—was measured in four degrees.</term> and dry in the first. It happens to be nourishing, though if eaten alone it produces **thick humor** <note>original: "pachy-chymos"; Byzantine medicine believed certain foods made the blood or other humors too viscous, leading to blockages.</note>, is hard to digest, and causes headaches. However, it produces good blood and thins roughness in the chest. It is beneficial for wounds in the bladder and for acrid humors in the stomach and the members, and for chronic coughs. It is also used for liver diseases and for those with internal abscesses, especially when taken with honey or a cleansing agent. It is not easily digested and aids in the "cutting" of humors; it fulfills its disposition through its pleasantness.

**Cimmara** <note>original: "Kimmara"; likely referring to a type of wild nut or perhaps mushrooms, though the context of chestnuts suggests a forest fruit.</note> are a food that produces bad humors, especially when they are eaten hard. It produces a bilious humor; evidence of this is that they generate a **melancholy humor** <note>original: "melancholikon chymon"; black bile, believed to cause heaviness of spirit or "melancholy."</note>. The juice of the **chestnut** <note>original: "kastanou"</note> is a thin, bitter juice. One should eat them without excess; even these should be eaten sparingly.

Regarding **Citrons** <note>original: "Kitrōn"</note>, also called **Median apples** <note>original: "mēlomedikōn"; citrons were famously imported from Media (modern Iran) and were the first citrus fruits known to the Greek world.</note>: the rind is hot and dry in the third degree, but the inner flesh is cold

<column-break/>

<page-num>15</page-num>
in the first degree. The acidic part in the middle is cold and dry in the third degree. The seed is hot and dry in the second degree. Thus, the flesh of these is hard to digest and generates **thick phlegm** <note>original: "phlegmatos pacheous"; one of the four humors, associated here with slow digestion.</note>; for this reason, we are accustomed to eating it with honey. The rind, with the aid of digestion, draws out melancholy humor and relieves most of the **eight types of despondency** <note>original: "athymias"; Psellos often links dietary health to psychological states.</note>. It is good for the stomach if one uses it moderately. For the middle part, if used, is hard to digest.

The preparation made with honey for those with hardened growths, called **Citriate** <note>original: "Kitriou"</note>, warms the stomach and sends its benefits to the liver above the stomach and to the stomach itself. While the acidic part is generally hard to digest, the acidic juice of the citron is an **antidote** <note>original: "antipharmakon"</note> against poisons. Indeed, it is beneficial for the bites of venomous creatures, especially for those bitten by scorpions.

When drunk warm in its proper portion, it is blood-enriching. It benefits disturbances in the stomach and stops excess bile. The heat in these citrons is wonderful, for it quickly produces a sympathetic reaction in the body. They say that when used through the nostrils, it benefits the eyes. These citrons, when used as sharp remedies, provide heat; they resolve **phlegmatic** and thick humors. For this reason, they are mixed into remedies for those with **asthma** and chest complaints, as they warm intensely. The seed of the citron is even more powerful in its effects for such purposes.

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s pharmaceutical survey of plants and foods. Following the previous discussion on fruits and the citron, the text now details the medicinal properties of the Cypress tree, Onions, Cumin, and begins a section on Cloves, focusing on their Galenic classifications (heating, drying, etc.) and specific internal and external applications.</meta>

<margin>Cypress</margin> **Cypress** <note>original: "Kyparissos"</note>; it heats and dries according to the second **degree** <term>apophasin; the classification of a substance's potency in the Galenic medical system, ranging from 1 (mild) to 4 (extreme).</term>. It is composed of various qualities. It possesses a certain bitterness in the distribution of its sharpness; it purifies, cuts <note>original: "temnei"; in a medical context, this refers to the ability of a substance to break down or "cut" through thick, viscous humors.</note>, and makes acrid, which is why it both heats and disperses. It also has an astringency that gathers and tightens; because of this, it is beneficial for **hard tumors** <note>original: "skirrōseis"; scirrhous growths or hardened inflammations.</note>. If it is applied as a poultice, especially with lead that has been prepared and burnt, it helps with toothaches and it has a strong scent. Both its fruit and its bark are quite strong; however, the petals, leaves, stem, and fruit are weaker. They soften **swellings** <note>original: "tomous"; here likely referring to hard lumps</note> and the hardness of **scrofulous nodes** <note>original: "choiradōn"; swollen lymph nodes, typically in the neck.</note>. They loosen the teeth and remove worms lodged in the ears. When its scent is inhaled, it helps with obstructions and poor blockages of the liver. It especially benefits the spleen when roasted. It also manages "mists" <note>likely referring to "mists" over the eyes or clouded vision.</note>, but it is harmful to the kidneys and the bladder.

<margin>Onions</margin> **Onions** <note>original: "Krommya"</note> heat and dry according to the second degree, yet are moist according to the third. Their substance is thick-parted. They are diuretic and "flesh-pleasing" <note>original: "sarkas terptika"; likely meaning they stimulate the growth or tone of the flesh.</note>. Furthermore, they are stimulants for the appetite for food. They are naturally inclined to cause headaches and are bad for the stomach. As some say, they are also <unclear>nourishing</unclear> for the rest of the body. The impulse of the onion brings either the most harm or the greatest benefit; it is useful for roughness of the chest and for coughs, as well as for hemorrhoids when mixed with

<meta>catchword: with</meta>

---

<page-num>10</page-num>
warm honey. When eaten or applied as an ointment, it clears away **white skin spots** <note>original: "alphous"; a skin condition involving white patches, often associated with vitiligo or lepra in historical texts.</note>. Even if its substance is thick-parted, it acts upon thick and viscous humors that have filled the stomach. Everyone uses them more effectively for respiratory ailments.

<margin>Cumin</margin> **Cumin** <note>original: "Kyminon"</note> belongs to the third order of those things that heat and dry; or to a lesser extent, the second. It is very fine-parted <note>original: "leptomeres"; substances with small particles thought to penetrate the body's channels more easily.</note>. Nothing dries as much with an equally heating substance. It purifies the stomach and benefits coughs caused by moisture. It warms the stomach and the liver. It dries out moisture in the head and the abdominal cavity. I define the "fine-parted" as being the whole middle portion; or such as the <unclear>mousoulas</unclear> of the city. There are twelve types of cumin, including the so-called Ethiopian and those from other regions. About the time it passes through <unclear>the proposed observation</unclear>; they are all good for the stomach, pleasing and restorative, diuretic, and capable of opening blockages. They promote **menstruation** <note>original: "katamēniōn agōga"; emmenagogues.</note> and are **abortifacient** <note>original: "embryoktoktrōtika"; substances that induce the expulsion of a fetus.</note> when drunk with honeyed wine for uterine pain. They also act as antidotes against the bites of venomous creatures and the ingestion of poisons. They clear skin spots with honey or a <unclear>shaved</unclear> mixture. For those with dropsy, it participates mostly in cooling. For this reason, it is found among the cold and moist things, whether by this or by its own nourishment; but it is harmful to **beans** <note>original: "kyamon"; possibly referring to the broad bean, which was the subject of various dietary taboos.</note>.

<margin>Clove</margin> **Clove** <note>original: "Karyophyllon"</note>; it heats and dries according to the second degree; some say the third. It is from the "case" <note>original: "thēkē"; likely referring to the flower bud or the husk of the spice.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the medicinal properties of cypress, onions, and cumin, detailing their Galenic degrees and their uses for conditions ranging from scrofulous swellings and toothaches to respiratory issues and poison antidotes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s encyclopedic medical survey of plants. After discussing the Cypress and Onions, the text moves into a list of spices and seeds including Laurel, Aromatic Nut, Cardamom, Cumin, Amomum, Coriander, and Saffron. It continues to utilize the Galenic system of "degrees" to describe the potency of their heating and drying effects.</meta>

<margin>
Laurel
</margin>
**The fruit of the Laurel** <note>original: "daphnēs karpos"; also known as bay laurel.</note> is beneficial for the stomach, the liver, and the heart. It calms the **womb** <unclear>mētram</unclear> from evil **flatulence** <unclear>emphysēseis</unclear>; however, it is harmful to the **livers** <unclear>hēpatas</unclear>.

<margin>
25
Nut
</margin>
The **Aromatic Nut** <note>original: "arōmatikos karyon"; likely referring to nutmeg or a similar imported spice.</note> is hot and dry in the second **degree** <term>apostasis; the classification of a substance's intensity in Galenic medicine.</term>. It binds the bowels; it is very effective for a **moist stomach** <unclear>hygron</unclear> and the liver. It helps with flatulence in the **sides** <unclear>pleurōn</unclear> <note>original: "pneumatōsin pleurōn"; likely referring to pleuritic pain or gas.</note> and for all pains, but it is harmful to the lungs.

<margin>
26
Cardamom
</margin>
**Cardamom** <note>original: "kardamōdion"</note> is hot and dry in the third degree. It is suitable for digestion, for pleasure, and for unbearable flatulence. It works by stopping **bitterness** <note>original: "pikria"; perhaps referring to bile or acid reflux.</note> in the stomach and kills worms. It is also a diuretic and **cuts** <note>original: "tmētikon"; a medical term for breaking down thick fluids like phlegm.</note> through phlegm. It is used for the **stomach** <note>original: "kēpon"; literally "garden," but used metaphorically in some medical texts for the gut.</note> when there is an excess of bile. It is also hot <unclear>in the</unclear> <unclear>second</unclear> <gloss>26</gloss> degree.

<margin>
27
Cumin
</margin>
**Cumin** <note>original: "kyminon"</note> is hot and dry according to the third <unclear>degree</unclear>; though some place it in the second degree. It is a diuretic and relieves unbearable flatulence. It is very beneficial to the stomach. If drunk by itself, it causes **paleness** <note>original: "ōchriasin"; a well-known side effect of cumin noted by ancient physicians like Dioscorides.</note>. It stops the flow of blood from the nose, which is why it is used moderately in side-dishes. When drunk with old oil, its potency is directed toward heat; it dries the stomach and burns like fire. That which is found in **a certain preparation** <unclear>... ...</unclear> helps with hemorrhoids. When drunk with <unclear>... ...</unclear> it acts upon heat, thickens the milk if it is too voluminous, and heals swellings. It is especially a <unclear>... ...</unclear> remedy for poisons and the bites of venomous creatures. It appears to be useful for those of us afflicted with **stones** <note>original: "lithois"; likely kidney or bladder stones.</note> in our parts, just as thyme is.

<column-break/>

<header>17</header>
<margin>
Amomum
</margin>
**Amomum** <note>original: "amomon"; an aromatic spice related to cardamom.</note>, which is used against **gnats** <note>original: "kōmōpasi"; suggesting it was used as an insect repellent.</note>.

<margin>
1
Coriander
</margin>
**Coriander** <note>original: "korianndros"</note> is cold and stops bitter discharges. It is dry in the third degree. It is good for the stomach and aids in digestion; for when it is applied, it brings delight to the stomach so that food is digested well. When it is parched or roasted, it binds the bowels and is effective against inflammations and **fevers** <gloss>153</gloss>. It stops "women’s flows" <note>likely referring to excessive menstruation.</note>. When drunk, specifically three parts of it with heat, or when drunk with wine, it expels worms. However, when used **improperly** <gloss>18</gloss>, it causes delirium. It is said that if it is drunk while exposed to the sun, it acts as a poison and can kill; for many have become deranged after drinking it.

<margin>
2
Saffron
</margin>
**Saffron** <note>original: "krokos"</note> is hot in the second degree and dry in the first. Through its heat, it cheers the heart. It calms the third degree <note>referring to a specific humor or state</note>. It is good for the stomach and assists in digestion. It is <unclear>effective</unclear> and very useful for flatulence and is quite distinguished; it is also a laxative and aids the mind. It is beneficial to the internal organs and also acts as a diuretic. When used in cooking, it provides a healthy **complexion**; but in immoderate amounts, it brings on paleness and headaches. It is used to heal inflammations, especially when the oil of it is dripped with rose oil and applied to those suffering from **gout**. It is beneficial, not only when applied to these, but also <unclear>... ...</unclear> as it seems <unclear>... ...</unclear> from the evaporation of its essence.

...laughter; if they cease, it will be poured out until [the weight of] talents <note>original: "talantōn"; an ancient unit of weight.</note> 
<margin>A paradoxical laughter is said to become a wonder; it is said that certain living creatures are thrown in, joined together, only as large as a nut, which it casts out through a curdled [substance]...</margin>
**Camphors** <note>original: "Kayphorai"; referring to camphor, a resin used historically in both medicine and incense.</note> are dry and cold according to the third **degree** <term>apostasis; a classification of a substance's potency in the Galenic system.</term>. Whether it is the gum or the resin of the "tears" of the tree. It is said that the ants there are exceedingly large and bloodless. They are produced in the parts near the sea. The wood of such a tree is light and porous. It benefits the limbs and the **sinews** <unclear>loura</unclear>, and before the discharge of the breasts, and all the heat of the head and inflammations, especially those of the **stomach** <note>original: "kēpou"; literally "garden," a common Byzantine medical metaphor for the digestive tract.</note>. However, it causes cooling in the **veins** <unclear>moschois</unclear> and the spontaneous vessels. When drunk, it is "without heat." It is tested for purity in this way: it is placed near heat, and if it becomes liquid, it is genuine; but if not, it is a counterfeit.

**Saffron** <note>original: "Krokos"</note> is hot in its **temperament** <term>krasei; the balance of humors in a substance or body.</term>. It benefits those who smell it, especially for a **cold head** <note>original: "psychran kephalēn"; likely referring to lethargy, congestion, or "cold" headaches.</note>. The oil made from it is dispersive and softening for the passages. Some use its root for **purging** <unclear>apolaxin</unclear>.

**The Mullet** <note>original: "Kephalos"; the grey mullet.</note> is the most [significant] of all fish, both in river and sea waters. <margin>flows with difficulty; phlegmatic</margin> It is hard to digest and produces **bad humors** <term>kakochymichos; the production of unhealthy fluids or "juice" within the body.</term>.

<column-break/>

...is restrictive. The river mullet is not fatty. The **Bubble-fish** <note>original: "pompholyx"; literally "bubble," possibly referring to a specific species or the texture of the meat.</note> is not hard to digest and is better for the humors, though somewhat thin and weak.

**The Goby** <note>original: "kōbios"</note> is a fish from the coastal waters. Those from the marble-like, sandy shores or the rocks and cliffs are good for the humors and easy to digest. However, those found in the mouths of rivers or in lakes are neither good for the humors nor easy to digest. Their broth moves the **bowels** <unclear>koilian</unclear>. Some eat only the **marrow** <unclear>ameualōm</unclear> for **dysentery** <note>original: "dysenterias"</note> and for **straining** <note>original: "tēnesmōdeis"; tenesmus, the feeling of needing to evacuate the bowels.</note>. 

**Shrimp** <note>original: "ikaridos"; likely a variation of garidos.</note> are known for their heat; they are moist. When cooked, they are bad for the stomach, yet they are **aphrodisiacs** <note>original: "aphrodisia"</note> and help specifically with a woman's **pregnancy** <unclear>kyēsin</unclear>. When ground up with **oxymel** <term>oxymeliti; a medicinal drink made of honey and vinegar.</term> and drunk, they expel **worms** <note>original: "helminthas"</note>. It is said that when ground smooth and applied raw... it draws out [foreign objects]... or likewise produces a vision after a cure... and they are roughly the size of... and are desirable.

**Crayfish** <note>original: "Karaboi"</note> are of two kinds. They are very nourishing and have a salty humor. If they are repeatedly infused in sweet water, they are medicinal or restrictive. It is said that the **shell of the crayfish** <note>original: "ostrakon tou karabou"</note>, when burnt and drunk with undiluted wine, clears **stones from the kidneys** <note>original: "meiphroislithior"; nephrolithiasis.</note>, bringing down much sandy urine.

**Hare meat** <note>original: "lagoōn krea"</note>, **Section A** <note>original: "Stoicheion A"; likely the beginning of an alphabetical entry for meat types.</note>

...[Saffron] is dry and very useful for **buboes** <term>boubōsin; inflammatory swellings of the lymph nodes.</term>. However, for those with a more "red" **temperament** <term>krāsim; the specific balance of humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—within an individual.</term>, it is unsuitable. It is digested along with thick matter just as bile is; it settles in the entire stomach and affects the urine. It is well-advised, therefore, to be used only in ointments, for they cause blockages in both the liver and the spleen. They are also harmful to the lungs and cause sleeplessness. The **rennet** <note>original: "pityas"; a substance from the stomach used to curdle milk, here used medicinally.</note> of the throat, along with honey, is strongly effective against discharges and flows. For this reason, it is used for all kinds of bites. It specifically hinders the bladder during the menstrual cleansing. They say that the female nightingale alone has a voice, and it is prepared even more when taken with wine. From this, it appears that for our physical arrangements, it remains for men. It is said that its brain is active by day, or it is all driven by scent.

**Concerning Walnuts** <note>original: "Karya".</note>: Those called "Pontic nuts" <note>hazelnuts</note> are warm when cold. Walnuts are more nourishing, yet harder to digest, and produce less heat. While they cause flatulence, they protect the middle... The other thing said is that it should not be passed through with vinegar. They say that it expels deer [horn?] and the middle skin, called the "husk." It is said that if one remains silent before eating this, it becomes white. It is a remedy for the poison of venomous creatures. The wax is not harmless... while scorpions flee from the poppy. Whence, greens are not only for those bitten by scorpions. And it adds a black humor in the stomach...

<column-break/>

...only a little warm and in the second **degree** <term>apostasis; a classification of a substance's potency on a scale of one to four.</term>, but dry in the first. It has a certain unique quality. It restrains rheumatic flows and dries the spirit, being more beneficial for such conditions. Also for menstrual flow alone... but it rather has a certain unique property. First, against the **plague** <note>original: "loimou".</note>. When burned as incense during a pestilential time, it helps the houses to be preserved, for it alters the corrupt quality of the air.

**Ladanum** <note>original: "Ladanon"; a sticky resin obtained from the Cistus (rockrose) shrub, used in perfumes and ancient medicine.</note> is hot in nearly the second degree. It makes meat easy to digest and has softening properties...

**Sea Bass** <note>original: "Labrax"; the European sea bass, highly prized in Byzantine cuisine.</note>, caught by ships near the shore, is characterized by its head. Its blood is thinner and it cuts through **phlegm** <term>phlegmatos; the cold, moist humor.</term>. This is sweet, and the head is more prophetic...

## Letter M

**Regarding Apples** <note>original: "Mēlois".</note>, there is a great difference among them. Those that are cold have a watery juice. Those that are sharp and sour are cold but made of fine particles. Sweet apples are of a moderate temperament, leaning toward the warmer side. Naturally, one should not use them [excessively], for a balanced temperament is harmless. They contain much moisture or settle in the middle. The bitter ones are of a certain quality, while the sharp ones seem to be more... because of the thick humors. Incense and cold mixtures... for they cut through this. They cause [unclear]... but sweet apples are suitable for those with a cold stomach. They benefit those suffering from **bilious humors** <term>cholōdē chymon; excess yellow bile, associated with heat and bitterness.</term>. Of all apples...
