<meta>This page continues Psellos’s encyclopedic catalog of medicinal plants and their Galenic properties. Following the discussion of walnuts and resins, it focuses on the dietary and curative powers of lettuce, mallow, and a pungent herb (likely rocket), specifically addressing their effects on the humors, sleep, and internal blockages.</meta>

->**The Properties of Lettuce and Mallow**<-

...in their own nature, they do not harm the nerves. Rather, they are easily dissolved—specifically those that have not been tested. It is said that they greatly improve memory in those who use them to satiety. They are also beneficial against fainting and **weakness of the heart** <term>kardiakas atonias; a state of low energy or "slackness" in the heart, often associated with lethargy or low blood pressure in Galenic medicine.</term>. **Lettuces** <note>original: "mazoulia" and "thridakai"; Psellos uses both the common medieval term and the classical Greek word for lettuce.</note> are cold and moist in the **third degree** <term>tritēn apophasin; according to the Galenic system, plants were classified by four degrees of intensity for the four primary qualities: hot, cold, wet, and dry.</term>. They are also used as a sleep-inducing compress. Elsewhere, it is said that they are similar to other vegetables in their balanced nature; they do not produce **bad-humored blood** <term>kakochymon haima; blood containing unhealthy proportions of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile).</term>, though neither is the blood they produce perfectly healthy. They benefit those with warmer stomachs. 

However, when used to excess, they cause weakness in the body and dull the senses, and they lead to **difficulty in sexual movement** <term>aphrodisia dyskinēsian; a reduction in libido or physical sexual capacity.</term>. Their seed, shaped like a rhombus, stops discharges <note>likely referring to hemorrhages or "fluxes".</note>. When mixed with rose-water and applied to the temples of those suffering from insomnia or excess heat, it induces sleep. One must be careful with the use of lettuce in cases of **spitting blood** <term>haimoptoïkois; hemoptysis, or coughing up blood from the lungs.</term>, asthmatics, and those of a **phlegmatic** <term>phlegmatikois; a temperament dominated by the humor of phlegm, characterized by coldness and dampness.</term> disposition, as well as those desiring to have children. For they say it reduces childbearing ability and causes a cooling of the kidneys. 

When boiled and consumed with **sesame oil** <note>original: "sesamelaiō".</note>, it helps those suffering from jaundice. They liken the coldness and moisture of these plants to that of water from steep cliffs. For this reason, they are very beneficial against burning fevers. It is also said that when taken alone, they ward off the harm caused by a change in drinking water. Their juice is found to be a remedy for poisons and purges...

<column-break/>

...is found to be a remedy when taken. 

<margin>+</margin> **Mallow** <term>malachē; specifically the common mallow (Malva sylvestris), valued since antiquity for its soothing, mucilaginous properties.</term> is cold and moist in the **first degree** <note>This is a milder classification than lettuce, making it more balanced for general use.</note>. It passes through the stomach easily. It is beneficial for the internal organs, and its slipperiness helps against **stones** <note>kidney or bladder stones</note> when taken with oil. It is particularly helpful during the seventh hour. It also benefits the chest and lungs, as well as **shortness of breath** <note>original: "brachychēlei"; likely a corruption of "brachypnoia".</note>. 

If mallow is eaten for three days on an empty stomach, it renders painless those who have been **stung by wasps**. If the sting is boiled with honey-water and anointed with the juice of mallow—especially the wild variety—it immediately becomes painless. It is said that its **decoction** <term>apozema; a liquid medicine made by boiling medicinal herbs.</term> when drunk, breaks up and expels stones. It also facilitates **easy childbirth** <term>eutokian</term> for those who use it, acting as a gentle aid. It stops inflammations when applied as a poultice and softens hardened parts of the body. Its most exceptional property is that, when applied to the bites of wasps and especially bees, it brings relief from pain.

**Rocket** <note>original: "omoladon"; likely a corruption of "euzomon" (rocket/arugula), given the description of it being "hot and dry in the third degree."</note> is hot and dry in the **third degree**. The variety called "Samaritan" is the most effective. It disperses **winds** <term>pneumatōn; internal gases or vapors believed to cause bloating and various illnesses.</term>. If there is phlegm, it is "cutting" <note>meaning it breaks up and thins thick mucus</note>, and it is beneficial for flows and **rheumatism** <term>reumatismois; any discharge of humors, often affecting joints or muscles.</term> when its scent is inhaled, especially after being warmed. It is said that when ground smooth with fatty pork and heated, it... and in this way expels **worms** <term>helminthas; intestinal parasites.</term>. And it is said that it is hardly...

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s pharmaceutical guide, moving from the cooling properties of lettuce to the warming and restorative powers of fermented preparations, bugloss, musk, and the various species of poppy. It maintains the Galenic classification of substances by degrees of heat and moisture.</meta>

...taken while fermented and slightly warm, it overcomes stones in the **kidneys** <note>original: "nephrois"</note> and the bladder. It is also a **diuretic** <term>diourētikon; a substance that promotes the production of urine, used in ancient medicine to "flush" the system of toxins or stones.</term> and addresses the cessation of menses. It may also serve as an **antidote** <note>original: "antipharmakon"</note> against poisons, if one partakes of it.

The leaf of **bugloss** <note>original: "bouglōssou"; literally "ox-tongue" (Anchusa officinalis), an herb long associated with promoting cheerfulness.</note>, when warm and swallowed, immediately benefits the stomach; it is helpful for those with a "chilled heart" and for the despondency caused by **black bile** <term>melainēs cholēs; one of the four humors, believed to cause melancholy, gloom, and irrational fear when in excess.</term>, as well as the irrational fears resulting from it. It procures a sense of cheerfulness. It effects a dissolution of the **melancholic humor** and the phlegm. It is even said to provide a new beginning for one's lot in life, as if watered by the spirit.

**Armeria** <note>likely referring to a species of thrift or perhaps a corrupted reference to "hermodactyl," used for joint pain.</note>... for this is specifically for the knees. It eases the gait when taken in a **syrup** <term>zoulapiou; from the Persian 'julab,' a sweet medicinal drink often made with rosewater and sugar.</term>. It is very beneficial for melancholic conditions and the despondency that arises from them.

There are different kinds of **musk** <note>original: "moschos"; a pungent secretion from the musk deer, highly prized in the Byzantine court for both medicine and perfumery.</note>, the best of which is honored by us in the City <note>referring to Constantinople.</note>. It is said to be much more "blood-like" than the rest of its class. If the color is somewhat yellowish, this is the kind brought from India. It tends toward a dark, earthy quality. Inferior to this is the so-called "snub-nosed" musk. All of these are cut from the **navel** <note>original: "omphalō"</note> of the animal—that is, the large musk-deer, which resembles a gazelle. The pharmacists say that in this animal, the navel swells; they allow the substance within to thicken, and the animal rolls upon the earth and sheds

<column-break/>

the navel-sac while it is still full of a muddy, blood-like fluid. When this congeals, after some time it acquires its famous scent. **Musk** is hot and dry in the **third degree** <term>tritēn apophasin; a high intensity in the Galenic scale, meaning it is a potent warming agent.</term>. The inferior kind, however, pierces the senses and cools the head. It is harmful to those with a warm constitution. It is used for swellings and for those desiring... it restores the power of the exhausted and is strengthened against **heart ailments** <note>original: "kardiakas pathē"</note>, for it fortifies the heart. However, it makes the body appear paler. 

Some test its purity in the following way: after it is cleaned with **myrrh** <note>original: "smyrnē"</note> in a basin, they observe it as it ages. If it retains a superior "bloom" <note>likely referring to the intensity of its aromatic crystals or "efflorescence."</note>, it is genuine; if not, they consider it to be adulterated.

**Poppy** <note>original: "mēkōn"</note>. Black pepper... [is used] for heat. But the flowers are cold and watery... when they are expansive. **Poppy** is cold and dry in the **first degree**. The white variety is better than the black for treating melancholy. If it is taken with honey as a sleep-inducing aid, it provides relief. It benefits the **chest** <note>original: "thōraka"</note> and the **windpipe** <term>tracheian artērian; the trachea.</term>, and it is effective against coughs because of its restorative power, even though some have falsely judged this. It urges the body to activity through its effect, but it harms the head when used to excess. When used with walnuts, it produces... some connect these two because they say honey acts in the same way, due to its "breath." The black poppy is colder and more sleep-inducing, so that if one partakes of it without moderation, they fall into a sleep...

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s pharmaceutical discourse, shifting from the cooling properties of the poppy (implied by the opening context) to the heating and cleansing virtues of honey, the properties of Moly (or darnel), and the astringent nature of myrtle berries. It maintains the framework of Galenic humoral theory, categorizing substances by their "degrees" of heat and dryness.</meta>

...it sits deeply and heavily, much like those in a **lethargic stupor** <term>lēthargikois; a state of drowsy dullness or unconsciousness, often categorized in ancient medicine as a "cold" disease of the brain.</term>. When applied to the head with rose oil, it dissolves **headaches** <note>original: "kephalalgias"</note> caused by heat. It also treats pus in the ears and "milky" discharges when applied as a poultice to gouty inflammations. Its juice is biting and has a **cleansing** <term>ryptikos; a substance capable of "scrubbing" or purging impurities from the body's surfaces or vessels.</term> quality. The juice of the black variety is used, while **hydromel** <note>original: "hydromelo"; a mixture of honey and water, often fermented or used as a base for other medicines.</note> is drunk on its own.

**Honey** is hot and, according to the second degree of classification, is like **oxymel** <note>original: "oxeikron," likely a corruption of oxymeli; a mixture of honey and vinegar used to cut through phlegm.</note>. It is fine-particled and cleansing, possessing a certain sharpness. Because of this, it encourages excretion and makes the bowels more **diuretic** <term>diourētikōteron; promoting the passage of urine to flush the system.</term>. It does not upset the stomach if it is mixed with plenty of water and boiled until the "thinness" <note>likely referring to the watery impurities</note> and the accumulation of foam ceases. Honey is suitable only for the elderly and those with **cold temperaments** <term>psychrais krasesin; in Galenic medicine, the elderly were thought to have naturally "cold" and "moist" constitutions, requiring "hot" substances like honey to restore balance.</term>. For those in their prime and of a hot nature, it turns into **bile** <note>original: "choloūtai"</note>. If taken hot, it purges the passages of sharp humors; hence it provides more nourishment. The best honey is that from the combs; it is most beneficial when it is sweet, sharp, yellow, and of a balanced consistency between thick and thin. Spring honey is superior to summer honey, and winter honey is useless. If its mixture changes, it can stir the bile, especially in warmer stomachs. It cleanses the body of filth and preserves everything placed within it. For this reason, the ancients covered corpses with honey in their **sepulchers** <note>original: "gaithēkais"; literally "earth-chests" or tombs.</note>. It also benefits **epileptics** <note>original: "epilēptois"</note>, for the honey alone cleanses the head and thus manages the appetite. For those with phlegm and...

<meta>catchword: των- (the-)</meta>

---

<page-num>42</page-num>

...humor; it cuts through **thick phlegm** <note>original: "pachumerē ptuela"</note>. We say that these various types are healing. However, if honey is not well digested, it causes **blockages** <term>emphraxeis; obstructions in the "vessels" or "pores" of the organs, a common diagnosis for internal pain or swelling.</term> in the liver and spleen. If eaten to excess, honey produces **scabies** <note>original: "psōras"; likely referring to various itchy skin eruptions or lesions caused by an imbalance of humors.</note>.

**What is called Moly or Darnel** <note>original: "mōly ē zizanion"; Moly is a mythical herb from Homer’s Odyssey, but in later medical texts, it was often identified with specific plants like 'zizanion' (darnel), a weed that can be toxic or cause dizziness.</note> is hot and dry in the second degree. It drives away the **melancholic humor** <term>chumon melancholikon; "black bile," believed to cause depression and physical sluggishness.</term>. Constant use of it causes a sickly complexion and melancholic conditions. Taken with vinegar, it opens blockages of the blood. It must be boiled, for through boiling, it loses much of its potency and ill-flavor.

**Myrtle berries** <note>original: "myrsikokokka"</note> are cold and dry in the first degree. They are beneficial for those **spitting blood** <note>original: "haimoptyikois"</note> and for "scoria" <note>likely referring to dross or impurities in the digestive tract</note>, as well as for those with a discharge from the bowels. However, it harms the bladder. The white berries are sweet and possess some heat. All of them "hold" the stomach <note>i.e., they act as an astringent to stop diarrhea</note>. They prevent hot and moist **vapors** <term>anathymiasēis; "exhalations" rising from the stomach to the head, thought to cause various ailments.</term>. The leaves of the plant do the same when smelled or applied to the head. They are also useful for **toothaches** <note>original: "odontalgias"</note> caused by heat, if one rinses the mouth with a decoction while it is still warm. To keep the hair from falling out, myrtle berries are beneficial when mixed with **oak-gall** <term>kikidi; an abnormal growth on oak trees used in medicine and ink-making for its high tannin content.</term>, crushed with oil, and applied to the head. <margin>153.</margin> For those bitten by a **viper or snake** <note>original: "echidnēs ē opheōs"</note>, the juice of the leaf with **marjoram** <note>original: "origanō"</note>, applied as an ointment with **honey-water** <note>original: "melikratou"</note>, is effective. **Democritus** <note>The famous Pre-Socratic philosopher, to whom many later medical and alchemical secrets were attributed.</note> says that the myrtle has a certain natural sympathy for this use.

<meta>catchword: φυ- (na-)</meta>

<summary>This page details the medicinal uses of honey, moly, and myrtle berries, emphasizing honey’s warming and preservative qualities and myrtle’s astringent properties for treating blood-spitting and hair loss. It references the philosopher Democritus and provides specific humoral classifications for each substance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s encyclopedic survey of medicinal substances, specifically focusing on the "Element X" (Xi) and beginning "Element O" (Omicron). The text moves from Mastic to Carobs, then into a lengthy discussion on the varieties and trade of Agarwood, concluding with the properties of Rice. It remains grounded in the Galenic system of degrees of heat and dryness.</meta>

...the names of plants placed near each other become very delicate; their roots intertwine with one another, and they can be seen coming from a single source.

**Mastic** <note>original: "Mastichē"; a resin obtained from the mastic tree, used since antiquity for digestion and breath freshening.</note> is hot and dry and of the highest quality in its season. It benefits those afflicted by **viscous phlegm** <term>glischrou phlegmatos; a thick, sticky bodily fluid associated with sluggishness and digestive blockages in Galenic medicine.</term>. It evacuates the phlegm in that same area, rising up to four drops, and possesses as much power as a purgative. It completely resolves cold-related ailments. This is true not only when taken internally, but also when applied externally as a warm **poultice** <note>original: "plassomenē"</note>. Likewise, the oil prepared from it is beneficial. Its scent carries a certain quality.

***

**Carobs** <note>original: "Xylokerata"; literally "wooden horns," referring to the fruit of the carob tree.</note> are said to come from the Vrata region, or so they are named. This fruit is criticized for having **bad juices** <term>kakochyma; food that produces harmful or imbalanced humors upon digestion.</term> for everyone, being woody even when one walks [past it?]. However, the modern physicians say these provide strength to the **stomach** <note>original: "stomachon"</note> when taken after peeling. It strongly stops **diarrhea** <term>gastrorhoias; literally "stomach-flows," a general term for loose bowels.</term> when used before meals.

***

**Agarwood** <note>original: "Xylaloē"; the fragrant, resinous wood used in medicine and incense, often imported from the East.</note> has a good flavor but is **residue-producing** <term>perittōmatikos; a substance that leaves behind "superfluities" or waste products in the body after digestion.</term> and hard to digest. Because of this, it is better to completely abstain from eating it. But if one wishes to partake of it vigorously, let him treat it with **pungent spices** <note>original: "drimési artymasi"</note>. It benefits all chronic **hemorrhoidal** <note>original: "haimorrhoidikon"</note> conditions, for although it is harmful [to digestion], it is medicinal. The tree grows in various places, occurring in different regions and seasons. Some varieties are quite flavorless, but the *Memphitic* <note>original: "memphechidia," likely referring to a specific trade variety from Egypt</note> variety excels over the others. A preparation of rose oil... for those among you...
<meta>catchword: smooth</meta>

<column-break/>

...smooth but rather causing harm. <unclear>It moves</unclear> and is covered by a great deal of color beneath. It is similar in color and border. They break the wood and sell it to **merchants** <note>original: "emporois"</note>. Others who are more level-headed in other lands scrape these and throw away the bark. They create the appearance of a [specific] kind in this way; however, the wood is of different types. It is fixed for ten days and they are valued for the scent they possess. Like sapphires or some similar solid substance among the city-dwellers. It is clear that the use of similar [woods] should cease, because he says these are no longer strictly "woods" according to the physicians.

Regarding the liquid variety, there are again four differences. One is distinguished by its **astringency** <term>stryphnotētos; a harsh, binding quality that constricts tissues and stops flows.</term>. All of these are hot and dry. They are restrictive to the bowels and hard on the stomach, especially the bitter variety. After this is the "Median" kind. The rest of the varieties close up the flows and cause hemorrhoids. Those that return again cause **obstructions** <term>ymphraxis; a blockage in the body's channels or "pores."</term> and melt away **dropsy** <term>hydrōpēpas; an accumulation of watery fluid in the tissues or cavities of the body.</term> in the belly. When the membranous kinds are used as **incense** <note>original: "thymiōmenai"</note> continually, they produce a sweet scent. Only the Median kind prevents the transformation of these substances into **pungency** <note>original: "drimytētai"</note>.

***

-> Element O <-

***

**Rice** <note>original: "Oryza"</note> is hot and dry in the first degree, and also in the second. It is sufficiently **nutritious** <term>trophimos; providing substantial sustenance to the body's tissues.</term>. The cooked kind—specifically the "sycamore-fig" variety, as the bitter one says—holds firm. For it does not fall away like seed. It is used for the complete nourishment of the stomach. To introduce a different opinion, so that I may bring it to him: white rice moderately binds the **bowels** <note>original: "derēn"; likely a corruption for "gastēra" (belly) as rice was known to stop diarrhea.</note> more frequently.

### On the Use of Wines

Some use wines for the sake of the benefit derived from them, others because of an impulse toward pleasure. Still others choose wines for their aromas. Among those that are warming and digestible <term>kataptikōn; substances that aid in the "cooking" or breaking down of food in the stomach.</term>, they occupy the first rank. **Old wine** is highly digestible. **Medium wine** is second in digestibility. It is the nature of wine to nourish the whole body. **Red and thick wine** is most suitable for the production of blood, whereas medium wine requires only a short transformation to become blood. Next to this is **black wine**. **White wine** is the sweetest. In addition to these is the **indescribable tawny wine** <note>original: "kirros"; an amber or yellowish-orange wine color often mentioned in Byzantine and Classical texts.</note>; although it has very little power in itself, it nourishes the body. Thick-bodied wines are digested more slowly than thin-bodied ones. Thin wines are safer because they are **diuretic** <term>diourētikoi; substances that promote the production of urine.</term>. Thick wines help food digest together, but some of them cause blockages in the **liver, spleen, and kidneys**. Consequently, those who use them excessively may suffer from **dropsy** <term>yderiōsin; a condition characterized by an accumulation of fluid in the body's tissues.</term> or **kidney stones** <term>lithiōsi; the formation of stony concretions in the urinary tract.</term>. This is especially true for those whose **temperament** <term>krasin; the specific mixture of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that determines a person's health.</term> is not balanced. Red, thick wine is not very beneficial for them. For **phlegmatic people** <note>Those with a constitution dominated by "phlegm," which is cold and moist, often leading to sluggishness.</note>, old wine is best, as are thin tawny and thin white wines. Wine does not only nourish; it also rekindles the spirit, stimulates the excretion of waste, and increases the **innate heat** <term>emphyton thermon; the vital heat believed by ancient physicians to be the source of life and digestion, centered in the heart.</term>. Many people who use it—

<column-break/>

—experience this. For when they feast in such a way, they turn toward disorder. Yet wine has a particularly unique quality: its delightful essence passes into the limbs and brings them to life. It is distributed through the blood, and its multifaceted nature warms the limbs, creates **good breathing** <note>original: "eupnoia"; can refer to healthy respiration or a "good spirit" / "sweet breath."</note>, and carries off urine. Some people excrete a significant amount of waste through it. The energy of wine is even more evident in bodies that are cold and moist. It is clear that it acts upon the innate heat much like the bitter substances among recognized medicines. **Old wine** settles the stomach and clears **blockages** <term>emfraxeis; obstructions in the body's channels or "pores" that prevent the flow of humors and waste.</term>. It is beneficial for those in whose bodies **phlegmatic humor** <note>original: "phlegmatikos chymos"</note> has gathered. **Medium wine**, however, causes rumblings in the gut because of its presence; it produces thick humor and causes **heaviness in the head** <term>karēbarias; a dull, weighted sensation in the head, often associated with congestion or intoxication.</term> and headaches. The use of wine can be prepared by being heated over a fire. For they say that when it is bright—explaining the mixing of wine—the speed of its transformation is such that nothing is hostile, provided one drinks well and falls [to sleep?]. Regarding its ability to gladden the soul: some say it thins the **melancholic humor** <term>melancholikon chymon; "black bile," the cold and dry humor associated with sadness, introspection, and "melancholy."</term> itself, while others say it expands and creates space for the blood. This blood becomes bitter in those who have an excess of it, carrying it into the head. These people suffer in the thighs <note>original: "mira"; the text here is slightly obscure, perhaps referring to the distribution of humors to the lower extremities.</note>, perhaps because of the "cutting" <note>original: "peritomēn"; likely used here metaphorically for a sharp separation or purging of the humor.</note> of the humor acting on the body—but indeed, wines alter the soul. As has been paradoxically stated, wine brings joy through the soul, establishing good cheer and courage...

<summary>This page provides a detailed medical and psychological profile of various types of wine (red, white, tawny, old, and new). Psellos explains how different wines affect the humors, aid or hinder digestion, and influence the soul's emotional state, particularly regarding courage and joy.</summary>

### On Vinegar
<note>This section discusses the properties and medical uses of vinegar.</note>

Regarding vinegar, there are many ambiguities. From each of its parts, it takes a share of heat in its perceptions. Some, however, say that it is cold to swallow at first, not in a fixed way. They say it falls down dry, but it is used by us against the damage to the spirits caused by poisons. For instance, the drinking of **aconite** <note>original: "akonitou"; wolfsbane, a highly toxic plant</note> disappears, and it treats the bitterness of the people’s burial; and it is a drink for thick and foul juices. Every use of it is beneficial...

<column-break/>

<page-num>25</page-num>
...not hot. None of these things are woven into the springs of those who rinse with it. Souls... the speech of sharp things; it stops the confusion directed toward the heat. 

<margin>
<unclear>...</unclear>
</margin>
When applied to those soaked in blood, it restrains the rush of the blood. It is used against the bite of the <unclear>creature</unclear> so that it does not foam up. Also, for those with **jaundice** <term>ikteriōntas; a yellowing of the skin and eyes caused by biliary imbalance</term> whose eyes are filthy and clouded, this heat is applied continuously. For stings, this heat is poured over them. It stops the mixture with heat and greatly benefits the heart by quieting the **bile** <term>cholēn; one of the four cardinal humors, specifically yellow bile associated with heat and anger</term>. It also helps those who are drunk or acting madly, and for vomiting, it is best for those suffering from **fainting** <term>leipomorian; a state of exhaustion or swooning</term>. Likewise, it is used for cold and **melancholic temperaments** <term>melancholikois; the humor associated with black bile, coldness, and dryness</term> and for the "juice of the foam." But drinking it excessively thins the body due to a lack of blood. If it pours out a cold mixture, it is for those who are weak; then spirits in the... the membranes. If ever this is useful, it heals the arteries, though it can be harmful to them. Again, for those with pus and very hot inflammations... <unclear>stone</unclear>. Regarding those from fiery... infusion, it perfectly dissolves them. It shows a sneezing property toward the nourishment of the parts' specific qualities. For those with wounds, it is used as a remedy; in its heat, it prevents their inflammation. And the steam of this, when boiling, if applied to the ears, cures **ringing** <note>original: "ēchon"; tinnitus</note> and **hardness of hearing** <term>barykoïas; the Byzantine term for hearing loss or deafness</term>. Vinegar is suitable and beneficial for inflammations of the heat, and against itching of the body and...

Those who have a warm stomach and happen to be yellow-bilious are harmed even more by this. <margin>+ <unclear>??</unclear> +</margin> For it is [purgative] and pungent, and it prepares the body to receive even more of it; it produces a more pungent humor in such bodies. In addition to these things, the phlegmatic should take the juice in this way: either by adding heat, while the yellow-bilious should apply it even to the skin, because that which lies near the skin is difficult to destroy. They prefer smaller melons over the larger ones for those who are sedentary, as they are more diuretic.

Regarding the **parotid glands** <note>the large salivary glands located in front of the ears</note> and painful **abscesses** <note>original: "apostematon"</note>, it is effective. It breaks down the **phlegmatic humor** <note>one of the four bodily fluids in ancient medicine, associated with cold and moisture</note>, providing rapid relief; and it soothes pains. It quickly removes the **scabs** <note>original: "escharas"</note> from **carbuncles** <term>anthrakōn; localized skin infections that can form crusty scabs</term> when applied as a poultice with honey or **soapwort** <note>original: "samphidos", likely a corruption of saphidos or sapōn</note>.

The juice of unripe mulberries does not only provide warmth, but also imparts strength to the **stomach** <note>original: "gastri"</note>. In these cases, it also acts as a **deobstruent** <term>apophraktikon; a medicine that opens the pores or vessels</term> for obstructions in the **liver** and the **spleen**. However, those who are **bilious** <term>xanthocholous; literally "yellow-biled," referring to the choleric temperament</term> must abstain from this; for it heats such people and softens the internal organs, and harms the stomach. It makes these people **melancholic** <note>referring to an excess of black bile</note>. But if someone drinks its juice... because it wounds. And if one partakes of its leaves for fifteen days, it acts similarly; and if one partakes of it... the person lying down will not be harmed by **venom** <note>original: "deleteriou"</note>.

## + Beginning of the letter Rho: +

**Pomegranates** <note>original: "rhoai"</note> are cold and moist. Of these, some are sweet and others are sour; others are mixed in their quality. The sweet ones benefit the **chest** <note>original: "thorax"</note> and produce good humors; they possess a certain amount of warmth. The sour ones are **diuretic** <note>original: "diouretikai"; promoting the production of urine</note> and are useful against **yellow-bile** conditions and for the quantity of **semen**. They are beneficial for the heat held by the stomach. They are useful... and for those suffering from severe biting of the **stomach** due to wicked humors. The juice with **barley-meal** <note>original: "alphitois"</note> is bitter or... as **Hippocrates** <note>the famous 5th-century BCE Greek physician</note> says regarding the... when dissolved... the liver... the juice of the sweet pomegranate...

<column-break/>

...with unmixed juice of the sour pomegranate for **hemorrhoids** <note>original: "hemorois"</note>. It flows... and they dissolve empty biles from both sides. The liver... the juice of the sour variety applied to the **eyes** with honey produces **sharpness of vision** <term>oxyderkeian</term>, especially when it is aged. The seeds of the sour pomegranates, when we are concerned about the **residue of food**, prevent the flow of **waste products** <note>original: "perittomatōn"</note>. For more bitter... but the juice of the sour variety, when applied, often cuts off severe **vomiting**. It also calms **inflammations of the liver** resulting from **wine-drinking** <note>original: "oinoposias"</note>. All pomegranates and their seeds cut off the urge for **sexual intercourse** <term>aphrodisia</term>, and especially... the temperament of the cold ones.

## Rhubarb or Mulberry <note>original: "rha d' ē moron"; rha likely refers to Rhubarb</note>

They have placed it in the third order of things that **cool** and **moisten**. Others place it in the first order... because it is bitter, it brings forth warm tears. They cut off **phlegmatic humor**, and for this reason they... and are quickly corrupted in the stomach. Because of this, those who partake of these too frequently and to the point of satiety are seized by **fevers**. They cause... the measures. One must take these... from food and drink **unmixed wine** <note>original: "akraton"</note> after them. Those that are ripe... turn tears, this bit... and the dry ones, more so. The juice of the leaves... removes **worms** <term>helminthas; intestinal parasites</term>. Likewise, the... of the leaves when applied. By another way, the **roses**...

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical section for the letter Rho, transitioning from the discussion of pomegranates (begun on the previous page) to the medicinal properties of the radish and the wild carrot.</meta>

...stimulates the appetite for food; it is beneficial for an **inflamed stomach** <note>original: "phlegmainousē gastri"</note> and for **bile** <note>original: "cholē"; one of the four humors</note> when applied as a poultice. The **radish** <note>original: "raphanis"; likely *Raphanus sativus*</note> affects the mind less than other plants; and it is often consumed as a delicacy, though it sends forth a fiery vapor through the breath, which does not cease for those dwelling in the excess of **pollution** <note>original: "molysmou"; likely referring to an imbalance of humors</note>. For those who are "low-heat" <note>original: "oligothermois"; those with a cold temperament</note>, it does not even dissolve, but remains for a long time as a source of grief. One must observe that for some of these people, drinking water should be offered with the food.

The **radish**, when warmed and swallowed after other foods, follows the principles which the **Ancient One** <note>original: "palamos," likely a corruption of "palaios," referring to an ancient authority like Galen</note> used as evidence there. Just as some peoples offer this without bread to those who are suffering, so too is the **wild carrot** <note>original: "daukion"; likely *Daucus carota* or parsnip</note>; for he says that those in the cities eat this raw due to ignorance, while those in the fields, and especially... offer it frequently. He wonders at those who take this after dinner, as it... the **tender** <note>original: "aimallon," likely "hapalon"</note> variety, or that which is harvested by day. **Oribasius** <note>a 4th-century Byzantine medical compiler</note> says that this moves the **bowels** <note>original: "gastera"</note> when taken after food; but when taken before food, it is more suitable as an **emetic** <term>emetón; a substance that induces vomiting</term>. Another foul pleasure appears to me... I seem to see these accounts agreeing with one another. They do not conflict when one examines the dispositions of the food accurately. For those who were about to be sated... and it does not float on the surface. For these people, it is proper to take radishes before food; but for those whose stomach is **flatulent** <note>original: "empneumatōthei"</note> and the food floats on the surface, and for those with weak stomachs...

<column-break/>

...it happens that all of it should be offered after the meal. They are also easily spoiled, and for this reason, they produce **belches** <note>original: "oryzas," likely "erygmous"</note>. It is necessary for those who belch in this way after use to abstain from them. It happens that the same effects occur in those who are flatulent. They have a certain **pungency** <note>original: "drimytēta"</note> and thin parts through which they most especially cut through **phlegm** <note>original: "phlegma"; the cold, moist humor</note>. They also cause wind in the body... and they blunt the teeth and the **trachea** <term>tracheian artērian; the "rough artery" or windpipe</term>. They also make the voice **hoarse** <note>original: "yphanchōsi"</note>. By a certain property, they are beneficial to **arthritics** <note>those suffering from joint pain</note>... They are also **diuretic** <term>diourētikai; promoting the passage of urine</term>.

**Dioscorides** <note>the 1st-century author of *De Materia Medica*</note> says something similar regarding the senses; and the **Indians** <note>original: "indoi"</note> say they even provide a natural talent and make the **intellect** <note>original: "dianoian"</note "larger" (more expansive). The decoction of these is said to be bad, but it is a **deobstruent** <term>apophraktikon; a medicine that clears blockages</term>. Because of this, it has often benefited those with **jaundice** <note>original: "ikterikois"</note>, specifically those having obstructions in the **liver**, the **kidneys**, and the **bladder**. Thus, they have provided much **sharpness of vision** <note>original: "oxyderkeian"</note>. The juice of these, when mixed with **rose oil** <note>original: "rhodimō elaiō"</note> and warmed and poured into the ears, heals **hardness of hearing** <note>original: "dyskoïan"</note>. Especially when offered by us, they heal **hemorrhoids** <note>original: "aimorrhoïdas"</note>. They are beneficial due to their sharpness, and when taken before food, they help the **hypochondria** <note>the upper abdomen, below the ribs</note>. With **honey**, they are useful for **coughs** and **shortness of breath** <term>dyspnoias; difficulty breathing</term>. Their leaves, when applied, reduce **swelling** <note>original: "oulētikos," likely "oidēmatikos"</note>. Some also offer these with **oxymel** <term>oinomeliti; a medicinal mixture of wine and honey</term> for the sake of vomiting; they purge much phlegm and relieve hemorrhoids.

...quartan fevers <note>original: "tetartaiōn pyretōn"; a fever that recurs every four days, historically associated with malaria</note>. However, by some specific property, it harms the testicles. ### The Letter Sigma (Σ)

**Figs** <note>original: "syka"</note>. They are heating according to the first degree and tend toward dryness. The pressed [dried] ones are of the first [degree]. They provide significant nourishment; similarly, they alone make the flesh firm and strong, just as bread and meat do. Elsewhere, however, they are somewhat spongy. They fill the stomach with **wind** <note>original: "physas"; gas or flatulence</note> if they are not well digested. They are [better] than other fruits and are flavorful. They stimulate the bowels and pass through easily. Their cleansing property is also noteworthy, as they excrete many sandy particles <note>likely referring to the seeds or fiber acting as a laxative</note> during their passage. Ripe figs, when eaten, do not cause harm at all. Much like **dried figs** <note>original: "ischasi"</note>, they have many...

<column-break/>

...useful properties, but a drawback is that they produce blood that is not entirely pure. They are also said to be a cause of **lice** <note>original: "phtheira"; a common ancient belief that certain foods caused "spontaneous generation" of parasites</note>. One should not rely solely on the use of figs, but should balance them with [lighter] food. **Galen** <note>original: "Galēnos"; the preeminent 2nd-century Roman physician</note> says, as he often mentions, that by eating these fruits from his twentieth year until old age, he remained free of all ailments because he consumed only figs and grapes. He notes that even those who avoided the [unhealthy] cause, such as his father, remained healthy by following this practice. Figs also provide protection against poisons <note>original: "dēlētēria"</note>; if one uses them daily, they possess the power of a **medicine** <note>original: "pharmakon"</note>.

**Over-ripe grapes** <note>original: "staphylai"</note> are heating and moist in the first degree. They are more nourishing than other seasonal fruits, or even than figs, and they do not produce "bad humors." If they have been ripened thoroughly by strong sunlight, their flesh becomes dense. Elsewhere... Galen says they are good for women... [to help] the bowels. Therefore, even in [daily] life, they are [valued]. For they do not fall [heavily] and are well-digested; rather, they produce a raw humor that is easily transformed into blood. The nourishment provided by **dried figs** <note>original: "ischadōn"</note> is dry but flavorful; it is primarily processed in the intestines. **Grapes that are hung up** <note>to dry or preserve</note> neither [spoil] quickly nor do they over-stimulate [the bowels]. They are more easily digested than others. The [texture] is different. All moist...

**Grapes** <note>original: "staphylai"</note> are both sweet and sour; and their temperaments <note>the balance of the four humors: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note> are [distinct]. For the sweet ones have a warmer **juice** <note>original: "chylon"; refers to the fluid produced by digestion</note>, while the sour ones are colder. The sweet ones induce **sleep**; and especially the <unclear>gymnosymirai</unclear> <note>This term is likely a corrupted name for a specific grape variety</note>. All of these are of a suitable temperament when they are fleshy and ripe; and one should partake of them in moderation. For some, [digestion] is preserved when the digestion of **almonds** is taken alongside them. By being hung up, they also become sweet. The white and <unclear>phylai</unclear> ones provide more nourishment; they are [better] for the transition [of seasons] and are soft. All of them, however, are superficial in their nourishment and dim the **appetite** if they happen [to be eaten in excess]. They do not easily <unclear>systasamoun</unclear>—that is, they contain moisture—nor are they dry, being cold and **pneumatic** <note>original: "pneumatikēs"; relating to "pneuma" or internal gases/wind</note>. This [quality] is not of the flesh itself, but from the scattered smoky moisture. Furthermore, they are beneficial for the **liver**, and for the membranes and the livers of those [suffering] from the [after-effects] of **fever**. Those that are **sour** <note>original: "omphakizousai"; refers to verjuice or unripe grapes</note> are cold and moist; they are astringent and good for the stomach. They settle the heat arising from **yellow bile** and the swelling of the **spleen**. The juice of these, when aged, becomes dull by boiling, but it helps those [in need] and restrains the excessive flow [of humors], even for <unclear>maiētois</unclear> and <unclear>phylas</unclear>. But if you consider all these fruits, it is better to consume them before all other food.
The **vertebrae** <note>This may be a metaphorical reference to the structure of the vine or a corrupted medical term</note> are warm-tempered...

<column-break/>

...the more oily ones, and those having a thinner skin, and the sweeter ones—these are more [easily] rubbed <note>processed by the stomach</note> than the <unclear>phyloumesi</unclear>. They do not cause [blockages], and they partake of a sleep-inducing and **diaphoretic** <note>original: "diaphorētikēs"; a substance that induces sweating to release toxins</note> power. When they become like **dried figs** <note>original: "ischades"</note>, they share the nature of figs; the sweeter they are, the warmer they are; the <unclear>auanroterai</unclear> <note>perhaps "darker"</note> are colder. These strengthen the **stomach**. They do not [cause blockages] in the belly, nor do they settle there. The sweet ones do this: they neither relax the stomach, nor do they cause suspicions [of illness] in the womb. They have an **obstructive** <note>original: "ekphraktikon"</note> quality; or rather, as is shown by **raisins** <note>original: "staphides"</note>, they are **laxative** <note>original: "hypaktikon"</note> and **cleansing** <note>original: "ryptikon"</note>. They are better for the stomach than those [fresh grapes]. They benefit the area around the **lungs**. They strengthen the liver and the [gallbladder] pipe; especially the sensory parts of the liver. They provide a solution for those who are experienced [in illness], for the liver suffers when it becomes hard and earthy from **fever**. Their powers [act] like **purgative medicines**; he says this not by a [simple] property, but by their ability to easily [benefit] the womb and to lessen the **phlegm** in the body, which increases in those who eat [excessively]. Let him eat raisins... except for daily [meals]. These pains are prepared from the... of raisins, or that of **honey**; some [are dried] in the sun... not even the same value as water.

**Sugar** <note>original: "sachar"</note> is hot and moist according to the **first degree** <note>In Galenic medicine, qualities were measured in four degrees of intensity</note>. It has a cleansing and diaphoretic power, just like **honey**; however, it [is less] troublesome in the stomach.

<meta>This page continues the medical discourse on dietary and herbal properties, transitioning from the previous discussion of grapes and fruits to the medicinal uses of herbs like horehound and celery, likely based on Galenic and Dioscoridean medical traditions.</meta>

**Horehound** <note>original: "prasion"; *Marrubium vulgare*, a bitter herb traditionally used for respiratory issues</note> has a **cutting** <note>original: "tmētikē"; an ancient medical term for substances that break down thick, sticky humors or phlegm</note> quality; it is beneficial for those with ailments of the chest, the kidneys, and the bladder. It is known that its juice, when cooled, makes those who take it thin; it is given to those with **warmer bellies** <note>referring to a "hot" constitution or digestive tract in humoral theory</note>. It is not particularly good for the stomach. It is better when it is quite old. The thin variety is used for the abdomen and for warmer temperaments; however, it causes a headache. When it is diluted with water, especially when taken with cold water, it has a cooling effect. Though it has a pleasant smell, it is not beneficial for those suffering from **rheum in the head** <note>original: "rheumatizomenois"; refers to the downward flow of excess fluid/mucus from the brain</note>. It is notably heating and digestive, and one should drink it entirely, for it acts as a diuretic and an expectorant <note>original: "apothorakistikon"; literally "clearing the chest"</note>. It is quite pleasant, yet it can cause gas and is used for spleen ailments and a bad stomach. Some people are wary of it for this reason, and it should be taken immediately before sealing the meal <note>likely meaning before the final course or as a digestive aid</note>. It tends to bind the stomach. Due to a certain unique property, it is harmful to **epileptics** <note>original: "epilēptikous"</note>. Such individuals must abstain from it entirely; for often it has caused an onset of epilepsy in us even when we were otherwise healthy. It is also harmful to the testicles. The leaves possess a greater power of action. For all these uses, the roots are very broad. **Celery** <note>original: "selinon"</note> is excellent for those who are thick-fleshed about the breast. It is cleansing for the kidneys and the bladder. It has a pleasant aroma with food and clears **obstructions** <note>original: "emphraxeis"</note> in the belly, the veins, and the arteries. The seed alone, when taken, is digestive. Celery contributes to the well-being of the

<column-break/>

body; many have been cured of persistent reproductive issues and **body odor** <note>original: "dysōdias"</note> through its constant use. It is most beneficial to women for their joints when used as a decoction, a hip-bath, or on its own. It is said that when used with **aphrodisiacs** <note>original: "aphrodimomou," likely a corruption of *aphrodisiakōn*</note>, it helps expel stones; it treats painful urination <note>original: "dysourian"</note> and heals <unclear>morphrou</unclear> <note>possibly a corruption referring to a skin condition or "morphea"</note>. However, those who are **breastfeeding** <note>original: "thēlazousas"</note> must abstain from its use, for it reduces the milk. Its juice is a useful antidote for those suffering from the effects of drugs or resins. The wild variety also has medicinal power against **kerion** <note>original: "kēria"; a type of inflammatory, honeycomb-like skin ulcer</note>.

<margin>for <unclear>the liver?</unclear></margin>
## Celery
**Celery** is hot and dry and is meant to be eaten. Its scent and juice are of a cleansing nature. When the belly is [distended] or feverish, it cleanses the stomach. It is most useful for those with **colder temperaments**. For this reason, it is somewhat harder on the stomach than other vegetables, as the expenditure of energy to digest it is greater. Being a diuretic, it clears obstructions wherever they may be. As we said before, it is not a desirable remedy for indigestion. Regarding its juice, it is harmful to those with liver complaints and those prone to **vertigo** <note>original: "iliggykous"; dizziness or fainting spells</note>. Thus, if the body is thick-parted or cold, it is edible and supportive of a healthy habit of body. And for this reason, nature has made it known that when mixed with water it cools and binds the stomach, especially through its fragrance.

<meta>This page continues the medical discourse from page 93. Having discussed horehound and its effects on the respiratory system, the text now transitions into the properties of leeks and garlic, focusing on their humoral effects (heating and drying) and their roles as both food and medicine in the Galenic tradition.</meta>

...of [leeks] <note>original: "prasou"; likely the conclusion of a sentence from the previous page regarding leeks or a similar pungent herb</note> being green. It is phlegmatic <note>having the qualities of phlegm, one of the four bodily humors</note> and haematic <note>bloody</note> in nature, and is thick; hence it is digestive and causes flatulence, and it stimulates the stomach. If one burns this, pours **honey** over it, and washes the head with the mixture, it prevents **scurf** <note>original: "achōra"; a skin disease of the scalp, similar to dandruff or seborrheic dermatitis</note> from turning into bald patches.
<margin>H</margin> It also [treats] **scorpion stings**. A little of its salty juice, if one can be persuaded to take it, provides no nourishment to the body's cavity; it eliminates raw humors. One should not consume this when eating pungent foods. For this reason, only this ancient [preparation] is chosen. It is said that if one takes a soft wick and coats it with "copper water" <note>likely a solution of copper salts or verdigris</note>, it will make the copper appear black due to the flash of the mixture.

<margin>H</margin> **Horehound** <note>original: "prasion"; possibly referring back to leeks (*prason*) given the context of smell and teeth, but the Greek specifies the herb horehound</note> is of good juice and provides sufficient nourishment. It is especially beneficial for those who suffer from a **warmer temperament** <note>the "krasis" or internal balance of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note> of the liver. It possesses the cooling nature of hens, which is the most hardy. It participates in the symmetry of the humors, especially those of the liver. Through it, the body acquires a healthy state and produces blood, provided one uses it in moderation.
<margin>H</margin> It heats, and this heating is of the **first order** <note>the lowest level of intensity in the Galenic system of four degrees</note>. It is moist and [easily] discharged only by the force of its own nature. It is oily and quickly fills those who eat it. It is, however, unsuitable for the stomach and the [esophagus], even as it is being digested. It gives the body a pale color and causes thirst through touch. If any of this remains caught in the teeth, it produces a **foul odor** in the body.

<column-break/>

They also stimulate the **menstrual flow** <note>original: "katamēnia"</note>; however, they are harmful to the [blood/bile]. Both are very productive of humors and cause **obstructions** <note>original: "emphraxeis"; blockages in the narrow vessels or "pores" of the body</note> in the passages <gloss>or the channels</gloss>.

<margin>H</margin> **Garlic** <note>original: "skorodon"</note> is hot and dry in the middle of the **fourth degree** <note>the most intense level of medicinal potency, where a substance is nearly a drug or poison</note>. It is most suitable for those who have thick and viscous, or raw or clotted humors, as it clears these out and cuts through them. It possesses a certain "bad juice" <note>original: "kakochymon"; referring to an undesirable nutritional quality</note> which it loses when boiled in water. For those with **cold temperaments**, it is often considered a guardian of health; however, it is upsetting for those of a hot temperament, and is especially bad for the **eyes**, the head, the lungs, and the kidneys. It has a unique property of stopping thirst caused by salty humors. It expels **worms** and disperses internal swellings. Although it may harm the vision through its pungent vapors, it is beneficial for the "cutting" [of humors] in those with warm bodies, and produces a good complexion in those with cold ones. It is heating and greatly incites **sexual desire**. It is an **antidote** <note>original: "antipharmakon"</note> against both lethal poisons and the bites of venomous creatures, acting almost exactly like a **theriac** <note>original: "thēriaka"; a famous ancient compound "universal" medicine used against poisons</note>. For this reason, **Galen** <note>the famous 2nd-century Greek physician whose system dominated medicine for over a millennium</note> calls this the **"poor man's theriac."** Snakes flee from it just as they do from **rue**. Because of this, the Persians, before they [travel], fill [themselves] with garlic. It is said that if a beast partakes of a poison, or is about to be harmed, taking garlic as a remedy is very useful.

<meta>This page continues the pharmaceutical catalog, moving from the discussion of leeks and pungent herbs to the specific medicinal properties of garlic. The same occurs regarding the wind <note>original: "pneuma"; here referring to internal gases or flatulence</note> if shown in the weight of an obol <note>an ancient Greek unit of weight</note>. It is clear from this that by a certain similarity to the heat within **garlic** <note>original: "skordō"</note>, it acts upon the contained growth through its heat. For this reason, it is also mixed into the treatment of ulcers... portions. So as to gather their vegetable leaves and shake them off. It is not only dispersive of winds, but it also checks the flow <note>catarrh or discharge</note> of the stomach and colicky conditions arising from wind. It provides a complete remedy for chronic **sciatica** <note>original: "ischiasas"; nerve pain in the hip and leg</note> and for the dissolution of **phlegm** <note>one of the four bodily humors, considered cold and moist</note>. It thins the nourishment and the blood and clears the complexion of the face. It also cleanses the windpipe <note>original: "tracheian artērian"</note>. If it is applied to an aching tooth, or for those pains in the ears... or if one adds a small amount to liquid eye-medicines. It draws out [humors] when used with healing ointments for arthritis caused by phlegm. It is also used for those not yet completely healed and for white leprosy <note>original: "alphos lepras"; likely referring to vitiligo or scaly skin patches</note>. Not only is it beneficial when taken internally for the bites of beasts <note>original: "koulo-dēgmata"; likely referring to dog or venomous bites</note> as we have already stated, but also when placed upon a bruise. For it is said to be useful against flatulent conditions and cold dispositions of the flesh. Especially where a swelling occurs; for those suffering from hemorrhoids, for physical wasting, for gastric flows, for **scrofula** <note>original: "choiradōn"; tuberculosis of the neck's lymph nodes</note>, and for hectic fevers. [Prepared] with much dust in oil, it acts as a raw cleanser.

<column-break/>

It exists for the moisture or winds in the head and the stomach. <gloss>no</gloss> <note>original: "ou"</note> It is also used for asthmatic conditions and for the disturbances and flows of phlegm. It acts by means of **clearing phlegm** <note>original: "apophlegmatismou"</note>. It clarifies the vision and treats the hot head and the hot liver. It assists in digestion by softening the thick parts of the nerves and the food. It dissolves the fluids in the bladder when applied as a poultice with **honey**. For other ailments these things are better. For those who have moistures and flows in every temperament, it should be taken with honey before the discharge of dregs. Applied as a poultice with dried figs, it is suitable for those with sciatica and abdominal complaints. For it draws that which is in the depths of the body toward the surface.

For when taken <gloss>half</gloss> <note>original: "ss"; likely an abbreviation for "semis," meaning half</note> because of the sharpness of the digestion... wherefore cheese is found by us to be sharper, hotter, and more burning. The fattier it is, the more it produces bad humors. In <gloss>half</gloss> the cutting of the parts... one must use it... then the thickness of the humor. There is nothing especially protective of the heat in such cheese, so that it does not evaporate at all. Neither the digestion within the body, nor the evacuations to the heavens <note>likely an idiomatic reference to the rising of vapors to the head</note>. As for those that produce good humors, or the wretched kind which is neither old nor [fresh]...

Regarding spring water, those springs that flow toward the north from white rocks and are <unclear>filtered</unclear> through sand are preferred. They are slower to heat up and slower to cool down. It passes through quickly, and if drunk cold, it is most painless and useful for digestion and <unclear>distribution</unclear> of nutrients. ### Broad Beans (Kyamoi)

The *babanoi*—that is, the broad beans <note>original: "kyamoi"; the fava bean was a staple but viewed with medical suspicion</note>—when dry, are **cold and dry** in the first degree. However, when green <note>original: "chloroi"; fresh or young beans</note>, they are **cold and moist**. They produce flesh that is not firm and dense, but rather spongy and loose. They are a flatulent food <note>original: "physodeis"; causing wind or gas</note> and cause a lack of sensation in the body, as they are filled with a universal pneuma <note>original: "pneumatos"; here referring to the digestive gas or "windy spirit" that carries throughout the body</note>. 

Because of their nature, they do not settle well in the inner organs, but instead deposit a great deal of wind. Yet, they also possess a certain cleansing property <note>original: "ryptikon"; a detergent or purifying quality</note>; for this reason, their flour can wipe away grime. They do not linger long in the stomach, nor do they possess the heavy power of "thick-humored" <term>thick-humored (pachychymoi): substances that make the blood and bodily fluids viscous or sluggish</term> foods. When eaten green, they affect the entire digestive tract. Of all the fruits we consume for nourishment, these provide a more moist nutrition to the body—not just in terms of the immediate digestion, but throughout one's entire physical state. 

They are not harmful to those who desire them and are prone to movement. Salt acts as a softener for them. As for the rest of the stomach's parts, beans fill them with wind and can cause heartburn <note>original: "kardialgikoi"; literally "heart-pain," but usually referring to the upper stomach or esophagus</note>, dizziness, and a "membrane-like" sensation <note>original: "meningizonta"; likely referring to pressure on the membranes of the brain</note> throughout the body. However, a decoction <note>original: "apozema"; a broth or boiled extract</note> of these beans can soothe the chest. They are especially beneficial when taken to ward off...

This does not merely affect a single part, but alters the very **temperament of the brain** <margin>temperament</margin>, causing both the senses and the mental faculties to become dull.

<meta>Continuing from the discussion on broad beans, Psellos now examines lentils and various types of Phaseoli (beans), evaluating their effects on the stomach, blood, and the soul’s disposition according to the Galenic system of humors.</meta>

### Lentils (Phakoi)

Lentils dry up the fluxes of the stomach; they affect the region of the stomach, the esophagus <note>original: "stomachou"; here referring specifically to the gullet or the "mouth" of the stomach</note>, and the intestines, reaching every part of the digestive tract. For this reason, they are a suitable food for the bowels and for those suffering from dysentery. When they are thoroughly boiled, they lose much of their natural potency; however, they become more nourishing than those that are not well-cooked. They are slower to pass and produce "thick humors" <term>thick humors (pachychymoi): substances that make the blood viscous, often leading to sluggishness or blockages</term>, having a binding effect on stomach flows, much like pitch.

Those who eat these in excess find them flatulent and they can lead to persistent ulcers and scirrhus <note>original: "skirrhois"; hard, cancerous, or non-inflammatory tumors</note>. They are particularly ill-suited for those with a melancholic <note>original: "melancholika"; referring to the humor of Black Bile, associated with earth and dryness</note> temperament, as lentils tend to thicken the blood and generate a melancholic humor. Only those whose bodies have a certain "bad habit" <note>original: "kachexia"; a state of ill health or malnutrition</note> in the stomach involving excess moisture might find lentils beneficial. For those who are already dry and parched, lentils are most harmful. Likewise, for those with eye conditions, they cause dimness of vision even as they dry out the skin. 

They are not suitable for the cleansing of the menstrual cycle <note>original: "emmenous katharseis"</note>, as they make the blood thick and difficult to move. Furthermore, they blunt the desire for sexual intimacy <note>original: "aphrodision orexin"</note> and the impulse toward it; therefore, they are useful to those who wish to practice temperance <note>original: "sophronein"</note>. One should avoid them, however, because they produce "melancholic vapors" <term>melancholic vapors: gasses produced during digestion that were believed to rise to the brain, causing depression or anxiety</term> which lead to distress. Most who eat them find they cause fears and dark dreams. Some people add beet to them during the boiling process for this reason. It is said that lentils are also good for treating wounds; specifically, when applied to a wound from a jagged

<column-break/>

arrow, they effectively check the flow of blood. A decoction made from small lentils, when consumed, is said to relieve difficult labor <note>original: "dystokian"</note> and also helps those who have a discharge of pus.

### Phaseoli (Kidney Beans)

The *phaseoli* <note>original: "phaseiloi"; likely referring to cowpeas or a variety of kidney bean</note> are **hot and moist** in the first degree. The redder varieties are even hotter than the others. They produce a thick and phlegmatic humor. According to <term>Galen (Galenos): the 2nd-century Roman physician whose theories dominated Byzantine medicine</term>, they hold a middle ground between seeds and wild plants. They provide a modest amount of nourishment. If they are eaten with their broth before other food, they turn the stomach; however, if they are consumed after other dishes, they lose most of their harmful qualities. The white *phaseoli* are thicker and more difficult to digest than the moister or red varieties. They tend to cause turbulent dreams <note>original: "tarachodeis oneirous"</note> and provoke the urge to urinate. A decoction of these also turns the stomach.

### Menstruation (Katamania)

Regarding the *phasioloi* <note>a spelling variant in the text</note>, they are quite beneficial and are capable of producing good, healthy blood. To put it simply, in terms of taste and nutrition, their "flesh" is very similar to that of hens <note>original: "alektoridon"; chickens or hens</note>; for their nature is akin to that of poultry.

### Pharoi and Nightingales

The *pharoi* <note>original: "pharoi"; possibly a specific pulse or a local variant of a bean</note> are difficult to digest, phlegmatic, and cause wind. Because of this, they should be eaten with caution. The larger ones are more difficult to consume, for the larger they are, the more "wicked" <note>original: "poniroteroi"; meaning harmful to the health</note> they become.

Nightingales <note>original: "philomela"; referring to the meat of the bird</note> consist of dense flesh. Because of this, they are hard to process and difficult to digest, and they are excessively...

...the pneumatic tunic of the flesh <gloss>of the flesh</gloss> having become compact. It nourishes well; it was said that this belongs to the "cutting of the fish." <gloss>of the</gloss> **Proclus**, when valuing the **Chaldean philosophy**, says that some speak "spiritually" <note>original: "pneumatodos"; referring to the pneuma or spirit-breath</note> and others "truly"; which is why he allows its great beauty to pass by.

### Phoukas (Fermented Grain Drink)

The so-called **phoukas** <note>original: "phoukas"; a fermented Byzantine beverage similar to ale or small beer, often made from barley or bread crusts</note>, when simply prepared, is cold. Compared to the qualities of water, it is warmer when dry. All the ancients considered it to produce "bad humors" <term>bad humors (kakochymia): a state where the bodily fluids are out of balance, often leading to disease</term>; they believe it causes "fluxes" <note>original: "reumata"; the downward flow of excess humors</note> and headaches, and it is not beneficial for the **holy disease** <note>original: "ieran noson"; the traditional term for epilepsy</note>. There is an addition made from rice for hangovers <gloss>and</gloss> for the sharpness of the blood, <gloss>for</gloss> it brings it to a rest. When the greatest part of its "tunic" <gloss>tunic</gloss> is taken through straining, it produces a very bitter juice from it, and it causes harm to our parts. For they say it is harmful to the eyes and the eyelids. However, it makes the skin smooth like marble for a time, for it softens it when used with wax. We have declared this to be beneficial for those with "fleshy humors" <gloss>of the flesh</gloss>, and we have shown that it is very useful for producing "good juices" <term>euchymia: the production of healthy, well-balanced blood and humors</term>. In truth, it benefits those who have the hottest temperament and especially those with hot bellies. When such a person is anointed with this in an excess of heat, it cools them; especially when they take more of it, it quenches thirst, stimulates the appetite for food, and turns the belly; often it also moves the urine. For in the interior of this house, even for those who have "souls of flesh," <note>a metaphor for those overly focused on physical health and bodily needs</note> it is superior to the "wood-cutters" <note>possibly a reference to those with coarse, dry constitutions</note>.

### Swine's Flesh (Pork)

**Pork** is highly productive of "good humors" <gloss>if</gloss> and is much easier to digest...

<column-break/>

...than others, because its parts are "half-similar" to our own raw flesh. <gloss>and</gloss> For at the same time, we would say it is the best of meats for hunger. And for this reason, some have warmed to it, <gloss>and</gloss> finding the usefulness of pork in its various forms. Among us, its use is cast aside <gloss>by</gloss> the common people, yet <gloss>and</gloss> it remains a most pleasant ally for the flesh when eaten raw by certain <gloss>animals</gloss>, but I hesitate <gloss>towards</gloss> the flesh of those who are being nourished. We have driven away the difficulty with <gloss>reason</gloss>, so that all pork is well-tempered and <gloss>useful</gloss>, and even those who suffer from "wretched diets" might struggle toward strength. We leave aside the excesses; for the juices mixed within are "bitter" <gloss>humors</gloss> to the taste, <gloss>and</gloss> many say it is very "good-juiced" when nature casts it toward health. So it is for the sick: for those with moist bellies, pork is well-suited for food; but it does not cause "blockage" <gloss>blockage</gloss> in such a person, which would otherwise create a residue or a gory juice. If one were to judge by the "tunics" <note>layers of tissue</note>, it nourishes and produces good humors, especially if the pigs are fed on **dried figs**. Among those who have spoken, some flee from us, while others prefer these, walking upon a "molded path." It is beneficial for the foundations of the feet, for all its parts are "marrow-like." For the parts of the "over-fleshed" pig, when taken, <gloss>and</gloss> are beneficial against the bites of venomous creatures. If you compare this even <gloss>in</gloss> **lard** <gloss>fat</gloss>, it will seem to be "dissolving" <note>original: "diaphoratikos"; having the power to disperse or sweat out humors</note>. For the spirits <gloss>and</gloss> within it wash the hollows of the body. By the parts of the foot, it is naturally disposed to "rub away" and produce good humors. It is the same with its beneficial ability to regulate the cold. Its bile, when the <gloss>animal</gloss> is hot, acts as a medicine and heals hemorrhoids. For all those with spleen issues, it is a primary remedy. <gloss>and</gloss> It is said to be able to benefit the "foot-pain" <note>gout</note> at its foundation. And you should listen more to these same things regarding the legs. The product is visible to those who call it "fatty" and "refined." It is superior for those who are "residue-heavy," provided they have good humors...

<summary>Psellos discusses the medicinal properties of 'phoukas' (a grain drink) and pork, noting that pork is uniquely compatible with human flesh and useful for treating gout and hemorrhoids.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s dietary treatise. Having previously discussed lentils and fermented drinks, he now moves into the nutritional properties of meat (specifically pork), fresh vegetables, and the various qualities of bread based on its preparation and baking.</meta>

<column-break/>

For just as the flesh of the old sow <note>original: "choiros"; referring here to pork</note> is inherently rich in juices <gloss>good-humored</gloss>, yet it yields no juice through its preparation or boiling, but rather preserves a moderate amount, it must certainly be sustained in the stomach during the **first alteration** <term>first alteration (prote alloiosis): the initial stage of digestion where food is transformed into chyme in the stomach</term>. For if this flesh—even if it is a fallen carcass—is poured around those who have already digested or those with **bad humors** <term>bad humors (kakochymia): an imbalance of the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</term>, it is seen to draw everything away, being hotter and moister as a generative substance. This is so the speed of the scorching might suffice without causing harm; it has a special quality in such a preparation, like a well-sprinkled portion to offer comfort, just as it is. For where it has been mentioned, to those of that division, it is not only able to "dance" upon the taste, but also by the drinking of grains and edibles, it makes something mild and most sweet; for we eat everything more sweetly when in a state of complete rest.

<margin>fresh vegetables</margin>
Regarding **fresh vegetables** <note>original: "artiolachana"; likely referring to young, seasonal greens or early-spring herbs</note>, they are not those whose first sprout is above the earth, but the second, which is made to warm by the earth. These have a beneficial temperament <gloss>mixture</gloss> for those who have heat, and for those with external bilious conditions. Thus, they soften everything nearby, containing everything within. Whether one has reached an advanced age, immediately taking the moist portion in the middle, they should not be hidden but rather trimmed away; even if in the decline of life they have been diluted by a mixture or harm, for there is still **digestion** <note>original: "pepsis"</note>. And for the rest of it, or even for its excellence, for it is salty-moist and contains a well-juiced quality like a moist sponge. Those who have a temperament in the daytime, or even that which is hot, would rather honor the preparation alone, because of its order, since the interval also causes harm. For some, hot water, which they take before drinking, is like an exercise for those having a cold [disposition] and going to the **baths** <note>original: "loutra"</note>; for through the density caused by the cooling, they further prevent the blood from dispersing through the whole body; and when heated in the **oven** <note>original: "klibano"</note>, it is as if many have been trained through experience and use in those who have fallen into illnesses.

<column-break/>

...and **poor health** <term>poor health (cachexia): a "bad habit" of the body, usually a chronic state of malnutrition or wasting</term>, and they are not only harmed by these things, but also by those that cause greater harm from above. For as many as pour out no moisture through the rubbing of the breads, it makes a difference regarding the preparations—some breads from the **bakers** <note>original: "artopois"</note>, since some are over-boiled, while those made by hand are better breads. Those made of wheat grain <gloss>of wheat</gloss> contain more of the whole, and are less likely to be crushed by the tooth, though they are praised for not being easily broken of their own accord. One must not be satisfied with the [immediate] use, but must divide or break it, only by the grains or those still called such, because they are of a lower quality. For as many as pass through, they do so because of the **cleansing property** <note>original: "ryptiken"; a detergent or purging quality</note> which is said to empty the residues in the **rectum** <note>original: "edra"; literally 'the seat'</note>. But the pure **barley breads** <note>original: "krithinoi"</note> are slower regarding the purest kinds and in the first stages, which brings a great quantity, but rather of grain. As far as purity goes, so much does the moisture of the blood cut through, bringing another of the purgatives. And concerning the pure ones, they are in the middle of such a range. For there is that which is carried about by the coarse; but the best are those that are **soft-leavened** <note>original: "malakomezymon"</note> or soft under pressure, and not only from this, being proportional to the fire from the oven. If they are hit by the first direct heat, or if the heat from the surface is sufficient, it happens to scorch them, which is a miserable thing for our breads, as the inside remains **unprocessed** <note>original: "akatergaston"; raw or undigested</note>. For when over-baked, the outside is dry, but that which has less than the proportionate fire also leaves the bread unprocessed; rather, so that it leaves all the juice, all the bread should be baked evenly throughout by a proportionate fire for a longer time. And for those who bake such a preparation, it is held only by its order, because the distance of the oven is nevertheless not strong in itself; yet we prefer those that are less under-baked, lest the rest be even more impure.
