### Explanations on certain orations by Symeon the Master and Greek Philosopher

<warning>Significant bleed-through and some faded marginal text.</warning>

<column-break/>

...for in the same way, death descends upon those who live by the oven <note>original: "klibanitais"; referring to those who eat bread baked in a *klibanos* (a covered earthen pot), often a symbol of the common or laboring class</note> and they are left behind. For the wealthy do not provide warmth, nor do they consume seeds, nor do they bear the witness of smoke. Unless they send out cold bodies—dead ones—and having ceased from the spirit, death then carries away an equal reputation, quickly infecting me with the present corruption; and having me as a starting point for my life, until a new and causal fashioner of little glory, which prays to follow yesterday. For this reason, I say to you, **rich poor man**, that your medicines and charms shall be your blessed food.

<margin>these halls</margin>
These very halls of mutual walls are inhabited by the possession of a marble statue. Someone is better than the august hens <note>likely a metaphorical or idiomatic reference to pampered domesticity</note>, but your judgment has charmed me. And wealth is the honor and fruit of piety. For he who has witnessed to it says it is better. For indeed, we see the honor of the work of "idol-madness" <note>original: "idomanias"; likely a portmanteau or error for "eidolomanias," referring to an obsession with images or material vanity</note>, it is called and prayed for only to be warmed. But as for fire, it would not cease until it moderately reaches the whole limit, while that eye brings laziness to the poor. Since it cried out from the roughness in the throat, he said, "O Master, it is not impossible." He who looks toward the "rough-sparkling" <note>original: "trachymarmarygen"</note> in the body; this is rather the glory in the warmth for the poor, which the ladies of the springs have made easy. He placed it among the mourners and it is hidden. And labor will make the pouring thick, where perhaps there is a playful thing for the humble. But he left behind the "body-massing" <note>original: "somatomazian"; referring to the building up of physical bulk</note>; perhaps upon the heat. And it is dissolved by the **bad humors** <term>bad humors (kakochymia): a medical state where the bodily fluids (humors) are out of balance or "spoiled"</term> of enemies. For life is dissolved by honor, and however many things the body acquires, perhaps a hand has burned you with a worse color, or it has yoked itself to her or to the feet. The acquired streams are taken away from her; and the "raw-flesh" <note>original: "omosarx"</note> is stripped, between the souls and the spirits and those remaining from the encompassing labors, it shall be hired. +

---

**A**lthough, O man, from the foundation of the world, the race is divided—those who attend and those who live. To them, they truly appear to be both wealthy and rich, but to those who have toiled, they are poor in their flutes <note>perhaps a metaphor for empty sound or vanity</note>. To some, death is like a wing of spirit <note>original: "thymos"; can mean anger or vital spirit</note>, and it is not easily borne in the winters. For the race of age is at its limit. **Pilots** with experience are found like a wing upon the sea, and because of this, they are found instead for Christ and silver. As they shall abound in treasures, the strength of their shoulders is their death, the hater of himself. The line of the goal is cut at the end of labor. For the path is like a root of teaching for all, manifold and most delightful, as the preparations of the seasons run about from memory.

And I pray for those who have been darkened and decorated, living entirely, to mix the bitter race of life with a sample of pure virtues; and let not the grace of the humble be in flux. And he shall sacrifice for us. Consider, however, the darkening of wealth for me out of fear. And likewise with the best joy, which is a treasure. For if greed did not prevail, he will lose his wealth; and he must cast it toward the harbor. From that part, let us take away what daily departed into death on that day through it. Those who received wages departed with the treasure, but the other in like manner. For they came into this world **naked** <note>A reference to Job 1:21: "Naked I came from my mother’s womb, and naked I will depart."</note> as if from the womb of their treasures, and again they are naked in the world, lest any labor of creation should appear to be in vain. But nevertheless, the earth knows from our goals this book, so that we may know that it is equal, and nothing is hidden from us after death. For indeed, the currents seek after that which you remember as the ease of life, but if you are divided in your...

### Michael Psellos: Philosophical Works
## On Knowledge, Education, and Action

...from such barbarians. It is as if someone holds thorns, and unless he crushes them, he will not place himself in any harm. Again, he spoke falsely, as if someone seeks to learn the word; since he loves his desire, he tortures his soul, either from his own ignorance or from a **Saracen word** <note>original: "sarakeniken lexin"; refers to Arabic vocabulary, which Psellos likely encountered through trade or diplomatic relations</note>. I have given proof to us as he did, for we placed the threat of the coming fate. But if we were as unconcerned as he, we would have to cast off the hatred of the witnesses. I think I have learned the word; the tongue hates what is, and his speech was mistaken. He failed his friend. It is said, "What kind of failure?" Or rather, the more dog-like <note>original: "kynteros"; an Homeric term meaning shameless or more cynical</note> one rejoices in the guidance of the cone. Thus one must acquire learning <note>original: "mathema"</note>, so that one may know accurately the portion of sin; so that through his <term>knowledge (gnosis)<gloss>spiritual or intellectual insight</gloss></term> he may cease from wickedness and join himself to what is right. For indeed, he must not let the matter pass by.

But if the arts are to abound, there is greater hope. It was said that at the same time he speaks of the affairs of his life; through these affairs, <term>education (paideia)<gloss>the traditional system of Greek learning and character formation</gloss></term> is both a cause and a purifier. Just as a well-foreseen man trims the lamp, or as a son sees to his education, knowledge saves him. And he who knows but does not act according to his own knowledge, shall be held at a loss; his honor shall flow away from him. For a **thief** <note>original: "kleptes"; a common New Testament metaphor for sudden spiritual reckoning</note> entered his house in the night: if he had woken up, he would have said to himself, "If I catch this man, I will make him rejoice." But having delayed in his bedchamber, he fell asleep. The thief, having gathered everything, fled. Was it not his sleep that condemned him? So too shall knowledge enter his sleep through a lack of action; and that knowledge shall be buried, a grave of his own benefit... the fruit of cooperation.

-> The hope which <-

<column-break/>

He who established knowledge but does not reap its benefit—it is in vain for him, like a friend of heat itself. And our experience is as one who runs the inner road toward fear; having traveled it, he will either be shaped by danger or be a wonder to all for his folly. And so, he who follows his own will is likened to his own folly, rather than being guided by knowledge. For just as a person who knows the harmful from the harmless in colors, yet does not restrain himself from desire and greed, eats what is harmful; he shall be reviled by those who see him. In the same way, the world—knowing both good and evil, yet abandoning the good to do the evil—shall be reviled. It is like a man who is honored but becomes like a blind man; falling into a pit, they both perish. Will he not be reviled even more as a slave? Or again, he who does not see unless it is for the benefit of another? 

He is likened to a **spring** <note>original: "pege"</note> which benefits others but not itself.
<margin>not</margin>
And for the one who seeks knowledge in this way: first, let him comfort himself. If he does so, he may teach others. But the lover of the world <note>original: "philokosmos"</note> seeks to acquire three things through it: knowledge, wealth, and discernment. It was said that from these things, he who does not know his own benefit labors in vain. And I say and have said: whoever reads this book and is deemed worthy of its teaching, yet does not act according to his knowledge, is called **blind**. He is likened to the earth which, receiving the rain... it does not benefit itself, and its own love is buried. He shall be placed at a loss, or suffer a marvel concerning himself... he carries away the pleasure of <term>action (praxis)<gloss>the practical application of virtues or knowledge</gloss></term> as a storehouse.

<summary>This page discusses the moral obligation of knowledge, arguing that education and intellectual insight (gnosis) are worthless—and even condemnatory—if not translated into ethical action (praxis). Psellos employs the parables of the negligent homeowner and the dry spring to illustrate the dangers of spiritual and intellectual stagnation.</summary>

### Michael Psellos: Philosophical Works
## The Parable of the Thief and the Master of the House

And you were swallowed up and it was scattered. He is stripped, it seems, of his little house; having fallen down naked, he fell asleep. Then a mocker <note>original: "myktes"; a term for a sneering or cynical person</note>, a certain thief, entered unto him. Having learned of the situation, the thief said to himself: "Every man of **God** <note>original: "thou," a standard abbreviation for theou</note> falls asleep when he arrives. And I shall take the grain that is in the jar and depart." Having stripped off the man's tunic, he spread it over the heap of grain. 

But when the **master of the house** <term>oikokyrios<gloss>the head of a household or property owner</gloss></term> woke up and chased him, dealing him blows, he took back his own tunic. And we, likewise, leave behind the grain of the earth, so that we may take up the things of **God**. Thus it is necessary in the present not to place too much hope in worldly things, lest we find ourselves in an evil hour. Do not pursue an <unclear>incomprehensible</unclear> matter. 

And again, through study and diligence, consider the story <note>original: "mython"; refers here to the moral parable of the thief</note>, and it will cause you to cease your wandering; for one finds waters there. For perhaps we might quickly find honorable things above the unavoidable fall. Do not follow the thief; for many of our people find themselves in ruin because of such a fall. 

The current is a snatching away for all. One must test the constant flow of the world <note>literally "the flow of the earth"</note> and look toward the eternal. Similarly, he who would run away from all evils—as if from a <unclear>storm</unclear>—should not let himself be settled among those who are like him. And though there are sacrifices made for the earth and those who live in virginity, let no one add to them out of a mere hope of doing so. 

But for our part, let us look to the future; you will see the <unclear>divinity</unclear> passing by you. Do not be negligent in this matter. For we know not how we are to live, nor what time one has in his **age** <term>aion<gloss>the current temporal world or lifetime, often contrasted with the eternal life to come</gloss></term> or in the one to come. There is a certain limit to temporary things, and they shall be made desolate. But what we know concerning this **age**, he who acquires it shall be punished. What **God** gives to the world is the lamb <note>original: "arion," likely a corruption of "arnion," the lamb</note>, so that we may acquire freedom from trials. Guard yourself against acquiring things for yourself again, for by that very act, everything is taken away...

<summary>Psellos employs a parable about a thief stealing grain to illustrate the danger of spiritual slumber and the superiority of eternal goods over earthly ones. He warns the reader to remain vigilant and prioritize the "tunic" of the soul over the "grain" of the world.</summary>

# Philosophical Works
## On the History and Translation of the Book of Wisdom

And not even how, together with those who have <gloss>departed</gloss> from us, one might ever be chosen as strong, <gloss>healthy</gloss>, high-spirited, or <gloss>good</gloss>. And this man must again not pursue meddling in affairs, nor those that rush and then cease, especially without purpose. For if someone does not tell us this, we shall be known truly. But we, seeing the Persians taking care of the books, [translated] this from the Persian language into the Arabic. # Philosophical Works
## On the Soul and the Vanity of the World

For the sake of my soul, I struggle against **temporary things**, standing in their midst and saying: "Soul, give yourself over to this life and let us enjoy its pleasures," even though when life flees from these matters, it is like grass <note>original: "chortos"; a common biblical metaphor for the fleeting nature of human life (cf. Isaiah 40:6-8)</note> that withers. But you shall receive affliction after these things depart, and finally, eternal punishment. 

**Soul:** "Do you not remember the eternal things, those beautiful things I inquire about?" I speak to the foolish throughout this entire life; from my own corruption, when the soul withdraws into itself, or chooses the needs of all. For indeed, the foolish pursue madness and contention. I love temporary things—yet I must seek the **eternal**; those things that are both complete and secure. You cannot hate the things that exist in the "flow" <note>original: "rhymatōn"; likely referring to the Heraclitean "panta rhei" or the ever-changing flux of material existence</note>. Soul, do not be led astray by my *philosophers*. 

Do not let eternal things be for this purpose, where I am a mourner. For either there is a shared life, or one should not rejoice much, but rather grieve; for already the separation is like a stranger. And life stands like a snail <note>original: "kochlian"; a metaphor for being withdrawn or perhaps the spiral-like traps of worldly life</note>, which one must distinguish from the drink of the "flow." When it is crushed, it is judged. Soul, do not force yourself to gather wealth for the sake of serving your kinsmen or brothers; perhaps regarding the spirit, things are preserved for themselves and your own. Soul, let not the sight of wealth deceive you; seek what is truly present. For even if you have acquired it, I dishonor it, except when it is separated from the soul—just like **hair**. When it is on our head, we care for it; but when it is separated from it, we are not pained. Soul, if you do not wish for the things of the senses... and since many have a way out, and more of the foolish live in folly, let us not think of friendship. Instead, let us likewise lament, because in the midst of necessity, we have taken this out of the soul itself; because of what it thought.

<column-break/>

To flow away from its part <gloss>or life-giving</gloss> for eternal souls. And since so many fools, because of my words, hate [me] more—for they are like those in want of sins. It is like a wave of courage, and they seem to be separated from the world and pleasures, which for us <gloss>you</gloss> are bitter and they are corrupted by them. 

And he said this: that blood has more reward than desires. Soul, do not be attached to **temporary things**, which have distanced you from the **eternal**; do not be surrounded by the distant, rather than the great things. Do not trade the great for something small, as if wearing a sleeve or a Damascene glove <note>original: "damaskēsai"; possibly referring to fine fabric or craftsmanship from Damascus, representing luxury items</note>. They are foreign to it and its actions; through seeking, a great world. For life is madness. And indeed, fleeing from actions for a small honor, they were wise. The soul in the midst of the "flow," having escaped from the hand. 

How can another be persuaded by these words? I shall answer them. And I began not to be left behind by human things; we seek the **eternal reward**. For all these who live, or who are forced to change their mortal temporary glory and honor? Leading the desire, I love and pursue with all sensation, perhaps lying upon those who only hate and are not worthy of hesitation. And to look upon it, having the power of the present moment. Thrice-blessed, I give the soul itself as a sun-like strength to men, and I have learned to despise bitterness. May I find the portion of eternal things that rescues me. For after suffering, we still seek bitter possessions. And having grasped this, I shall turn away from the religious practice of the sense-organs; for religion <note>original: "thrēskeia"</note> on an empty lyre... for our religion <term>thrēskeia<note>In this context, Psellos likely refers to the external forms of worship or piety, distinguishing between superficial ritual and the internal life of the soul.</note></term> is taught to us; and indeed, religion—

<summary>This page contains a moralizing dialogue where the author addresses his Soul, warning it against the "flow" of temporary worldly pleasures and wealth. He uses metaphors like grass and hair to illustrate the fleeting and external nature of material life compared to eternal rewards.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general philosophical exhortations to the Soul regarding vanity into a specific narrative illustration. # Philosophical Works
## On the Soul and the Vanity of the World

Let us consider a <gloss>childish</gloss> scene. And such a thing happened with me: if I speak, no one listens to foolish things, because no longer do they say we have the wealth of hearing; and everyone shall boast against us, if they have anything else more desirable than us. And as for the rest, shall we and you cease to hunt for this benefit which was saved from the turning away? It is said, then: still having commanded this turning away from them, we all hold ourselves as leaders. And even if they enter while stumbling, they remain; for they are children, and such is the nature of their inclination. It is not necessary to be offended by me; but you say, and all of us say as we depart, "Why do you spare my cheerfulness regarding the garment?" And as we were departing from the windows of the house, we who wished to enter; and children said to us—for they were under the windows—seeing the **moonlight** <note>original: "selēnoselon"; a charming, perhaps colloquial term for a moonbeam or the glow of the moon</note>, and running upon the road, they thought they had caught the moon as they entered the house. So why do you find fault with me? For the mercy toward me—for this child—shows itself to those within the house. We took ourselves, though not willing, and they were trying to grasp the **rays of the sun** <note>original: "aktinas tou hēliou"; this image is a classic Patristic metaphor for the soul trying to grasp the divine essence through its "energies" or reflections</note>, because they said again that while the children heard us as we left, the grace that is possessed rejoices with me more than that great thing which has been accomplished from this house—deeds which have found us better and not similar. And let us proceed with haste and be without fear. Thinking that they had lulled the house to sleep, to lighten the dawn for them, and more than the rest... again toward the windows themselves, having been warmed, he enters and upon the moon... And sitting up from the <unclear>couch</unclear>, he fell headlong; because the spleen...

<summary>Psellos recounts a parable, adapted from Gregory of Nyssa, where children attempt to "catch" moonbeams and sunbeams coming through windows. The story serves as an allegory for the soul's futile attempt to grasp material shadows instead of eternal truths.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s exploration of the soul’s detachment from the material world. The previous page discussed the "Tree" and the "Well" as spiritual symbols; here, he contrasts the "blessed mother" (likely a metaphor for the Soul or Wisdom) with the physical senses, using the imagery of gold, pearls, and a ship at sea to describe the soul’s ascent and its struggle against worldly fear.</meta>

# Philosophical Works
## On the Soul and the Vanity of the World

<margin>
<unclear>Faded text</unclear>
</margin>

Beyond the perception of the blessed mother <note>The "blessed mother" likely refers to the Soul in its generative capacity or perhaps Divine Wisdom.</note>—that she does not possess what is her own—the one who is now attached to the world does not even perceive the future that truly belongs to him. And for this reason, in that place, the one who does not bear witness when called to testimony might receive the hidden thing he requested. Thus, in that state, their alienation is a cause for punishment; and because of this, they perceive nothing of the enjoyment of participating in those punishments, being toward the non-contemplation <term>theoria</term> <note>original: "theōrian"; the meditative or visionary state of the soul seeking God</note> of the Speaker. But that one who listens to them, seeing how gold and silver are nothing more than a name to him according to earthly habit, perceives nothing more than that. And the whole of it was left by him empty of pearls. And having learned the things of the world, I again take pleasure in that place; and here, like a skin-bag <note>original: "askos"; a reference to a container for wind or liquid, perhaps alluding to the body or the winds of the Odyssey</note> stretched out toward the reckoning, in the enjoyment of a snatching away <term>rapture</term> <note>original: "apharpagēs"; the sudden lifting of the soul to a higher state of consciousness</note>, just as in a ship—this ship of his—he was signaled as seeing nothing superfluous to those with him. Various and many are the ways this snatching away thickens; for in the redness of paradise, the spirit <term>pneuma</term> <note>original: "pneuma"; the divine breath or the vital force</note> is active, and the mechanical sound <note>A curious reference to the "mechanics" or ordered operation of the heavens or divine providence.</note> in that place again points toward an unutterable cause, just as a flight from the old world toward the things of the divinity within him. And again, he is another in contemplation, fleeing from anger—though it does not exist there. But this truly most perfect thing exists in the world: that which stood together and, like the things that are more truly the cause, I remain and bear witness by a divine work. For having learned the order and the freedom from fear, I am left alone by the sea, having willed to be something from it. Again, there is the fear of mortal expectations—to endure—because of the things concerning him, and not being directly more than the harms and the cowardice with someone, or even the things after many movements. He follows those things which they themselves also release toward the starting point of his glory. And indeed, of human sins, even if he does not overcome all, he does not do so through grief again—

<column-break/>

—I say again, the superior path of the martyrs. Whence even upon the sea, he was placed upon that superior part of the vessel, so as to enter and receive him. Just as in that place, as far as it was left empty, they feared the despair; being afraid of that snatching away and the creation, he did not give his impulses toward it. Moreover, even the harms and the same gains and many better things in between, the yoke-beams <note>A technical term for the cross-beams of a ship, here used metaphorically for balance or stability.</note> not having this divine spirit, and the things happening to them, are faith fitted together through possession. Therefore, the structure of the resurrection <term>anastaseōs</term> <note>original: "anastaseōs"; the rising again of the soul or the body</note> was reasonably protected again in the present world; just as the evil lie places as much honor upon him as it can. It is as if one names that man the "lamp of the world," so that they saw the creation belonging to the Creator Himself, or the cargo of his ship. And as much as in those always present, for more than twenty days, it is just like fire. For before every portion of creation, there are bitter things in a circle around that same thing again; and the righteous things for this blessed man, so that cowardice might never come upon him through the pains of living things. For in general, the things that are to be are one's own most of all. And they saw the things within him as being most of all the things to be considered of God; nothing divine was with him at first, except for a wolf <note>original: "lykē"; possibly a metaphor for greed or a predatory spiritual force, or a scribal error for "lypē" (grief).</note> and to enjoy the beginning alone again. It is like the one who sees strength under the enemy, yet having woken from sleep, he again sees his beginnings; as if some invisible judgment had rested the past punishment, not toward the path of journeying, but toward the prevention of what follows, having been sufficient unto itself. The evil one is no longer a greater battle of judgment, even from its path. For having learned the penalty in a practical way through the ascetic practice <term>askēsei</term> <note>original: "askēsei"; the disciplined training of the soul and body for spiritual progress</note> within him, and all hearing, and because of this, to live in memory of the order, and having placed that blessed man as the starting point.

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the soul's detachment from the material world, transitioning from the general philosophical argument of the previous page into the specific, famous allegorical "Parable of the Man in the Well" from the story of Barlaam and Josaphat. # Philosophical Works
## On the Soul and the Vanity of the World

To him: "I have fallen into a depth of perplexity" <note>original: "bython aporias"; a common philosophical idiom for an existential or intellectual crisis</note>; you were learning from us how he explained it—how he who agreed with me regarding my affairs as they stand, yet again, the stay of this life was not lengthened. Thus, by a divine judgment, he who judges is both just and a savior. By condemning their possessions again—whether of the body or of ascetic practice—for he does not do his own digging, nor does he revel in the things he conceded, which those before us manage. Or rather, that we might exchange our soul and be satiated by this life; for having already spoken, as if already tasting a certain bitterness, those who have led us into evil: every kind of luxury is skin-deep. 

We depart from the world by small degrees, and by small degrees it becomes more bitter; through these things, they grieve in their fittingness <gloss>or</gloss> the foolish passions of others through their perception. For they fail in their foolishness. But for us who have come immediately, or for the very bitter things of the eternal, to be entangled; it is a small thing, and like a parable <note>The author begins to pivot toward the allegorical story of the man in the well.</note> <unclear>from which</unclear> how the eternal things perish <gloss>or the</gloss> and how this life is full of pain, full of turmoil, and full of waves. Having been cast into it like movements, so also at the same time a budding growth, from its beginning until its end. For indeed, as he said of the man, speaking in parables: our eternity is for those not yet nourished by a woman; there being both beast-like and manic passions. 

Just as instead of the spiritual, there is a clear sky of darkness, the days having separated us from God; let me not be tempted. For he who is liberating the body persuades one to endure the things of the many. It was necessary for something to be done by them; the virtues and those things from the path toward God are good. The final hope is to kick away the things in the world. Again, in our teaching, many of our ways are a spectacle—both means and medicines <term>pharmaka</term> <note>original: "pharmaka"; used here in the sense of spiritual remedies or antidotes to worldly vice</note> for all our immoderate age, having separated us from, or rather by, insatiable wickedness.

<column-break/>

In the removal of dangers, let us keep these things within ourselves: a treasure-house of enemies—the blood, the phlegm, <gloss>the humor</gloss> and the bile <note>These are the Four Humors of ancient medicine: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile. Psellos views them here as internal enemies that destabilize the soul.</note>; the poisons, the arrows that come forth from the lion; the scorching heat, the cold, the atmosphere; the great variety of the waters. Torments, punishments, and once a fearful cause; through which we are provoked. To those who have been frightened, <gloss>either</gloss> to be persuaded or to be baptized; the departure from the world <gloss>or</gloss> the stings <gloss>or</gloss> the high things <gloss>or</gloss> the brothers. 

Above all, let us meditate on this life. Since I do not think one should be occupied with the cares of the world, nor with this life, or because their end is uncertain. One must take up those things which are corruptible; the heavens are called, the flesh is divided, in such things as chance might strip away. <gloss>Or</gloss> all the virtue of our harmful parts has been acquired; this life is incorruptible, the kind which is desired by the good. And for me, they are of the ethers. From the affairs of life, which seem to some to be distant, just as through our own things, seeing the evil ones nevertheless, and not being strong, yet there is a naming of the pleasure of the senses; <gloss>or</gloss> full of wrath. 

The pit is like no other, which he did not plant out of wrath in the body, but the hollows, or those things of the lips... he was suspended <note>The parable begins: a man fleeing a beast falls into a pit.</note> likewise holding onto branches, through which he shows mercy with his hands, or where he placed his feet. It was a place of no confidence, nor in myself; all nature completes the heads of the ancient whole. Looking down from below, he saw a fearful dragon <note>original: "drakonta"; representing Hades or the Grave in the parable</note> having its mouth open... he saw two mice, a white one and a black one <note>The white and black mice represent Day and Night, which continuously gnaw at the roots of human life.</note>, eating the roots of the...

<summary>This page discusses the soul's struggle against the material world and physical ailments (the Four Humors), culminating in the start of the famous 'Man in the Well' parable. The allegory illustrates the precariousness of human life, suspended between worldly pleasure and inevitable death.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the famous allegorical parable of the "Man in the Well," a story used by Byzantine authors to illustrate the precariousness of human life and the distractions of the material world. It then transitions directly into the title and opening prologue of "Stephanites and Ichnelates," the Greek translation of the Arabic fables of "Kalila and Dimna," traditionally attributed in the Byzantine context to the physician Simeon Seth at the request of Emperor Alexios I Komnenos.</meta>

## [Left Page]

<margin>
<unclear>...you are...</unclear>
<unclear>...the first...</unclear>
</margin>

He clings to the branch with all his might; meanwhile, others go about their business, even if they must eventually run back to their own place. As for the sprouts—whether they represent the care of bodies—they do not act the same for everyone. If one considers even their creation, it is then that the sense of self-governance goes astray, and the freedom of these same things is not hidden. Under the branch itself, one is not immediately handed over to the clutches <note>original: "tarsōn"; literally "flat of the feet" or "clutches," referring to the trap below</note> because this was stored within the small twig. But indeed, all these branches, not having made a judgment of the ruling mind through their preoccupation, enjoy its sweetness. Similarly, this life is like a pit <note>original: "lakkon"; the pit in the parable represents the world</note> for them, full of fullness and evil wickedness. Therefore, through these things, the way of living is set free; for he did not abandon all that is unavoidable—alas for us in this formless age! There is a danger of a sudden melting away, from a single pit and bushes toward nature, and the fleshly things of it. In the same way, they live for the prunings. For those who enjoy the one sweet honey, the days and the nights—one black and one bright <note>The black and white mice in the parable represent Night and Day gnawing at the thread of life.</note>—are sensitive to the destroyer who has uprooted us from our original parent, and the belly of the beasts <note>original: "gastēr thēratōn"; referring to the dragon waiting at the bottom of the pit</note>. Whenever one passes by the power of the honey—the small sweetnesses of this life—we are provoked by the injustice of the beginning for that same cause. Having understood all these things, I sat down and examined my own way of life, as one who has greatly desired the truth for my own salvation. For in these things, I have the opportunity to write a small book. This book was written, regarding:

->**Ichnelates**<-

## [Right Page]

<margin>
This is the beginning of the book which is called Stephanites and Ichnelates... <note>original Latin: "Hoc est principium libri qui dicitur Stephanites et Ichnelates..."</note>
<unclear>Additional Latin and Greek notes in upper margin</unclear>
</margin>

->§§§§§§§§§§§§§§§§§§§§§<-

**This has been translated from the language of the Arabs.** <note>original: "Ek tēs tōn arrabōn dialektou"; a reference to the work's source in the Arabic version by Ibn al-Muqaffa.</note> It is perhaps useful for all who are wise, but especially for the righteous who wish to guide their own souls. We say something in the Saracenic <note>Saracenic: a term used by Byzantines for Arabic/Islamic culture.</note> tongue; it is said that this was sent between two kings, containing the precious words of a philosopher. I wish to show you how a deceitful and wicked man, getting in the middle, transforms love into enmity, even when setting a high value on all friendship.

The narrator took up the word and said: There was a certain merchant who was very wealthy, and as he reached the end of his life, he had acquired much. He had sons who were prone to idleness <note>original: "mochthērian"; referring to a lack of industry or moral effort</note> and would not practice any trade. He exhorted them with these words, saying: "O my dearest children! He who is brought up in this life needs three things: a sufficient fortune, a good reputation among men, and, by fortune's grace, the pursuit of the good things that come hereafter for the righteous. These three things, most honorable ones, do not come about except through four means. That is: to acquire wealth from a just and reasonable source; to manage and conduct oneself well regarding its possession; then to share what has been acquired with those in need—which is profitable for the life after the remainder of this one; and furthermore, to avoid the pitfalls of calamity as much as is within one's power. If anyone neglects one of these four, he will not find peace. For if he does not find wealth, he will not be able to—"

<meta>This page continues the introductory moral philosophy of the text, transitioning from the allegory of the "Man in the Well" to the specific virtues required for managing life and wealth. It concludes with the opening of the first book of the fables, introducing the characters of the Lion King and the two jackals, Stephanites and Ichnelates.</meta>

Regarding life, if at the same time one is deprived of some of the honors of virtue—for example, if only wealth remains—one must manage their life wisely and with zeal. For such things soon lie within a measured limit <note>original: "arithmois"; referring to a finite or calculated amount</note>. If one makes only the smallest expenditures, bit by bit, and adds nothing further to them, eventually the entire wealth will be consumed just like blood. For blood, if it is shed bit by bit and not replenished, is eventually spent. 

Even if wealth is acquired and divided among many contributions, one should still share it with others. One might send out what they have in abundance, and perhaps the cause of total loss is like when water pipes <note>original: "sōlēnes"; a metaphor for the circulation of resources</note> burst because the water finds no outlet or path for its flow. 

Frequently, however, the will of the spirit acts with purpose through these intermediaries. Its first intention was to send this forward. In some way, by setting aside the fable <note>original: "mythos"; here referring to the allegorical nature of the stories to follow</note> because of its simple style, let us not seem to reveal ourselves plainly upon the road, but rather let the common things coincide. For if the stepmother <note>A common figure in Byzantine rhetoric used to represent a harsh or deceptive influence</note> who stands over all signs can mold the clay, then the fable also works alongside the truth, through suspicion, and is poured out. 

Advancing and prevailing, he came upon a trembling disk of cedar and a source of mud and springs where he remained, dwelling <unclear>lovingly</unclear> with the Muses. And though it was burdensome, he was left behind, from which point the masters held a nakedness of innovation and a great bellowing because of what he held near him.

<column-break/>

<page-num>59</page-num>

...from the neighborhood of the lions, and I am raised together with this animal life. There were lions, bears, wolves, leopard-wolves <note>original: "lykopantheres"; likely referring to lynxes or a mythical hybrid</note>, foxes, and other species. The Lion was a champion in body, yet he suffered in part from diseases. Having heard of his father’s schemes—sounding of such things—he became very afraid, though he did not wish to reveal his cowardice to his subordinates. 

I fear for my own part that which is most wicked. Those who were subordinates were present, but they did not quickly make a choice. There was one called "The Tracker" <note>original: "Ichnēlatēs"; one of the two main jackal characters, known for his cunning and ambition</note>. Both he and his companion possessed a variety of character, except that The Tracker was wicked in his soul and desired more. 

When his father was caught, he did not even appear to fly or show himself. Why is it that the Lion remains unmoved while being watched? And I shall cease often to hide the seals <note>likely referring to official signs of authority or secrets</note>, and having taken it up, he said: "Who is at home and upright in all these unseemly questions?" For nothing would stand before us other than a gate toward the strength of our King; let him only be guarded and let us provide nourishment. But those who think they can speak and survey these matters will not stop. Such a man, because he is not used to it, forces himself toward good things. He will experience the same thing as the one who tries to say what a carpenter <note>A reference to the fable of the monkey who tried to imitate a carpenter and got his tail caught in a split log</note> should do while they are gathering wood. One must watch another so that the honor is not taken away by two people. The one who acts similarly to this, manfully honoring a man on a horse, kisses the ground only for necessity... sitting upon the wood...

# Philosophical Works
## Michael Psellos

...he attempted to do the same as that one. Therefore, the **donkey**, making use of the wooden saddle, was then weighed down and **fainted**. The carpenter, having understood the shape of things, remained with his own. But Ichnelates <note>The "Tracker," one of the two main jackal characters in this collection of fables</note> replied: "If only I had known what you proposed! But know this: not everyone who approaches **kings** attains worthy goods. Rather, there are times when one who desires glory does so to gladden his own friends and vex his enemies. For it is the way of lowly and earth-bound men to be content with whatever they happen upon. But since death is often found to be useful, he looks away from this. A virtuous man <note>original: "hymenios anēr"; likely referring to a man of integrity or 'hymned' virtue</note> must not stand with those who are in no way deceptive, but he comes from a man of worth and pursues their merits. Just as the **lion** <note>original: "leōn"</note> who is hunting a **hare** <note>original: "lagōou"</note>, when he sees a **camel** <note>original: "kamēlon"</note>, lets the hare go and hunts the camel instead. Or do you not know the dog <note>The OCR reads "kolōna," likely a corruption of "kyna" (dog)</note>, who wags his tail until he enjoys a morsel of **bread**? But the **great elephant** <note>original: "megas elephas"</note>, when food is brought to him, does not act like a flatterer; when they are filled with this, even the high-minded man—being great of hearing and a stream of giving—might let his son pass through a short time, but he calculates for the longest duration. And his son says: 'Why do we live in such misery, neither dying nor being of use to others?' If he falls into the deep, he seeks old age." And the member of the household <note>original: "phamitis"; likely "phamilias" or household servant</note> said: "If you do not speak, then consider how the measure remains from what is good, and when..."

<column-break/>

"...regarding present things, an honorable man must be content with his own possessions. Such is how we dwell, and we are not uneducated in the moderation that fits us." But **Ichnelates** said: "I believe that in every life and every station, the high-minded man always makes his recompense. But the base man always sinks down. For it is a **difficult thing to leap from below to the top**, but easy to descend from the heights. Perhaps it is better to go around the low path gently. One rises with difficulty, but is carried down easily. Therefore, I too, being pursued by those greater than me, and especially by one driven by his own fate, wish to go around the **virtue of the lion** and gain the benefit of his company. For I see him isolated, and though he alone wears the **purple** <note>A symbol of royalty and high status</note>, he is moved by his own nature. Perhaps I shall find the honor I deserve." And the **Elephant** <note>original: "elephas"; likely the interlocutor in this version of the dialogue</note> said: "And how will it be clear what the lion intends?" Ichnelates replied: "One does not simply know his reasoning; but a strong man reveals it, departing from vain opinions when remaining near. This is known from his **disposition** and his **outward form**." The Elephant said: "And how do you hope to find favor with the lion himself, having never served a king? Or having had no conversation with his child by reputation?" He answered: "A strong man is not hindered by such things, nor does he possess inexperience in like manner. He who is not such a man fails even in his own craft." And the Elephant said: "It is better to be carried away or to be taken by the one who is superior."

# Philosophical Works
## Michael Psellos

...concerning them. But he is more closely likened to the **vine** than to the rest; for the vine does not entwine itself with the greatest of trees, but with those nearest to it. It is proper, therefore, to become an intimate of the **lion**, even if one has been a stranger <note>original: "aptōkismos," likely a corruption of "apoikismos" (exile or separation)</note> to him. 

But **Ichnelates** <note>The "Tracker," the more ambitious of the two jackals in this fable</note> said: "Even what you say is true; but it is necessary for us to become more intimate, even if we were not so before. Let those who speak with heat fight amongst themselves; to such as these, one must extend a hand. For it is said that anyone who approaches the **royal gate**, having cast aside pride, subduing his anger, and maintaining reverence toward the subjects, very quickly becomes an intimate of the king." 

But **Stephanites** <note>The "Crowned," the more cautious and philosophical jackal</note> said: "I am indeed eager to know, once you have approached the lion, how you can find favor with him?" 

**Ichnelates** replied: "When I approach him and discern his mind and the flow of his thoughts—if I see his eyes desiring to attempt something beneficial, I will show him the way more clearly so that he may act upon it. I will prepare him further for the completion of the work. But if he handles anything disadvantageous, I will reveal to him the caution <note>original: "eulabeian," meaning discretion or religious-like care</note> required regarding such a beast. I will do this through a subtle covering that is useful to him. In this, I shall act as a counselor and a corrector. And I believe that in this way the lion will love me and prefer me over others. For a man of such power is able to invalidate the truth and to write a falsehood harmoniously, as if it were a song of spring; he has the power to forge the truth, shaping it into a perfection of honors and access."

<column-break/>

...not for how much. 

**Stephanites** said: "If you plan these things, you must watch yourself regarding the intimacy of the king. No mindless person dares to stand upon the peak of a rock, for he is not easily saved from it. For it is said concerning kings that dealing with them is like the taking of poison through experiments, or the ancient company of women. For the king is situated like a steep and impassable mountain, covered in all kinds of fruits and leafy herbs, but full of wild beasts and reptiles. One who uses it carelessly finds it a site of ruin and a slippery fall." 

**Ichnelates** said: "Truly, if everyone refused to dare any danger, they would never meet with good fortune. If we prioritize a life without risk, we remain deprived of all things. For it is said that these three things do not enter the minds of the small-souled: the **lion**, royal suspicion, and seafaring trade—along with the rapid clash with enemies. For these were set apart for high-mindedness <term>megalopsychia: the virtue of the "great-souled" man who is worthy of great things</term> and bravery. Royal courts and quiet stays are found in unexpected places, just as it is for the **elephant** to live in the desert and then suddenly to dwell in palaces." 

**Stephanites** said: "I am not acting wisely in speaking to you further about this; but go and do what you wish." 

Then **Ichnelates** went to the **lion** and did obeisance to him. The lion asked him <gloss>him</gloss>: "From where have you come after so much time?" 

**Ichnelates** said: "I do not cease approaching the royal gate, living in the hope of being deemed worthy of cooperation..." <note>The text here becomes abbreviated or fragmented: "dimos matou d’ou maiō"</note>

# Philosophical Works
## Michael Psellos

...to the king; knowing that the knowledge of affairs and the recovery from errors is a <unclear>manliness</unclear> <note>original: "androsyne," meaning bravery or a manly spirit of resolve</note> of <unclear>comprehension</unclear>; and just as many who often find themselves in moderate circumstances by low fortune are like the wood cast aside on the ground, whenever they are mocked with colors or masks <note>A metaphor for how talented people in low positions are often treated as mere props or scenery until their true worth is revealed</note>. When the **lion** heard such words, [he considered] if some conscientious man might be found among those under him. Such a man is often unknown until he speaks or associates with others, just as fire remains hidden under the ash until it emerges and produces an ethereal flame.

Thus, among us, **Ichnelates** <note>The "Tracker," the more ambitious of the two jackals in this fable</note> saw that the **lion** appeared most excellent toward the king. It is necessary to bestow the honor and dignity that is fitting upon the king’s flock through a natural selection of what is necessary. And just as the seeds of various plants lie hidden beneath the earth, and are not perceived until they sprout forth, so too is excellence <note>original: "kalokagathia," the classical Greek ideal of being "noble and good" in both character and action</note> under him, which is immediately revealed. 

The king must neither attach the ornament of the head to the feet, nor that of the feet to the head; nor should he intertwine hyacinth and precious pearls with lead—rather, the pearls are more precious to us than lead. For the ruler must distribute fitting honors to those under him: to the soldiers, their due; and to the high priest, the learned and diligent men. For it is not wealth or the legislative schemes for money that matter, but the bright expressions of wisdom. Since there are few who are generous toward others among those who have shared richly in...

<column-break/>

...a good leader, [it is necessary] not to despise the lowliness of his subordinates. For that which is small is not small when it brings forth something great. The ruler must also not bring into his circle those who have fallen despite being conspicuous <note>Likely referring to disgraced nobles who maintain only the appearance of status</note>, but those who are noteworthy and of sound understanding in their power. Nor should he be satisfied only with those who are present and depend only upon them; but he should also reach out to those further away who are adorned with understanding and education alone. For nothing is closer or more precious to those who see clearly. But when this occurs to me, the distant remedies and fellow counselors are sought; since they themselves often enter royal houses, approaching another because of good fortune; and the lover of gold, seeing the lowliness, calls for and receives them from the king as if he were being carried toward a high-minded person.

Hearing these words, the **lion** was greatly amazed and said to his council: "The ruler must not overlook diligent men even if they are of low fortune; but he should deign to accept each one and all, even those who seem displeasing."

When <term>Ichnelates</term> <note>The Tracker; one of the two jackals who serves as a court counselor in this collection of fables</note> saw the **lion's** favorable inclination toward him, he spoke with him in private, saying: "Is it because of this, O King, that they remained immobile for a long time, while the evil situation shifted?" But the **lion** took counsel to hide his own cowardice; but he happened to hear the lowing of the **bull** in the meantime. Becoming still more fearful, he said: "I fear lest the body of this animal, with its wild mind and <unclear>mighty beauty</unclear> <note>original: "kalodynamos," a compound perhaps meaning "beautifully powerful"</note>, is superior, but such things are not in the body alone; and if it is so, then..."

...having made a sound; but the **Tracker** <note>original: "ichnelates," the name of the jackal, meaning "one who follows tracks"</note> replied and said: "Do not fear, O King, these oversized voices; it is often like a hollow vessel; for it is said: that a certain hungry **fox** came upon a place; and by chance a **drum** was hanging in the woods, caught in a tree. And because it was moved by the winds, it made a great crashing sound against the branches. When the fox heard this, she was afraid to approach. But then, driven by trial and her appetite, she grew bold against it; and approaching, thinking to find a life full of fat and meat, she grabbed the drum. When she had struggled and torn it open, seeing only the hollow frame, she said: 'Alas! With how much great sound and loud voice did such a great thing promise a feast!'"

"Therefore, O most excellent King, we suffer being led astray by the voice of this animal. And if you wish, I will go to him and see what he is and return quickly."

And with these words, he calmed the **lion**. And he [the King] permitted him; but after he had departed, a great regret seized the lion regarding his absence. And reasoning within himself, he suffered, and many thoughts flowed toward the Tracker; he thought that the lion did not possess all the land, as one who for a long time had overlooked things, or like a greedy and insatiable man; or because there was no occasion for his own comfort, just as heat or the loss of wealth and glory breeds such things in men. # Philosophical Works
## Michael Psellos

The Crab said to him: "You seem to me to be quietly despondent."
And he replied: "And how should I not be despondent? Before the inhabitants of this lake, I was sufficiently and abundantly fed every day by its fish. But today I saw two fishermen waiting around this place and speaking to one another about how they might net all the fish therein." The Crab, having heard these things, went to the fish and reported to them all that he had learned from the <term>swan</term> <note>original: "kyknos." While most versions of this fable feature a heron or a crane, this Greek translation identifies the predatory bird as a swan.</note>. They approached the swan, saying: "Now, become our counselor; for he who saves a person is often a counselor even to his own enemy. No blame shall befall them for the benefit derived from the deed." The swan said: "No food is set before me here at all, but I must certainly move you from this place. There is another place, soft and reedy and watery." And they said: "Then move us to that place." He replied: "I fear lest, before I move you all, the fishermen arrive. Nevertheless, I shall attempt this as far as is within my power." And he began, under such a pretext, to carry away certain fish each day. He had a certain <gloss>having</gloss> building on a cliff of the mountain, pretending it was the promised place, and he carried them there immediately. Again, in the passing days, the crab did not <unclear>envy</unclear> them, but asked the swan to move him as well, just like the fish. Taking him up, the swan flew away. He used to eat the fish while they hung from him. Wanting to eat this one as well, the crab—sharing the fate of the fish—saw the bones lying there, detected the trick, and within himself...

<column-break/>

...the one doomed to death calculated: <gloss>if</gloss> "Just as he has played his tricks on us," he said of the swan while thinking to himself, "I must not endure an inglorious death, but die either well or ill." And suddenly seizing the swan's neck with his claw, he violently strangled him. "For this reason, then, I have narrated these things to you, O crow, so that you do not die filthily, as one is often caught by one's own snares on a reed instead of receiving honor. But it is necessary for you to take in hand this destruction of the snake." Then, having <unclear>lurked</unclear> beneath a rose-bush and <unclear>cleverly</unclear>... he took away a certain ornament of ours and <unclear>rested</unclear> at the snake's hole. <note>In this fable, the Crow steals a piece of jewelry from a human and drops it into the snake's burrow so that the humans will kill the snake while trying to retrieve the treasure.</note> Honors are paid to you for this. Alas, the <term>servants of Hermes</term> <note>original: "hermetes." Likely referring to travelers or messengers, or perhaps a corruption of a word for "servants/men."</note> kill the snake. Now the crow has done what he long intended against the snake. Then, besides these things, there is the crown of life and grace. For these things are possible, truly, as wisdom prevails over power.
And the <term>Crowned One</term> <note>original: "Stephanites," the second jackal, whose name means 'crowned' or 'garlanded.'</note> said: "If the bull were matched against bravery, I would be ashamed to say such things; but I know this much: the Tracker truly spoke as such a person. But by this [wisdom] I am able to cast him down, trusting in my own power in many matters; just as through such a piercing of wit, the hare cast down the lion." For then they say that there was a grassy and watery plain, where various honorable beasts dwelled together, leading an abundant life apart from others. However, they were troubled by the fear of the lion. Therefore, having devised a plan, they all approached the lion together to <term>pay court</term> <note>original: "therapeuein," meaning to serve, heal, or attend to a superior</note> to him, saying: "O King, [do not hunt] us..."

It then begins the fable of the Lion and the Hare to further illustrate how a small creature can defeat a mighty one through intellect.</summary>

...and that we may be freed from labor, we shall make them untroubled. Having agreed to that great proposal, [one] was learned from us; and we, in turn, shall hunt their borders. We continue to be terrified every day; it would therefore be a terrible thing if your table were not provided for each day from among us. And having greeted the **Lion** with this [proposal], they carried it out each day, casting lots against one another. Marking the one chosen by the lot, they would send him to him. When the lot fell to the **Hare**, he said to them: "If you will cooperate with me, take heart; I shall free you from this harsh tyrant." But they said: "How shall we not do so?" And he, since you have taken away what was owed to the lion, said, "Do not hurry my journey; but because we have found [another] near him, I shall hide myself." And they did this. The hare used a slower pace in his journey, so that the lion became wild with hunger. When he saw the hare, he said to him: "Why have you delayed until now?" And he [the hare] spoke quietly and quickly: "O Master, I was being led with some of your fellow-yoked hares, but another [lion] snatched them away. Though I shouted many things at him and bore witness that they were sent for you, I was not heard." And they said, "Let us plunder him." Being enraged, the lion said, "Who else [is there]? For I alone am the god of this land." He [the hare] deceived him, leading him toward a certain very deep **well** <note>original: "phrear." This is the classic "fountain" or "well" motif where the predator sees his own reflection.</note>, which was prepared so that the lion could look into it. And he [the hare] said to him: "Look, the lion who eats from your table!"

<column-break/>

And the hare, taking him, showed him the water <note>original: "limnen" (lake/pool), though typically a well in this fable.</note> and the reflection of himself and the other. Being completely deceived by this, he threw himself into the water and was drowned. And he [the hare] was praised for having destroyed the bull <note>The text here appears to conflate the lion's death with the ongoing plot against the Bull, or perhaps uses "bull" metaphorically for a strong adversary.</note> and not by the barking of the lion, being asked of this. And there are many others among us who, through the friendship of the **Bull**, have been neglected. But if his blood is of the same nature as yours, you should abstain from the task. For such a thing for him is <unclear>sudden</unclear> to you. Then, so that he might not remain as a witness, **the Tracker** <term>Ichnelates</term> <note>Ichnelates, meaning "The Tracker," is the Greek name for the jackal Dimna in the original Sanskrit cycle. He represents the cunning, often treacherous advisor.</note> went to the lion, full of dejection. And the lion asked him about his suffering: "Has something bad happened?" And he said: "If something has happened or some slip has occurred, whether to another or to me..." but when the lion perceived that what was being said by him seemed to be heard with obscurity, he dared to pour out [his heart] through him, whether through goodwill for the one hearing what had been done. Unless someone of his unrewarded [servants] often takes courage, thinking that they might move a <gloss>suggestion</gloss> for me. "Knowing therefore, O King, that you are adorned with understanding and <unclear>shame</unclear>, I have taken courage to speak with you about these things you hear, if you wish. You yourself do not gain; my service is excessive, pure, and sincere. And it remains so, as the things spoken to me do not appear to end. But let us be delivered, though we are irrational, even as our souls have sinned against your soul. And always those close to me appear, hiding nothing of what is necessary and profitable for me." He did not say then that the subject had good fortune toward pleasant things and...

67
<column-break/>
...to prevail even further; through which he serves some other leader; or to attain honor from a king; and to pretend to appear earnest, until the grasp of a completely wicked habit is achieved. Having attained this, he will add a wicked turn against us; just as with this one, like a snake, being naturally crooked and twisted, since there is no bond of upright-heartedness that <unclear>is</unclear> a guide, those who are far from the bond of earnestness appear to hold a crooked impulse once again. 

O King, he who does not receive from his counselors as they converse even the most burdensome words is like a sick person who turns away from the usefulness of bitter and beneficial medicines and ignores the physician. It will be known to these that it is better to have subjects and friends who hold onto goodwill than those who put on the masks beloved by kings. Nevertheless, with the praises from good men—those leaders who are celebrated and praised—you might treat their arrogance similarly by making it clear to yourself. To me, however, among the wealthy, though led about by much toil, [I find] that among friends it is not far from <margin>the</margin> animals. 

It is said of this that it is preferable to prevail where someone sleeps amidst fire and thirst, or an eternal sleep due to the plots of those around him, rather than his own life. But the lion [replied]: "If anyone looks kindly upon the ancient ways, even if he is disposed most sharply, he cannot cause harm while being a grass-eater <note>herbivorous</note>, while I am a blood-drinker <note>carnivorous</note>. For nature prepared that other kind of food for him." 

But the tracker <note>The jackal Ichnelates</note> said: "Do not be misled

<column-break/>

by such a goal. Even if the bull does not strike you down and pursue you by his own hand, he will do this through another. For it is said that if some lover or kinsman does not <unclear>tread together</unclear> with him upon the same boundaries, before you recognize his habits, you might suffer the same fate as the louse. 

For since a certain louse lived in the precious bedding of the greatest of lovers, it remained hidden for a long time, feeding on his blood while he slept a quiet sleep, and it did not even crawl <unclear>on its own</unclear>. But on one of the nights, he took in a flea beside him. The flea, sensing the prey and biting him unbearably, woke him from his quiet sleep. Enraged, the man felt around his bed, and finding the louse, he killed it, while the flea quickly jumped away and was saved. Thus you have known the bull by his voices, but you will fear those who promise these things." 

The lion, persuaded by these words, said: "And what must be done?" 

The tracker said: "When a road is worn, it is better healed if one does not roll further upon it; and the harm of corrupted food is driven out through vomiting." <note>An analogy for expelling a dangerous member from court.</note>

The lion said: "Henceforth I will make it clear to him to depart from us." 

But the tracker, knowing that if the bull is not separated from the lion, his own trickery will be revealed later, [thought]: "This is not clearly beneficial to me; for if the bull realizes he was caught by you, being ignorant he becomes useless; or he will prepare himself for a future battle-array. For the most intelligent kings punish openly those things that are manifest..."

...having sinned, both secretly and obscurely. And wishing to maintain control, the lion said: "I will go with him, I will give him a sign, and I will be fully convinced of the truth of what has been said." The counsel of God. 

The Tracker intended to go to the bull and rouse him against the lion. When he stood before the lion's presence, he disguised himself as one who had heard such a deceptive thing, and said: "If you would command me, O King, I will go to the bull to persuade him; and his mind will be driven by me from our very first conversation." And the lion commanded him to go. 

And having reached the bull, he entered looking gloomy and dejected. The bull received him kindly, since he noted his delay, and asked about the animals and their well-being. But the Tracker said: "I am not performing deeds of good things, for they are not under my own control, but due to another cause with him." But the firm [one]. For honors are like brothels which are joined to many men; or a school which children attend, who are always departing as others enter <note>This is a common medieval metaphor describing the fickle nature of a King's favor—it belongs to everyone and no one for long.</note>. But those around him are envious ones who were persuaded with envy, so that they are hot with the conversations of the wicked around him, showing forth a portent from which suspicions arise concerning the good.

The bull said: "You have spoken well; for I was persuaded by his sweetness before; and following the taste, one must experience the bitter poison <note>original: deleterion</note>. And why must I, through something irrational, be together with a raw-flesh-eating beast in another greedy way? He has entangled me in such a coincidence, suffering something similar to the honey-gatherers <note>those who seek the sweetness of honey but are stung by the bees</note>."

For it follows that there was once a very great lion, and near his domain there was a road. There were three animals there, befriended to one another: a wolf, a crow, and a jackal. Now, while some merchants were passing through, a camel was left behind from the group. She, having come before the lion, showed her submissiveness. The lion said: "If you choose to live your life with us in this place, I grant you this: you shall live with us in safety and in a peaceful life and release for the rest of your days." And she remained there until the time of death <note>meaning, until the conflict arose</note>. ...they were fanned into life; and what sort of small creature do we find here? They supposed it to be fire and were blowing on it, toiling away. <note>This refers to the Fable of the Monkeys and the Glowworm. The monkeys, shivering from cold, mistake a glowworm for a spark of fire and try to kindle a blaze from it.</note> A certain crow seeing them, as was his custom, said: "Do not be deceived, O you! For what lies before you is not fire." When they heard this, the crow came down. But one of them, seeing him coming down, said: "O crow, do not be mad; do not put yourself into our hands." <note>In traditional versions of this fable, the monkey kills the bird for giving unwanted advice, illustrating that it is futile to correct those who are willfully ignorant.</note> ...straightway... <unclear>the crooked</unclear>... it was necessary for you... for he tests... which the crow did not take to heart as <term>interference</term> <note>original: "παραμέσεως" (interference/mediation). The crow's meddling in the monkeys' business leads to his demise.</note>, and he was punished. So that you do not suffer the same thing and [suffer] for your kindness and the wickedness of others. You hear the advice; and this he does, like the way of a **wicked man**.

### Of the Partner and the Simpleton.

A father said that a wicked man and a **simpleton** <note>original: "σχολαστικός" (scholasticus). While this literally means a scholar, in the context of these fables it refers to a naive, pedantic person who lacks common sense.</note>, being companions, made an agreement with one another. And while walking much together, they found a thousand gold talents. The simpleton said to the wicked man: "Let us divide the gold." But the wicked man said: "No, lest someone see us and take it, let us take a little together, and hide the rest under the earth. And so we shall come whenever we have need, and we shall draw from it in portions. In this way, our partnership will last for a long time." And the simpleton was persuaded by his advice. They hid the gold under a certain tree with good omens. But after hiding it, the wicked man went out secretly that same day and took the whole treasure of gold. After a short time, the simpleton said to him: "Let us go...

<column-break/>

...it seems to me that we should take back some part of the gold. Let us go out and set aside the value of what we find." And the wicked man answered, tearing his hair and striking his chest, crying out against the simpleton as if he had stolen the treasure. <note>The wicked man uses a display of false grief to frame his innocent partner.</note> But the simpleton swore ten thousand oaths that he had done no such thing. "It shall be a matter for the judge to decide between us." And the judge said: "Present your proofs." [The wicked man] said: "The site itself will bear witness." He boldly said: "The tree itself will bear witness for me." When they arrived at the site, he had already persuaded his father to hide inside the hollow tree so that he might answer the questions asked: that the simpleton alone was guilty of stealing the treasure. The father said: "I will do this, but see that no one discovers me." And going off, he entered the tree. When the judge arrived there and asked what sort of man the gold-thief was, a voice was returned saying that the simpleton had taken it. Having heard the voice, [the judge] sensed the **trickery** <note>original: "δόλος" (dolos). A common theme in these fables is the exposure of sophisticated fraud by a wise authority figure.</note>. He ordered a fire to be set under the tree to burn it to ashes. When the fire was lit and the smoke began to rise, the [father] immediately cried out. Falling out, he confessed the deceit. Whereupon, being punished by the judge, the [wicked man] was held responsible and [the judge] seized the gold. And so, the simpleton remained alone. Such is the way of the wicked path regarding property. But I am not... like the snake... for not only...

<meta>This page continues the Greek translation of "Stephanites and Ichnelates," an 11th-century adaptation of the Sanskrit "Panchatantra." Following the previous page's discussion on the dangers of bad company, it recounts the famous fable of the "Iron-Eating Mice" and begins Book Two, focusing on the Lion King and his courtly intrigue.</meta>

...he declares: for it is well said: one must flee the **wickedness of men**, even if they are acquaintances and friends. For they have done something truly marvelous: a merchant who was a neighbor brought one hundred pounds of iron to another merchant and deposited it for safekeeping. After a short time, he came back seeking it. But the other, answering, said: "The mice ate it up." The merchant listened with mildness and said: "This is nothing strange; for mice have strong teeth and they do eat iron." 

After a few days, the merchant found the neighbor’s little child playing outside the city; he took him and hid him. When the father came looking for the child, he went to the merchant crying. But the merchant said to him: "I saw a hawk snatching your child." The father wept, saying: "And how can a hawk snatch a child?" And the merchant replied: "In a place where mice eat iron, there hawks also snatch children." The man, being ashamed of this, returned the iron and received his child back. 

Thus, may you also put an end to all wandering cowardice. But the wicked man is now even more wicked; for just as **bitter fruit**, even if it is smeared many times with honey, does not change its own nature and does not receive sweetness, so too the company of base men must be fled. Thus the affairs of men <unclear>...</unclear>

<column-break/>

...association; and wickedness must be fled. For just as those who have anointed themselves with myrrh take on the fragrance of the myrrh, so too the company of good men produces a certain fragrance from itself. Likewise, the company of the base instills wickedness. In addition to these things, I may appear burdensome, just as before; for the generous are always burdensome to the ungrateful, the wise to the unlearned, and the liberal to the stingy, the forbearing to the bitter, and the obedient to the disobedient. 

We say this, lest the lion ever act like the bull. And immediately, not intending his removal, did not <term>Ichnelates</term> <note>original: "ἰχνηλάτης" (ichnēlātēs), meaning "the tracker." He is one of the two jackals who act as protagonists; his name is the Greek rendering of the Arabic 'Dimna'.</note> go to the lion, his companion, and finding him, say: "Why do you labor in vain over the hunt? Do you not know that when someone is bitten by a viper, he cuts off his finger? He rejects his own limb as if it were a stranger's, so that its venom does not spread to the whole body." 

And the lion, hearing this, was already comforted by his words. Thus the <term>philosopher</term> <note>original: "φιλόσοφος." This refers to the sage Bidpai (Sendebar), the narrator of the stories.</note> said that a wicked and wretched man, if he persuades others among the honorable, corrupts their glory and instills disturbances. Such are my words.

But the philosopher, taking up the word, said: "After the removal of the bull, the lion went out with his counselor; the **leopard** was the teacher and faithful counselor to the lion. <meta>This page continues the narrative of "Stephanites and Ichnelates," specifically the aftermath of the Lion King slaying his faithful friend, the Bull, due to the machinations of Ichnelates the Jackal. ...gate. He found, I say, the one who was smiling mockingly at him <note>original: "καπομειδίζοντα" - implying a sneering or condescending smile</note> regarding the things done to the Bull. He did not even look up, but having learned that the **claws of the lion** <note>original: "λέοντος χείρας" - literally "hands," often used for paws/claws in this personified context</note> were so great as to tear things apart, and having heard that he should go to his mother’s side, the Lion already knew the bitter things he had heard. As day was dawning, they came to him before the Lion.

Seeing him, she called him dejected—for how could he not be, since he was about to dance <note>A metaphor for the erratic behavior of the guilt-stricken</note> through many absences? "O child," she said, "never be dejected because of me, for it works another way; rather, I care for your speech and my own heart. But I was unaware that you were a free man, though it has been witnessed to me. By no means should you be disheartened because of this, as if on account of the bull, especially since you are a man of justice. Let the favor toward him be distinguished by the disposition he held toward you. For it is said that they do not fall to the ground, but rather—tell me—how was your heart disposed toward the bull?"

The Lion replied: "The bull was always a **friendship** <note>original: "φίλοτης" - the Lion uses the abstract noun for the relationship</note> to me. And yet, I was led to impulses by all his transgressions, and a suspicion of sin concerning him once stole over me. Nevertheless, I am overcome with grief rather at his death, and a great shame is upon us, lest this was not as it should be. I wish to learn this: was I truly deceived by the **crafty one's** <note>Refers to Ichnelates the Jackal</note> claws of words and false syllogisms? I would be ashamed if I have heard such a thing, or if a true assumption is confirmed. Why do I say I am coming to you? I have told you my words, O Mother, before your presence, as if departing by a breath of air, for I was lied to regarding the bull."

And the Lion said—

<column-break/>

"And what blood is there for you to fear such a thing? It was being tracked <note>original: "ἐφωδεῖτο" - related to the name Ichnelates, 'the tracker'</note> again and again. When I do this, let him not ignore his own gentleness."

The Lion said: "It is necessary for me to observe such things in others, but for myself, my goal is the **revelation of truth** <note>original: "ἀλήθειας φανέρωσιν"</note> and to be the avenger of the wronged. Since I call you to this sin, a just king does not hold back what I might know of a doubtful case. For I fear lest, having similarly passed through the slaying of the bull, I might fall into the tracks of the same error."

The Mother of the Lion said: "I do not suppose you will be destroyed by us, nor will you fail to gain your life."

The Lion replied: "But I wish to learn the truth of the matter."

She said: "I fear that if I do this, I shall be shown to be **unscrupulous** <note>original: "ἀσυνείδητος" - literally 'without conscience'</note>."

Hearing these things, the Lion excused himself and presented himself as responsible for all of it. He did not call upon the mouthpiece <note>original: "στομίχῃ" - a derogatory reference to the Jackal as a mere 'mouth' or 'talker'</note>. But behold, the Tracker <note>term: <term>Ichnelates</term> — the name of the Jackal, meaning 'the one who follows the tracks'</term> was dejected, and he said to those nearby, seeing the Lion dejected and filled with gloom:

Taking up the word, the Mother of the Lion said: "Behold, neither is dejected, but nevertheless, they have allowed those living together to be afraid until now, having persuaded them that this wretched servant was a **Bull-slayer** <note>original: "ταυρομάμελεῖν"</note>."

The Tracker said: "I see that every false pretense has passed; I am ready to receive the misfortune rather than continue doing this. On this account, let there be a greeting to the ascetics and to all who weave words together, for I was pursued into the deserts. For as long as I was well-disposed toward the King, I ran about against the Bull and made him of no account by our will. But it remains for me to be accurately examined until I find myself in his presence..."
