...all things are a punishment of anger; for the domestic fire <note>original: "οἰκουρὸν πῦρ" — a metaphor for latent or hidden anger compared to a hearth fire that stays within the home</note> is revealed in the stone or the wood, but it is made manifest in battle by honor. **Technical Vocabulary**
<term>Tracker (Ichnelates)</term>: The jackal protagonist, here a symbol of the slanderer.
<term>Chief Cook (Protomageiros)</term>: A court official who here acts as an accuser.
<term>Compassion (Eusplanchnia)</term>: A royal virtue Ichnelates appeals to.
<term>Slanderer (Sykophantes)</term>: The primary charge against Ichnelates.
<term>Murderous (Miaiphonos)</term>: An epithet used in the heated debate.
<term>Physiognomy (Physiognomia)</term>: The practice of judging a person's character from their facial features.
<term>Stephanites and Ichnelates</term>: The title of the Greek version of the fables of Kalila and Dimna.

<summary>Ichnelates is subjected to a character assassination by the Chief Cook based on physiognomy, leading to a debate over hypocrisy and the law. Despite the Tracker's clever defense using scripture and parables, he is returned to prison and sentenced to physical mutilation.</summary>

### Page 77

Indeed, there is a need for money; without these things, none of the necessary tasks can be accomplished. For just as one who desires to reach the crossing towards piety <note>The author uses a maritime metaphor where "piety" is a destination reached by a difficult sea-crossing.</note> over the sea, he cannot reach it through the air and a favorable climate alone, even if he desires to reach it with a super-abundant cargo. When he dares the danger and attempts things through sins, he often loses what he has here <unclear>of which</unclear> so that he also loses what comes after. For just as good and well-tempered breezes carry the cargo, so this cargo, hating and being hated, grieves the one who accumulates it, rather than gladdens him. 

And even if it does gladden him, it is not beneficial; he himself is its master. Everyone knows the nature of what belongs to the wealthy: in his lapses, he is hungry and blamed. For if the poor man is energetic, he is called impudent; if he is of tall stature <note>Likely referring to a person who carries themselves with a certain physical or social presence.</note>, he is called a wastrel and expensive; if he is mild and approachable, he is called powerless; if he is knowledgeable, he is called a fool; if he is quiet, he is called speechless; and if he is entirely high-minded, he is called uncultured. For he would not be escorted through life, nor by the sun, nor by speaking; he is made to use shameful testimonies. By Zeus, such a man, who might be honorable, makes pleasure the source of his honor; but among others, the cargo is what matters. He alone, when he makes the choice of the debtor; for in the one receiving honor, the head bows down; and similarly, it draws him along lightly. Instead of which, nevertheless, this water is according to strength alone; he who was sensible took in his hand a certain branch from those lying nearby—

—And he struck me on the head. And he himself upon the same... at least again <gloss>somewhere</gloss> once more <gloss>somewhere</gloss> they grew bold; so that again he struck me with the branch on the head violently and directly. I hated the hand so much that I no longer refrained from hearing <note>The text suggests the Mouse became so traumatized by the monk's strike that even the sound or mention of gold became unbearable.</note> of gold; and gracefully everyone the evil one in life, or greedily humbled.

In our cheerfulness, we are not even able to attempt [sin]. Resting after life; the sensible and unchangeable one alone, whom we similarly held in regard.

And the King said again to the philosopher: I have learned through this example the nature of sincere friendship; now therefore, give me a similar example of how one must watch out for an enemy, whether one must flee him or hide from him. The philosopher said: He who places his trust in an enemy will suffer the [evils] of life. For it is said that upon a certain very great and high tree, a gathering of crows dwelt—as many as a thousand crows. There were also in another place nearby, a thousand owls <term>kouphoi</term> <note>original: "κοῦφοι" (kouphoi). Literally "light" or "vain" ones. And one night, they came upon [the crows], and killed many, and wounded others. At dawn, the leader of the crows gathered those under him, and having formed a council, said: "See the rage of the owl, what it has done to us. It has overcome our power, committed slaughter, wounded us, and plucked our feathers. And the cause of all these things was their daring to raid us and test their strength against us. See then, O humble ones." Now there were with him five chief advisors of such character; one of whom, taking up the word, said: "Nothing else will save us from this raid but to flee this wrath, and leave, and no longer endure living here; nor yet..."

The third said: "These men do not speak well, O King; but we must learn from those enemies if they wish to establish friendship with us, so as to make peace through worldly gifts. But if we are rejected despite the money, let us cast it away and rush into battle. For kings, caring for their own country and standing against the enemy, shedding one another's blood for their need, give up the things they possess."

The fourth said: "This is not a good counsel; rather, it is better to endure in life and wander about than to fall so far under enemies over whom we are superior and more honorable. For fire is quenched by water; poison is healed by medicines; and even love ceases when it finds success. But the fire of malice is always burning between us.' Having said these things, the Owl departed, full of wrath and hatred. But the Crow, regarding what he had accomplished, remained. When the King heard these things, he said nothing more about the matter at hand..."

"And what need is there to do this here, if the talk of war is not silenced? For where was this before? But perhaps we can succeed through force by means of a strategy. At night, taking their positions, the Owls arrived at the tree, and it was not a matter of <gloss>prophesying</gloss> <note>original: "μαντεύειν" (manteuein) - to divine or predict</note> but only of seeing the Crow lying there, beaten. The Crow said: "I am such-and-such, the son of so-and-so. You will not hear where the other crows have turned, for how would you know them? I lie here alone in blood. Do you not know who I am? I was the first advisor to the leader of the crows. Because I gave him such counsel, they treated me with such cruelty." The Crow continued: "How could I have looked upon anything good? They entangled me in such evils. When a council was formed among us Crows, each gave their opinion as they saw fit. You were the subject of the plan. I refused to agree, and I spoke with great sorrow. As a result, the Crows attacked me, and because of this, I have fallen here before you."

...And indeed, as we have said, she prevailed. But she, having been filled with rage, threw the table even at the King's head, and the wine was spilled as she reached out. The King, becoming utterly enraged, commanded his first counselor to behead her and to show her no mercy at all. The counselor, taking her away and departing, reasoned within himself that perhaps the King would later feel regret <note>Original: μετάμελος (metamelos). Because of this, he secretly handed her over to his own household and ordered that she be treated with every honor.

Now the King, having become exceedingly regretful and remembering her graceful disposition, was greatly disheartened. The first counselor, perceiving the King’s dejection, went in to him—not as he was accustomed to do for matters of honor, or for parts and cares of state, but appearing cheerful from the pleasure of his wisdom and his thoughtful jests. Using jokes from books, he comforted the King, and said that he must speak of a certain necessary thing. 

He told of a certain passion involving pigeons. There was a certain male and a certain female pigeon. They filled their own nest with the finest wheat, and both made a pact not to touch the stored grain until the time of food-scarcity arrived. But when summer came, the sun, drawing out the moisture from the wheat, caused it to shrink into a very small volume. The male pigeon assumed the female had consumed it; because of this, he did not cease striking her mercilessly until he killed her. But when winter

<column-break/>

arrived and the wheat became swollen again, he realized his mistake—that he had killed his mate for no reason. Having realized this, he mourned continually, and from his grief, his heart broke. "In this way," the counselor said, "I believe those mastered by anger suffer. But what does it matter to you, O King, concerning one woman when you have ten thousand others? Yet if you mourn her departure as my doing..."

When the King heard these things, he became very sorrowful, fearing that he had truly destroyed his wife. He said to him: "Such is my fate, such a hateful seat." But the counselor said: "Do not remain in a state of hopelessness." The King replied: "No one among all men is without sin, and perfection exists in no man." After much discussion concerning the King’s despair, the counselor brought to him the more favorable news. When the King saw her, he was overjoyed and honored the counselor with great dignities.

### Book II

The King said, "Let us rise, O philosopher, and let us see how one must beware of enmities and guard against holding grudges." 

The philosopher said: "It is told, O King, that once a certain dog lived near a house by the water with a weasel <note>Original: γαλοπίκην (galopikēn). This refers to a small carnivorous mammal, often translated as a weasel or a marten in the 'Seven Sages' tradition.</note>. For this reason, both were under the King's protection. It happened at that time that the woman bore a child. The dog used to play with the child of the house <unclear>as if it were his own</unclear>. Every day he stayed with the child and remembered the King’s child as his own..." <note>The text here transitions into the famous 'Faithful Hound' archetype, where a pet protects a child from a predator but is initially misunderstood by the parent.</note>

# Philosophical Works

And to Apollonius himself, as he was pursuing honor—which happened first to all—the King’s son once became enraged against a guard. I was called privately to the garden, and he intended to kill him. If he stood there, he would have found for himself the very beginning of calamities, for which men say: **“Woe to those who dwell in the courts of kings!”** <note>Original: οὐαὶ τοῖς συν αυλίζουσι τοῖς βασιλεῦσι. This is a common Byzantine proverb warning that the favor of a monarch is volatile and dangerous.</note> And like white birds, my mind turned to smoke; for they do not perform their acts of kindness for those who are in need. And as long as they possess someone, they receive useful things in abundance. Into this difficulty, they even accuse their servants of the greatest things, nor can they see how it is sung; whether the things said are a treasury of discoveries or something false, so as to take me away.

And as for this very harsh child—those who desired it once in the child's old age—their eyes were cast out. But they did not place even the slightest blame on him; rather, because the King, being wise in his anger, supposed the chest <term>kista: a basket or box, often used in ancient mysteries or for storing sacred scrolls</term> had fallen from him. And the witness, coming to him, called out and said: “O! And what punishment do I have after this suffering? I shall not disregard those who are likewise burdened after you, but it shall be fulfilled in the future. You shall pass me through until I am cared for by your children.” But the King said: “Indeed, I knew of you. I shall be an assistant in correcting the children; it is not necessary to do this. Your possessions are indebted to me, or rather, it is for me to come together with you. O, since I first showed no mercy!” And that one said: “Do not call me anymore; for it is necessary for me to be after you, and I do not dare even to be an enemy of mine.” I take heart in the words of that blessed man; both now and in the holy things to be brought to fulfillment. This is honored from afar...

<meta>catchword: until</meta>

<column-break/>

...until friends ought not to be with those who divide. But toward a brother and a foster-sibling, keep the pains of their insults in silence. Do not make the children public; and what kind of education or strife is there? As for those of pride, let it please him alone. And having received this, I left behind a virtue of grief among you, that I might be away from the return.

<gloss>Before the brothers; regarding the preceding book.</gloss>

Where is the potter with the rustic complexion in this book?
Why is this present volume a lake for all?
They play with my eyes for the sake of joy;
A friend is called, binding the legs that flow around.
And in the monastery, let the common speech of words be told;
In justice, you placed mercy upon those who hold power.
<margin>
Indeed
to yield
first: mine
</margin>
To the eyelids of those, and the race of crows;
Of bulls, turtles, goats, and gazelles;
Of whales and those that are possessed;
Of ivy-vines, swans, fish, and crabs;
And of the ancient things existing from vice.
They encounter a fate that is not favorable;
By images and the judgment of former children;
And by the flow, may you learn with those of understanding.
May you find from this very thing the life of self-control,
Fleeing the starting points of evils and flatterers.
Blaming the first thing, limp away from such ways;
But I compare friends to friends, a clear judgment.
And everything of the first things, gracefully and orderly,
As one who looks down upon the beautiful before the rose;
As the witness of the interpreter, not a sacred memory.

# Philosophical Works
## Of the Most Learned Michael Psellos

->**A Dedicatory Poem**<-

Beautiful are the things of our golden race,
and I, like a wooden vessel full of precious stones,
seek a healing of the anointing from the Thracians,
holding the present moment, O Renowned Paleologus,
shining with glory, a fame branched out to all;
give of the Thrice-Great Lord himself <note>A common Byzantine epithet for God, echoing the "Trisagion" or perhaps the "Hermetic" tradition of Hermes Trismegistus.</note> 
to Andronicus, who grew most beautiful of the Graces.
And in July, amidst the hymns of praise in Cyprus—
may you also drink with a temperate trembling here,
and do all things as if they were for your 
benefit, as a spokesperson for the Great King,
most swift in glory in those things which must be done;
for to those who approach you, there is no mockery.
For you might say this work is worthy of you, all-learned one;
to us, your wretched and un-anointed servants, you provide
a great consolation and a rest from our despondency.

<column-break/>

<insert>What he says concerning the forms</insert> <note>original: "quid dicit de formis"</note>
As much as beauty concerns sense-perception <term>aisthesis: the faculty of perceiving the world through the five senses</term>: 
for it exists in the sight of colors, and in everything providing the reason to judge what is around us; and in hearing, in the compositions of words. For this is not the same as that; these things might change by a superior rhythm, which the hand-made imitations of youth have labored to soothe. The beautiful and the just are manifestations that adorn harmony; they are the symphony. For the things here do not come from the primary things <note>A reference to the Platonic "Forms" or "Ideas" which are the perfect originals of which earthly things are mere copies.</note>; rather, everything here is an image and an imitation. 

Since beauty resides elsewhere in divine things, my reasoning is in accord with this: because they are "reasons" <term>logoi: here meaning the rational principles or blueprints that give structure to matter</term>, even as the law-witnesses the living. But also in the underlying subject there is a manifestation of sense-perception. It is said that they are the same equal allies. And to that same thing, demonstrating nature just as all other things are delighted from that source, so too the perception of the golden-oracle <note>Original: χρυσμοῦ (chrysmou). Likely a play on words between "chrismos" (oracle) and "chrysos" (gold).</note>—you do not know those who say these things: that the wandering bodies are encompassed by Reason <term>Logos</term>. 

Rather, everything that is orderly is the Cosmos. Why speak of other things above us, placing the same state in another form? For this is Nature: either the brilliance of the Beautiful, or of Justice, or of Truth itself. This is the Cosmos <note>The Greek word "Cosmos" implies both the universe and "order/beauty."</note> for us all, so to speak. You shall not possess a partial spirit that has an "idea," unmixed; but from the Sun, those things both in beauty and magnitude and in sense-perception are held and have arrived in the same way. You have let them pass. And in immeasurable movement, these things in a state of flux <note>A reference to the Heraclitean idea that the physical world is in a constant state of "flow" (rhoe).</note> do not know how to participate—outpourings from that place, whether they are participants or if you create a spirit. But not in those things which are least; for beauty is not there—beauty itself is not that which is the Good. It has fallen away...

...is stationed between **Eternity** <term>Aeon: the realm of the timeless and unchanging divine.</term> and **Time**. For since this likeness is, on one hand, alteration; on the other, they are our changeable motions; are they not the same causes in my view? The eternities shall be warmed together; for indeed its eternal essences have been constrained by the colors of motion, nor has she <note>Likely referring to the Soul.</note> run back toward the motions, to burn me away in vanity, or to reach the unchanging supreme. And it <insert>the Soul</insert> has let go of the time that was being cut and has withdrawn from the whole toward the eternal, participating in the glory of the better portion <insert>s</insert>, which are warming and providing for the **Universal Restoration** <note>original: "apokatastasis," the Neoplatonic and early Christian idea of the return of all things to their original, divine state.</note> of the whole from vanity. For the soul that has become hardened <note>The Greek "cherrotheisa" suggests a soul that has become barren or solid like earth, needing divine cultivation.</note> is honored eternally when she comes to be with Him; by how much more shall she not be dissolved into time for you, but into eternity? From which point her progression stands still, as she is then all-sufficient under eternity, steady and unmoved. Yet a certain meddlesome property moves this, or rather the soul, being weak, has the form of a part <insert>s</insert>; she was neglected in the hunt for **Being** <note>original: "tou ontos," referring to ultimate reality or God.</note>, having undergone this; and again, by fleeing from the gentle one, I am [moved]. For perhaps, according to the distance of all things from eternity, she receives a more ancient portion; and while she is in the midst of this, he who loves escapes. And having reached the end, she consumed the motion; then, the divine having run along with her, the outcome shows all things: having been surrounded by the grains of the days, she is again patient in movement; having received the ring, <note>The "ring" or "seal" is a common metaphor for the divine influence or "stamp" placed upon lower reality.</note> let the seal not be unmoved, nor the one who thinks the calf is not falling.

...either of the eternal rest, or of nature. For to us who are joined to this, every harmonious sound uses the eternally strengthened powers. Neither living nor burying those who, in the month of July, see the same thing. And I am hardened together with the sea. And the name is "tongue"; in a craftsman-like way, you alone, Harmony, describe the construction thus: for it is necessary, while I am the **First Cause** <note>The "First Cause" refers to the ultimate source of all existence, often identified with "The One" or God.</note>, that these things in the universe do not stand still; and must not the outrunnings and the motions be settled into a stillness of identity? +

But you cease beforehand and produce outpourings: which are more numerous, the angels or the stars of the heavens? For just as the angels are a multitude—consider it—just as the stars, being similar and drawing near to the **Monad** <term>Monad: the "Unit" or "The One," representing the highest point of simplicity and source of all numbers in Neoplatonic thought.</term>, are fewer in quantity and in their total number. For example, the number three, just like the number four, are fewer than the number twenty; and three is the same [in principle], just as the later numbers of the unit are fewer, but those further away are more numerous. Thus, those closer to the one and only God are fewer in quantity, while those further away are more. Since the Archangels, Angels, and the **Super-cosmic Power** <note>The "Hyperkosmioi," or deities and powers existing above the physical world.</note> are above, the numbers are further away; therefore, humans are more numerous than the angels. Whence the angels are unique in their number—marvelous or as many as the middle-ground pours out toward the infinite. But she stations herself, the whole of the perfect...

## Of the Most Learned Michael Psellos

<margin>
Situated in the discourse concerning God <unclear>as it is written</unclear>

Something is stationed between the world and **Time**; since some things of ours exist as a result of our own alteration, while others come from a greater source. They are changeable and in motion; because of these very things, and in relation to **Eternity** <term>Aeon: the realm of timeless, unchanging divine reality.</term>, which holds life in a state of warmth, life is eternal. This [eternity] itself creates the works of **Time’s peace** <note>original: "eirēnēn." Psellos suggests that the order or "peace" found in the movements of time is actually a product of the eternal realm.</note>. You have constrained the motions which run headlong against the **Mind** <term>Nous: the divine Intellect.</term>. And of this [mind], the most unchanging of all is found somewhere. And time, being unmoved, has let go of the whole downward [region] toward which it had reached in the eternal; and it has obtained the better portion, nothing being more beautiful than the Father's provision: the transformation and restoration to the most beautiful state <note>original: "apokatastasis," the Neoplatonic and early Christian concept of the ultimate return of all things to their original divine perfection.</note>. Indeed, the time of a divine soul is called "eternal." Therefore, neither in its stay—by how much is it not time nor life—will it be dissolved into eternity, from which it also had its procession <term>Proodos: the "flowing out" or emanation of existence from the divine source.</term>; for it is encompassed by eternity from the beginning. And it is moved, a certain essential "many-suffering" <note>original: "poly-patheia," likely referring to the soul being subject to various influences, passions, or external stimuli.</note>. But the motion of this is the rather weak soul; she neglects the brief portion—not being moved toward the hunt for **Being**, which she undertook. You shall not cease, running away again under the influence of the wandering <unclear>...</unclear> and from the [source] of life from eternity, as it lies older beside the Father. For she <note>The Soul</note> participated in this, and having run out, the category [of soul] is consumed by motion; then, having run back up, she will again withdraw, which restricted her, so that she does not flow down again—not motion alone, but the cycle of the **unmoved seal** <note>The "seal" (sphragis) often refers to the divine Form or Idea imprinted upon the material world.</note> and the whole of the world that seems to be.

---

<page-num>94</page-num>

The pause of **Everlasting Life** <note>original: "aeizoia"</note>, our nature having fitted us to the harmonies of this whole, holds a concord toward eternity and that which can be participated in, because we do not [participate] in that which is more commonly agreed upon. For these things do not strike the same [note] as soulless things; for **Mind** alone is similar and participating in reverence. It is not even living in the state of the unmoved eternal, through the connection of it; and it is a necessary cleanser of the eternal. And the names do not mix its harmony, which alone undermines that [other] one. For it is necessary for me, being like the **First Cause**, to be in the same [state] as the same; and into the original identity, the excursions and motions must be settled. But this poses a difficulty regarding the food <note>original: "bora," possibly used metaphorically for spiritual sustenance or a corruption of a term regarding "parts."</note>: whether there are more angels, or the portion...

<margin>Note that the most divine angels are beside the **Monad**, while those [who are] less so are toward the external [realms].</margin>

...the angels are those of number and multitude. Those among us <note>meaning those closer to the human realm</note>, who are similar and approaching the **Monad** <term>The One: the ultimate, indivisible source of all reality in Neoplatonism.</term>, are fewer according to the quantity of number—just as the numbers under three or four are fewer than twenty or thirty. As the numbers closer to the Monad are fewer, while those further away are more numerous; so those closer to God, being similar in Mind, are fewer in quantity, while those further away are more numerous, reaching toward the limit of the archangels and angels and the **hypercosmic power** <term>Powers existing beyond the physical universe.</term>. Thus the [higher powers] are more [than the angels]... God is the only [source] of angels, and the whole is according to their number. For the Mind is poured out into the infinite...

# Philosophical Works
## Of Michael Psellos

The earlier discourse stated that not every part is participated in by every soul; rather, the most honorable of things is participable by a single soul <note>original: "methekton." In Neoplatonic thought, a "participated" thing is a version of a divine principle that has stepped down into the world to be shared by lower beings.</note>. All things of the same kind and all things of our own kind belong to this; for I would posited that every soul is a "portion." For the uniform and **Unparticipated** <term>Amethekton: A transcendent principle that remains pure and "unshared" by the material world, acting as a source for those that are shared.</term> principle leads the entire multitude of portions and the crowd of parts. Primarily, this is the uniform God. This unparticipated Monad is for those portions that are likewise uniform. Or rather, that which is participated in is in the world, while the **Hyper-cosmic** <term>Hyper-cosmic: Existing above or beyond the physical universe.</term> are not. It is separated from the World, if indeed the World is one; it contains infinite and many of the rational souls alone. And this one world is itself simultaneously ensouled and possessed of Mind. Where there is a soul in the world, it does not come from just anywhere from the hyper-cosmic alone, but rather the soul of the one world follows an order in heaven. For where we have the whole portion, this is the unparticipated, uniform, hyper-cosmic principle and that which is in the world; the hyper-cosmic soul is as the soul within the world.

<margin>
Concerning virtue
</margin>

### On Virtues: +

Since virtue is twofold, one being **intellectual** and the other **moral**, intellectual virtue owes its birth and growth for the most part to teaching—for which reason it requires experience and time. Moral virtue, however, comes about from **habit** <note>original: "ethos." Psellos is following Aristotle’s famous wordplay where "ēthikē" (moral/ethical) is derived from "ethos" (habit/custom).</note>. From this, it has even received its name, which is slightly diverged from the word for "habit." From this, it is also clear that none of the moral virtues arise in us by nature; for nothing that exists by nature can be changed by habit. For example, a **stone**, which by nature moves downward, could never be habited to move upward, not even if someone were to throw it upward ten thousand times. Nor do they arise in our other capacities contrary to nature.

<column-break/>

...to be held. The portion is perfected by the habit of virtue. +

### Further on Virtues: +

Virtue is indeed a **middle state** <term>The Mean: The Aristotelian "Golden Mean," where virtue is the balance between two opposing vices.</term>. It is by nature destroyed by deficiency and by excess. For example, regarding the strength of **courage**: the man who flees and fears everything, and endures nothing, becomes a coward. But the man who endures everything and fears nothing, but marches into everything, is rash. Likewise, the man who avoids all pleasures entirely is "insensible," while the one who marches into everything and indulges in all pleasures with excess is profligate. Regarding the unjust, he is ignorant. Virtue, then, concerns **actions** and **passions**. For every passion and every action is followed by pleasure or pain. Because of this, virtue is concerned with pleasures and pains. The man who performs just acts becomes "at one" with justice; the temperate man with temperance. And as for the brave man, they are brave; and for the prudent man, prudent. It is thus for all the virtues. +

### On Passions: +

There are three things found in the power of the soul: **Passions**, **Dispositions**, and **Capacities**. A "passion" is something such as anger, rage, fear, envy, confidence, joy, friendship, hatred, longing, jealousy, or pity. A "capacity" <term>Dynamis: The potential or biological ability to experience an emotion.</term> is that by which we are said to be capable of these—such as those by which we are able to be angered, or pained, or to feel pity. A "disposition" <term>Hexis: A settled state of character or "habit" that determines how one handles their emotions.</term> is that by which we stand well or poorly in relation to the passions. For instance, in relation to being angered: if we feel it violently or too weakly, we are in a "poor" disposition. It is similar with the others. But the virtues are neither passions nor capacities, but rather **dispositions**. For we are not called "excellent" or "base" according to our passions. It is by our virtues or our vices that we are called excellent or base. For example, when punished—

<summary>This page analyzes the Neoplatonic hierarchy of souls before transitioning into an Aristotelian summary of ethics, defining virtue as a "mean" between extremes and distinguishing between innate capacities and acquired habits.</summary>
<keywords>participation, unparticipated monad, world-soul, intellectual virtue, moral virtue, habit, the mean, courage, temperance, passions, dispositions, capacities</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the general nature of Eternity and Time discussed on the previous page to the specific way Souls participate in the divine hierarchy. It then shifts abruptly into a practical summary of Aristotelian ethics, categorizing the types of virtues and the mechanics of human emotion.</meta>

...we define them; just like the desires. But virtues are specific types of deliberate choices; and not just any choice. For we are said to be "moved" according to our passions <note>original: "pathe." In this context, passions are involuntary emotional movements like anger or fear.</note>, but according to our vices and virtues, we are not moved, but rather we are disposed in a certain way.

->On the Topic of Virtues: +<-

# All courage is a mean <term>Mesotēs: The "Golden Mean," Aristotle’s idea that virtue is the perfect middle point between two extremes of behavior.</term> of itself; acting as a middle point toward the excess of boldness and the deficiency of cowardice. It also concerns confidence regarding hardships. Of temperance, the excess is licentiousness <note>original: "akolasia," meaning a lack of restraint or indulgence in pleasure.</note>, while the deficiency is a lack of feeling. In the giving of money, the middle state is liberality; the excess is a wandering from oneself <note>original: "plane," here implying wasteful extravagance or losing one's way through spending</note> or a misguided choice of use, while the deficiency is illiberality, as seen in stinginess. Regarding large sums of money, the middle state is magnificence; the excess is vulgarity <note>original: "apeirokalia," literally a "lack of taste" or "inexperience with beauty."</note>, while the deficiency is meanness. Regarding honor and dishonor, the middle state is magnanimity; the excess is vanity—as it is called—and the deficiency is small-mindedness. Regarding the desire for honors, the one who exceeds in his desires is "ambitious," the one who falls short is "unambitious," and the one in the middle is unnamed. Many of the things spoken of here do not have proper names assigned to them. + What about the virtues: +

# Regarding gentleness, which is a certain middle state, the excess is the vice of irascibility, while the deficiency is a lack of anger. Regarding truth, the man in the middle is truthful, and this state is called truthfulness. As for pretension, the tendency toward the "greater" is boastfulness, and the one who has this is a boaster; the tendency toward the "lesser" is irony <note>original: "eironeia." In ancient Greek, this refers to self-deprecation or "playing down" one's abilities, the opposite of boasting.</note>, and the one who has it is ironic. Regarding pleasure in wit or in play; or regarding life, the one who does not show—

<column-break/>

—himself to be such is a friend, and the middle state is friendliness. The one who exceeds is a flatterer; the one who falls short is a sort of quarrelsome and difficult person. Regarding the pleasure found in play, the middle state is wit; the excess is buffoonery, while the deficiency is boorishness. The modest person is in the middle; the one who exceeds is over-ashamed, while the one who falls short is bashful <note>original: "katoplēx," literally "struck down" by shame or stunned.</note>—the one alongside the modest man. 

Concerning the principles of belief:

<margin>
Concerning the principles of belief
</margin>

# The first principle <term>Archē: A beginning, origin, or fundamental source of all things.</term> of all beings is the hyper-cosmic God. After this, some say there are many principles of physical things. To some, the principle of principles is said to be, for example, water and the Form. To those associated with the number nine, the elements of bodies are simply the elements themselves. Other philosophers have different views, having explored the matter thoroughly. To some, fire seemed to be the principle because of its power, as it consumes everything into itself. To others, it was air, because of the breath of living things. To others, it was water, because of its fertile nature. The evidence for these views and the ancient opinion was that most animals are born from the earth and are mostly dissolved back into it. To others, the four elements themselves were whispered to be the principles of beings. Plato called God the principle as the primary creator <note>original: "poietes," the Maker or Poet of the universe.</note>, and he held that the creation was received materially in an Idea. By "Idea," Plato means the first likeness of God. He does not seem to have spoken of the unutterable things, except as a reflection or impression, just as he said the world was created. + Concerning Ideas: +

# It is necessary to speak also of Plato’s views: the first of the creations are a kind of impression and the shaping of the thing created. For that same man says, concerning the sensible world, that a pattern <note>original: "paradeigma," a blueprint or model.</note> appeared to God before the ordering of the universe, and he spoke well of this. Regarding soulless things, and according to reason...

<summary>This page outlines Aristotelian ethics, defining virtue as the "mean" between extremes of behavior, before transitioning into a survey of the physical and metaphysical principles (Archai) of the universe according to ancient philosophers and Plato.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page moves from the discussion of Aristotelian virtues and the "Golden Mean" to a comparative analysis of Platonic and Aristotelian physics and metaphysics, specifically focusing on the nature of Forms, the classification of Causes, the perception of Color, the mechanics of Mixtures, and the hierarchy of the Soul.</meta>

And [they are distinguished] by the separations of bodies; for example, the universal Form of "Man," or of "Horse," or of "Ox," which Plato calls Forms <term>Eide: Often translated as "Ideas" or "Forms," these are the perfect, eternal archetypes that Plato believed existed in a non-physical realm, of which physical objects are mere shadows.</term>. The physicist studies the nature of the elements and the things within them. Aristotle, for his part, accepts some of Plato's doctrines but does not admit others. For while he grants existence to the Idea, he says that these do not subsist by themselves, but are separable [only in thought] from the body.

### On Causes

Aristotle dogmatically asserts four causes, as does Plato. Among these, the first is the **efficient cause** <note>original: "poietike," the "making" or "productive" cause.</note>, as in the case of a bed; for first, the carpenter makes the bed. Next to this is the **material cause**, such as the wood; for wood is the matter of a bed—one would not call neglected, raw matter a "bed." The **formal cause** <note>original: "eidike," pertaining to the "eidos" or shape.</note> is its shape, according to its specific form. The **final cause** <note>original: "telike," from "telos" or "end/goal"</note> is that for which the body rests <note>meaning: the purpose of the object, such as providing rest for a human body</note>; for the bed was constructed for this very reason. While Aristotle introduces these four, Plato adds two others. First, the **instrumental cause**, such as the saw by which we saw the wood when making the bed. Second is the **paradigmatic cause**; this exists in the intellect, as it is within the craftsman even before he performs his creative art. He looks toward the mental model lying within his reason and shapes the object according to it. 

Regarding colors, for us, the surface of bodies is what we see. But Plato—and he speaks to me in the *Timaeus*—says that colors are "offshoots" <note>original: "ekmageion," here referring to the theory of visual emissions.</note>. He says they "flame out" from the underlying bodies and, being driven forth from the particles, they send "effluences" <note>original: "aporrhoias," a Platonic theory that objects emit tiny particles that travel to the eye.</note> to us. In the space between our sight and the objects, these interact with the "Forms" of the colors. They say that colors do not correspond to these Forms by total likeness, but by their difference. And just as water and earth existed before colors—

<column-break/>

—to the earth alone, because of its abundance, things appear pale or yellow to the sight. To those who have a yellow tint in the pupil <note>original: "kore," the "maiden" or pupil of the eye.</note>, things appear yellow; to those who have phlegm <note>One of the four bodily humors in ancient medicine.</note> around the eye, they appear whitish. Others see differently according to their different **temperament** <note>original: "krasin," the specific mixture of humors in an individual's body.</note>. +

-> **On Mixture and Blending** <-

**Mixture** <term>Mixis: A physical combination where the components remain distinct and can, in theory, be separated again, like grains of sand and salt.</term> occurs in bodies by "juxtaposition," as seen with oil and water. For when these are mixed into a whole, they only touch at their outermost surfaces; for example, the lower surface of the oil mingles with the upper surface of the water. **Blending** <term>Krasis: A more profound union where the ingredients are thoroughly merged and transformed into a new substance, like wine mixed into water.</term> occurs when the mixed things are dissolved and blended together, as with wine and water. These do not appear to be mixed merely in part so that they might be separated again; rather, when they are entirely blended, they are partitioned through one another, both cutting and being cut, and poured together. To us, this is a "mixture," but it is specifically called a "diffusion" and a "blending." They produce a natural, purifying, and clarifying effect. These things will be of the same kind when they are difficult to distinguish in their own places, <margin>155</margin> for they exist according to a common abundance. +

### On the Soul

The physical soul and the sensory soul are of the same kind. The mortal soul is entirely directed toward different forms relative to one another. Yet the powers of different animals are of the same kind when compared to the human; for instance, the sensory power of a horse compared to the sensory power of a human. For all the senses, having become specific parts, reflect the human character in its form. There are different "formative principles" <note>original: "logoi," the rational blueprints of creation.</note> for the creative works of Plato and for each specific form. Our soul and the souls of others differ from one another by being "prior" or "posterior," which is why they fall under a single genus.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Aristotelian and Platonic metaphysics, specifically transitioning from a classification of "homogeneous" and "heterogeneous" substances into a deeper investigation of the nature of the Soul (Psyche) and its relationship to the body and the Creator (Demiurge).</meta>

For such are the homogeneous things; but the different things share with the first, just as blood and flesh share in a common nature. For if all were of the same form, they would also be mixtures of all things; but if they are of different forms, each will be something else. And while the most specific form <term>Eidos: Here referring to the *infima species*, the most detailed level of classification that defines a thing's essence.</term> reveals existence to all, the other kind <unclear>makes room</unclear> in nature alongside the form. Thus, the particular reason, being contained by the form, is distinct from the form itself.

## On the Soul

We observe that the passions <term>Pathe: In this context, "passions" refers to anything that happens to or is felt by the soul, including emotions and sensory perceptions.</term> of the soul preserve the body, though they are not themselves sensed; for these [passions] are separable from the body. While the soul gives form to the living body, we see the soul only as it is already "colored" and moving the instrument of that same living body. Some passions merely follow the body, yet those which involve the imagination <note>original: "phantasia"</note> after much labor are activities <note>original: "energeia," or actualized work</note> rather than passive affections. Other things, such as anger or mildness and the like, must be able to use the body as an instrument; these are passions of the vital spirit when it is moved. It is like a movement of the soul acting upon a suffering subject. If it settles and remains within these activities, we call it a passion in a different way—it shares in the state of the body through such an affection, for there will be changes. 

However, when the melancholic are roused to fears without anything external happening, we say this does not arise from the cause of the body. Every disposition of the passions follows this rule: the soul alone, in its perfect state, does not follow the impulse [of the body] regarding its own striving for purification <note>original: "katharsis," the process of cleansing the soul from bodily influence</note>, in a manner analogous to how the body is constituted. On account of this purification, souls are as if shut up in the body like a prison; but when they recover their own power and their

<column-break/>

ancient dignity, they have no need to be moved by labor. Nor, indeed, are they concerned with the body and things external to the body. Therefore, when you turn back into yourself, look upon the soul living according to its nature. That soul which does not use such a life is weak and full of vice. For to be in need, as if of irrational things, and to honor something merely because it is honorable rather than because it is necessary, is the mark of a lesser soul. 

The soul that is immediately superior begins its investigation of our causes from a hypothesis. At the third level, it treats the "muses" <note>The text here likely refers to levels of intellectual or divine inspiration, often called "mousis" in late Platonic thought.</note> which are not called so by God, but which the soul fashions for itself through its own finishing power. At the fourth level is "fate" <note>original: "moira"</note> and the contemplation of beings in a quantitative way. At the fifth is the "over-soul," which is called "divinely inspired" <note>original: "entheos"</note>, where my own self gladly communes with what is its own.

## Whether our soul is a first principle

<margin>none 15 3 21</margin>
The soul is no longer a principle <note>original: "arche," meaning a beginning, source, or ruling principle.</note> in and of itself; nor is it a principle by deficiency, as if it existed before all others without any other cause. For its first cause is linked in a chain, and this is clear to all as the **creative principle** <note>The Demiurge or Creator.</note>. This principle preserves the distance between all things. This is its procession <term>Proodos: A Neoplatonic term for the way reality flows out from the divine Source into the world.</term> and even before its name, it is more fully participated in by the Creator into the first "marbles" <note>Likely a metaphor for the fundamental building blocks of reality or the first emanations.</note>. The Greeks discuss this in various ways—as they do regarding teeth or other things—concerning why its permanence is surrendered to the perfection of the result. Therefore, He who demonstrated that the whole universe is perfect brings all things forth together, yet provides for each according to its own proper rank. Some things come from themselves, and others from the Creator. The soul participates from the Creator in a likeness to life. Thus, while there is soul in all things, it is more useless to those things that are partial...

<summary>This page explores the relationship between the soul and the body, defining the soul as the form-giving principle that can either be weighed down by bodily passions or elevated through purification. It also discusses the soul's place in the cosmic hierarchy, clarifying that it is not the ultimate first principle but an emanation from the Creator (Demiurge).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s investigation into the metaphysical components of the human being, moving from the general definition of "forms" to the specific distinctions between the soul's powers and its relationship with the biological body and nature.</meta>

# Precision regarding the powers of the soul:
Most philosophers simply name two powers of the soul; however, one must introduce a sub-division, so that the parts of the soul are understood as certain portions, and not merely as powers of the soul. Some act as a "part," while others act as a "power." For example, Aristotle <note>Psellos often critiques or refines Aristotle’s "De Anima" using a Neoplatonic lens.</note> does not hold that the superior part of the soul—its very essence—is defined by its "well-living." Others define the powers of the soul as its matter and its internal arrangements. Some see only the "part," while others see such dispositions; like specific groups, these transform the higher forms of life. Some are distinguished by their forms, while others are honored for their beauty. Iamblichus the philosopher <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a hugely influential Neoplatonist who integrated theurgic rituals and complex hierarchies into the study of the soul.</note> held this regarding the person: that according to the rational soul, there is either "being" or "intellection" <note>original: "noein," the act of intuitive thinking</note>. But regarding the parts of the whole, there are two following types: some belong to the soul itself in this way, while others exist within the elements, and still others in the all-containing body.

# What is Nature:
Nature is a power, invisible to the eyes, yet how it provides for bodies, speaking through a harmonious arrangement. It is the principle of motion and rest; for it is the cause of motion in natural bodies and the cause of rest. For all bodies—such as the elements and the things composed from them—whether they possess motion or rest by force, they are moved appropriately by nature. For that which moves bodies is much greater; first are the heavenly things, and then the souls. But there are more things within natures, acting as an instrument or as something belonging to God. For the part through which sensation is not moved, the soul exists through nature; but the bodies of which...

<column-break/>

...simply and the same parts of nature are established; it is moved with a changing motion.

# What is the separation of the dormant part of the soul from the body:
The non-homogeneous part <term>Anhomoiomeres: A term used in ancient biology for parts made of different substances, like a hand or an eye, as opposed to "homogeneous" parts like skin or bone.</term> which comes from the soul acts like the superior and containing powers. But those which extend from the body—such as the experience of shivering or some other such thing—the soul also participates in. From this, the soul provides for what the body lacks. Again, some experiences are essential "passions" <note>original: "pathemata," things experienced or felt by the soul</note> of the soul, such as blushing, because the soul feels something shameful. Others belong to the soul because of the body, such as pleasures and pains; these happen to the soul through the mediation of the body. The things of the "dormant" part differ equally as if by place. For one remains in a place, its own in the body, but the other moves according to motion; the soul is the measurer <note>original: "merteios," likely a corruption of a term for "measuring" or "proportional," referring to the soul's role in governing the body's dimensions.</note>.

<margin>As and how</margin>
# How the soul is in the fetus <note>original: "kyemati," the developing embryo or fetus</note> and not in the body: Theology according to Gregory.
Regarding the removal of this part; you would not easily find it, as this view and that are both attached to opinion. But the divine Gregory the Mystic <note>Likely St. Gregory of Nyssa, whose work "On the Making of Man" argued that the soul and body are created simultaneously, not one before the other.</note>, who voiced this confession in his wisdom many times, says that the soul is neither older than the body, nor is it added later by some false rewriting of divine plan. Rather, at the sowing of the seed, the body is prepared for the reception of the soul, with the soul subsisting at that very moment. According to the "Greek" view <note>Referring to the pagan Platonic belief in the pre-existence of souls.</note>, perfection comes through the natural instrument and the birth of the child; they attribute these things to the body... for when the embryo is rooted from within.

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s investigation into the metaphysical components of the human being, moving from the general definition of "forms" to the specific distinctions between the soul's powers and its relationship with the biological body and nature.</meta>

...they teach this about the physical soul; from which they defined it as mortal, while the intellectual soul they defined as vital and immortal.

The soul judges the things pertaining to the body; just as light from the sun falls upon a person. For this illumination ignites the whole body into a living being and gives it life, whenever it is well-disposed toward this innate quality—much like the breezes that hang around the heavens; even if they happen to be far away, they do not move. And what is called the ether receives the power of fire; for just as a portion is formed from air and fire, so also the body of a human is illuminated by the soul. And no one who possesses a life without a share in the soul could even conceive of these illuminations or participations; but even if nothingness participates in that which is Being, then non-being—just as it might be present to us "colorlessly" relative to Being—so too does the soul be present to the body colorlessly <note>original: "achrōmōs," meaning immaterially or without physical qualities, much like how light lacks a solid body.</note>.

## On Choice and Attention: ##

Since these two belong to the soul—attention and choice—choice <note>original: "proairesis," often translated as "moral choice" or "will."</note> is concerned with the works of actions, and it judges what is beautiful through reason; and through desire, it reaches for the particular good of each. Choice is composed of those things that fluctuate between reason and desire, fulfilling them both simultaneously. Attention <note>original: "prosoche," a state of mindfulness or spiritual alertness central to Byzantine philosophy.</note> is that by which we attend to the works we do and the words we say; for sometimes it observes the habits <gloss>of this</gloss> soul—what it does and how

<column-break/>

it relates to them; and sometimes it contemplates what the living being does, what it transgresses, and what it lacks. These are also intermediate exercises of learning; for they are practices of the most beautiful virtues. They are called "intermediate" because they lie between right reason and the world of opinion.

## On the Virtues: ##

There are three ranks of virtues: some of them adorn the individual—that is, the soul's transition from the body; others purify the soul from the body and turn it back toward itself, which are called "purificatory" <note>original: "kathartikai." In Neoplatonic thought, these virtues cleanse the soul of material passions.</note>; and others, once the soul is perfectly purified, occupy it with the contemplation of beings, which are understandably called "theoretical" <note>original: "theōrētikai," from "theoria," meaning spiritual or intellectual vision.</note>. And these virtues follow one another, though not always reciprocally. For just as their essences differ, so do the virtues. And the soul is properly called the "rational" soul; that which is above the soul is "Intellect" <note>original: "nous," the highest form of intuitive knowledge.</note>; that which is after the rational soul is the "irrational soul" and the "image of the soul." Every man who possesses virtues in the body finds that virtue is not silence; beginning from the spheres above, whenever they are established in virtues, they possess every perfection.

## On the Virtues: ##

There are grades of virtues for me: among which we find first the physical virtue or that which is in the character; second, the moral virtue <note>original: "ethike."</note> which is the middle state for the uneducated; third, the political virtue <note>original: "politike," the social and civic virtues.</note>, which is governed by reason and wisdom; fourth, the purificatory virtue which cleanses the soul from every passion; fifth, the theoretical virtue which contemplates the Intellect; and sixth, the deific virtue <note>original: "theotike," the highest stage where the soul becomes god-like or attains "theosis."</note>.

soul, virtue, choice, attention, intellectual, theoretical, political, purificatory, moral, deific

<summary>Psellos describes the soul's illuminating relationship with the body and categorizes the mental faculties of choice and attention. He concludes with a hierarchical ladder of virtues, ranging from basic physical traits to the highest state of deification.</summary>
<keywords>psychology, Neoplatonism, proairesis, prosoche, hierarchy of virtues, theosis, rational soul, Michael Psellos</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s discussion of the soul's higher faculties, transitioning from the nature of "attention" and "choice" to the hierarchy of virtues that allow the soul to return to its divine source.</meta>

...or the most divine part of the soul; a portion of the indivisible <note>original: "amereistou." In Neoplatonism, this refers to the higher part of the soul that remains "above" and never fully descends into the material body.</note>; and the soul living in God himself; and being born in the divine form. There is an <unclear>obscurity</unclear>; but there are also other virtues beyond these, belonging to the souls themselves, acting like archetypes <note>original: "paradeigmata," or blueprints/models in the mind of God.</note> for the remaining parts. These are beyond every individual portion; they act as counselors to the universal revolutions of the heavens and are not divided into parts, but exist as "portions" that are super-substantial <note>original: "hyperousioi," meaning they exist beyond the level of ordinary being or essence.</note>.

## On the Topic of Virtues

<margin>These are the virtues more perfect than the human: holiness.</margin>
Or the portion of them directed toward God, being like the portions of the life of the gods. Second are those below, which exercise providence <note>original: "pronoian," the divine care for the lower world</note> over all things that are distinct. Prudence <note>original: "phronesis"</note> is the portion directed toward our own life, which wills the whole. For in holiness, there is not only freedom from deception before God, but also a state of seeing nothing that is harmful or unjust. Knowledge is the portion of the Ineffable <note>original: "arrhētou," that which cannot be spoken</note>, as is prudence. One side is brought in by the ethical virtues, having been saved. Yet these do not belong to the parts of prudence alone; but before it was partitioned into us, it existed through the divinity of virtue in all these things. For if virtue is both super-substantial and substantial—and super-cosmic, if it is a world—it arrives in the portions of the soul without harshness. These are the sources of the virtues. We take all these fragments when one speaks of our "indivisible division," meaning the most perfect virtue is a portion of the indivisible.

## Further on the Virtues

Of the political virtues <note>original: "politikon areton." These are not "politics" in the modern sense, but "civic virtues" that allow a person to live well as a citizen among others.</note>, which only bring order to the human portion, the goal is to not suffer passively.

<column-break/>

These are the virtues that strive for community and doing no harm to <insert><unclear>the</unclear></insert> neighbor. Because of this social exclusion of harm and the act of sharing, they are called "political." But of the theoretical <note>original: "theoretikon," meaning contemplative or intellectual</note> virtues, the goal is likeness to God. Temperance is viewed in one way in the context of prudence, and courage and justice are viewed one way in the political virtues, but differently in the theoretical ones. For according to the measure of the underlying subjects, the powers of the virtues are also named. From this, they are defined; from this, they distinguish the greater virtues and the lesser. Often, those who possess the greater virtues also possess the particular, smaller virtues in a partial way, but not vice versa. Those who have the lesser virtues do not necessarily have the greater, unless they are moving forward by spiritual progress <note>original: "prokopēn," a technical term for advancement in the philosophical life</note>.

## What Pertains to the Virtues

Likeness to God is not one and the same across every virtue; rather, it is diverse. For we are made like Him through the political virtues, but first only as if in a shifting shadow. We are made like Him through the purificatory virtues <note>original: "kathartikas," the virtues used to "cleanse" the soul from bodily passions</note>, but only as if from a certain impression. We are made like Him through the theoretical virtues, but as if from a delightful radiance. Finally, we are made like Him through the theurgic virtues <note>original: "theourgikas," meaning "god-working." These are the highest rituals or states of being where the soul acts in union with the divine</note>. This is the most perfect likeness. For the soul to be purified in contemplation is one thing, but we are not made like God through that alone—for He is beyond the need for purification or contemplation. But the ability to "deify" <note>original: "theopoiein," to make divine or participate in the divine nature</note>, providing a starting point for material virtue and releasing one from the passions—so that the soul itself may be made perfect and the goal of deification be set—that is the most perfect likeness. For us, the final stage is the divine God.

<summary>Michael Psellos describes a hierarchy of virtues ranging from civic "political" virtues to the "theurgic" virtues that achieve deification. He explains that the ultimate goal of the soul is "likeness to God," which is achieved in stages of increasing spiritual purity and intellectual contemplation.</summary>
<keywords>virtues, Neoplatonism, deification, theurgy, political virtues, purificatory virtues, theoretical virtues, Michael Psellos, soul, likeness to God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s synthesis of Neoplatonic philosophy, likely drawing from Porphyry’s "Sententiae." The previous page discussed the soul's "attention" and "choice" as it begins its ascent; here, the text transitions into a formal hierarchy of virtues—the civic, the purificatory, and the theoretical—and the nature of the higher Intellect.</meta>

...deemed worthy of messengers, as in no way shameful, which he has placed toward the truest **love of God** as an assimilation; but let us love to be at rest and to adorn ourselves through the **civic virtues** <note>original: "politikon areton." These are the practical social and moral virtues like justice and temperance used in daily life.</note> of which we are capable.

<margin>On the Topic of Virtues</margin>
For him, essence is solely "being," because of the absence of all form and the essence of that which is; but perfection through that which is formed comes from God. For as you are of the First Being, so you are not of the first good of the same. For rather, essences and virtue are from this; but only essence according to the **progression** <note>original: "proodon." In Neoplatonism, this is the process of the One overflowing or "proceeding" into the lower levels of reality.</note> from it, which is present to all from God. But perfection is according to the **return** <note>original: "epistrophen." This is the soul's movement back toward its divine source after its descent into the material world.</note> to it. Nor does he demand to be perfected, for he alone is, unless he is a beginning toward the earthly. Therefore, just like one painting from a model, or otherwise taking the prescribed **archetype** <note>original: "archetypian," a primary pattern or blueprint in the divine mind.</note> for the imitation, if he did not keep it with him to record it, so too he who would sketch and color virtue within himself could not otherwise be assimilated to God through the divine, unless he had used that alone to record it. And because of this, my own self will be accurately fashioned after the prototype through the contemplation of the Reason and the model.

### On Virtues

One who possesses the **theoretical virtues** <note>original: "theoretikas aretas." These are virtues of pure contemplation and intellectual vision.</note> is called "God-fathered" <note>original: "theopator." A technical term for the highest level of Neoplatonic virtue where the soul becomes like the source of the divine.</note>. One who has the **purificatory virtues** <note>original: "kathartikas." Virtues that "cleanse" the soul of material attachments.</note> is called "daemonic" <note>original: "daimonios." This does not mean "evil," but refers to a spiritual or "spirit-like" being that bridges the gap between humans and gods.</note>. One who has the **civic virtues** is called "excellent" <note>original: "spoudaios." A term for the person who performs their human duties and social roles with moral excellence.</note>. He who is beloved of God is called "God-fathered," because the soul's state is toward what is called "divine" through theoretical virtue. It is necessary

<column-break/>

therefore, that which is darkened must be assimilated to God, changing from the "excellent" man into the "daemonic." If it becomes divine—which is again "God-fathered"—the one among us who is only "excellent" might at some point also change into vice. But perhaps the divine man is not [subject to change], namely the theoretical man. He who is beloved of God, the "God-fathered," who is seen to be entirely obedient to the divine, might at some point be transferred to the innate, being the very form of virtue from himself, and having become divine in color.

<margin>On Intellect</margin>
### On Intellect

Every **Intellect** <note>original: "nous." In this context, it refers to the higher, divine Mind that perceives truth directly and intuitively, rather than through step-by-step logic.</note> holds its essence, its power, and its activity as eternal. For its whole essence is present all at once. It neither increases nor decreases; nor was it not present yesterday. Its essence alone is similar to itself; for it thinks the whole and the all as a complete whole together. It neither sees the past nor knows the future as something yet to be, but knows everything as it is in the "now." It is not moved, nor is it embellished, nor does it reason through syllogisms <note>original: "syllogizomenos." The Intellect does not need to argue from point A to point B; it simply "sees" the truth.</note>—which is like an echo where the soul reaches a conclusion by placing perceptions. Instead, all knowing is found in its very being. For being unmoved, it could not be measured by time, neither in its essence, which is in its **indivisibility** <note>original: "amereian." A state of being that cannot be split into parts.</note>, from which its essence is unmoved. But the intermediate and middle of these is like an eternal power that has been allotted its substance.

<margin>On Number</margin>
### On Number

Numbers are an essence, indivisible. For that which has no parts is not of the same body. That which is unmoved is entirely **indivisible**. Everything that is divided is divided either by magnitude or by quantity. But if it is eternal in every respect and I assume a certain body, it is therefore indivisible. From where then is essence...

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s exploration of the Neoplatonic hierarchy of being. Having established the role of virtues and archetypes on the previous page, the text now moves into a technical distinction between the body and the mind, specifically focusing on the "Intellect" (Nous) and the "Soul" (Psyche) and their respective capacities for self-knowledge and divine participation.</meta>

Similarly, regarding the body, it is clear that the soul thinks of itself and **returns to itself** <note>original: "pros heauto epistrephei." In Neoplatonic thought, this "return" or "reversion" is the act of a conscious being looking inward to its own source rather than outward at material things.</note>. For no body thinks of itself. How is this evident? Because its thinking is not divided, whereas everything in a physical place is divisible. For this reason, those who study these matters do not consider them material. Since every individual substance possesses a share of the **immaterial intellects** <note>original: "nois aulois." These are "mind-beings" that exist without physical matter.</note> which are unparticipated. Nor is it fragmented. For those things which subsist for the sake of the world remain **unchanging**; and these are distinct from those things which change. And it was necessary that form not be the same as that which is below it, but rather that it transcend it. The entire order of things there does not descend from its own rank; its unique character merely proceeds forward, while remaining in its own forms and identities. Some things exist according to their nature, others according to their connections.

### + On the Intellect +

Because the **unparticipated intellect** <note>original: "amathekton nous." This refers to a "pure" Mind that exists independently and is not "shared out" or diluted by being joined to a physical body.</note>, and that which is unparticipated in the primary form, holds itself within itself. It possesses the measure of its own essence, both in its primary aspects and in those that transcend it. Hence, even the unparticipated intellect thinks in a "formal" way. For even if it is unparticipated, a certain manifestation is given to those things that come after it from its own existence. And it thinks of the **soul** and the **forms of nature**. It does not think of them merely as they exist in nature, for it does not know lower things in a lower way, nor higher things in a higher way; rather, it knows both "formally" (according to their ideal patterns). It does not change along with the things it thinks about. For it has the power to look toward the better, and it wills to look toward the lesser. Therefore, as its nature is, so it thinks both of itself and of the things after it. It does not possess things as they are in their changing states, but rather as their causes, knowing even the lower things through their higher forms.

<column-break/>

<margin>ai</margin>
...in a sensory formation. 

### + On the Soul +

Every soul is divine. One type is **self-complete** <note>original: "autotelos," meaning it is perfect in itself and doesn't need a body to function.</note>, while another is directed by the Intellect <margin>o</margin> toward a diversity of things that always remain, specifically the divine souls. Every divine soul acts in a "soul-like" manner. Every soul that is formed we hold to be eternally moving. Every change is a movement of the soul. Every soul is, in part, an essence that exists apart from the body, **unchanging and imperishable**. It is a **middle life** <note>original: "zoē mesē." Neoplatonists viewed the Soul as the "horizon" or bridge between the eternal, indivisible divine realm and the temporary, divisible material realm.</note> between the indivisible beings and those beings divided among bodies. Every soul that can be participated in also possesses an eternal activity, making use of what is immediately above it and being made like that which is primary. From all these things, souls exist as the **paradigms** (models) of the essences and the living substances mentioned.

### + On the Soul +

Concerning the "passions" (affections) of the soul: the highest part of the soul is united only with the **intelligible** <note>original: "noētois," the realm of pure thought/forms.</note> and is capable of "seeing" them. The lowest part of the soul communicates with **sensible** (physical) things, known through the senses and through opinions. The middle part is **discursive reason** <term>Dianoia: the type of logic that moves step-by-step, like a mathematician, rather than the instant "seeing" of the higher Intellect.</term>. Up to this point, it is similar; reasons and opinions belong to the "living being" (the human). For this multi-faceted nature of ours consists of divine portions. For **desire** is a "multi-headed beast." **Spirit** (or anger) is a "lion-like crowd." But the **true part** of us is the Intellect and something even more divine within us. The truest part of us is the friend of the **uniform** <note>original: "monoeidous," meaning simple, single, or monadic, as opposed to the complex and messy material world.</note>, which gazes upon sacred things.

It concludes with the Platonic tripartite division of the soul using the imagery of the beast, the lion, and the divine intellect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s exploration of the Neoplatonic hierarchy of being. ...therefore, the body is a triple thing, homogeneous and of a similar kind; this part is rational, tripartite, and triple-bodied; this part is sensory, multiform, and diverse. But that which reasons and contemplates is the middle portion, [like] myrrh <note>original: "smornen." This is likely a scribal corruption or a rare metaphorical use comparing the soul's pervasive nature to the scent of myrrh.</note>, this very soul.

### On Mind and Soul

We possess our forms within the soul. For example, the unparticipated <gloss>u</gloss> <note>original: "ameilikton." In this context, it likely refers to the "unparticipated" (amathekton) Intellect—the pure, divine Mind that does not descend into the material world.</note> forms are separated from those where the "all together" belongs to the parts. One part is taught toward the lower things more subordinately by the illuminations; another might call it a "portion," not by power but by the instructions. For if the soul were something, it would not only be from itself, but also from the Mind and from God, striving toward the **salvation of the gods**; and it is the bond through which the senses [operate]. Similarly, from those things which are neither participated in nor called "lesser," they say [it is] not a flow. We possess this **Mind** <term>Nous: In Neoplatonism, this is the "Intellect" or divine mind, the second level of reality after "the One."</term>, both as a common thing and as a whole. As a common thing <gloss>the</gloss>, we are divided into portions; but as a whole, insofar as it possesses every direction and the psychic forms as parts, it completes our essence. These portions perfect and adorn us, and the parts are joined. By these psychic means, we have a mixture, and we have the Mind as a vehicle for the state of the portion.

### On the Soul

The soul, while embodying itself, uses the body as an instrument <gloss>the</gloss> <note>original: "porio chretai." This reflects the Platonic view that the soul is a distinct entity that "uses" the physical body like a tool or a garment.</note>, yet it remains separate in itself. But just as fire is not [divided into] small coins, it does not simply inherit the passions of the body. For if it were separate from the body, it would either be mixed with it or interwoven somehow as a form, something "in-matter." But even if it is mixed, the soul is not the one that suffers or experiences passion, but rather the body [suffers] from the mixture <gloss>u</gloss>. If it is interwoven, the soul is able to remain impassive <note>The term "impassive" (apathes) means the soul itself is not harmed or changed by physical sensations or pains.</note> by means of the Mind; it does not suffer poorly even when [the body] suffers—just as light

<column-break/>

is moved [when] the air changes. If we were to lose the form, both would cease to be. The part of existence <gloss>the</gloss> belongs [to the whole], but it is not the soul itself. For even in the case of the **ax**, the soul is not the shape <note>This is a reference to Aristotle’s famous analogy in "De Anima": if an ax were a living thing, "ax-ness" (the ability to cut) would be its soul, but the soul is the function of the whole, not just the physical shape.</note> that is formed; it is not merely the "cutting" part, but the whole. But the Mind, having been given, is what gives it shape. If the soul were again a material form, it would be impossible for it to be receptive to such passions and knowledge. But it is the nature of incorporeal things to receive [these]; sensations are certain passions, but the soul does not grow weary of sensation.

### ON THE SOUL

Grasping the nature of the soul is not easy, since every partial habit is also a "grasping." For the source of every completion is to wish to receive even the difference. It is disputed whether the soul is a genus, a substance, a quantity, or a quality. For some say it is [a substance]; others say it is a quality, such as a **harmony** or a **mixture** <note>The "Soul as Harmony" theory suggests the soul is just the correct "tuning" of the body's elements, a view Psellos and other Platonists generally argued against.</note>. **Democritus** <term>Democritus: A pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed that everything is made of atoms; he believed the soul consisted of smooth, round "fire-atoms."</term> named it "fire," so that it might be mobile. But we do not see it purely. For the perfect grasp of it is not divided <margin>not divided</margin>, but by some lower means, we perceive it as a more subtle grasp <gloss>it</gloss> and not purely. It is indivisible. This is shown by its parts and its movement toward the Mind—this is its exit. It is worth investigating whether it is of a single form, and why souls [differ] through the manner of their mixing. For if the addition and the mixture are equal, then the mixture is, paradoxically, [the same].

### On Generation and Corruption

The soul is defiled by the partial things through the change of the "in-flow." One part is changed through honor. Our movement is blessed insofar as it does not remain around the world—that is, under [the influence of] movement. **Corruption** <term>Phthora: The process of breaking down or passing out of existence, the opposite of "Genesis" (coming into being).</term> occurs when one is not [led] from the earth toward the...
