The <unclear>first</unclear> and <unclear>inferior</unclear> of all our <unclear>knowledge</unclear> is both that of <unclear>providence</unclear> and that concerning the imagination of God; for God is to us above <unclear>all</unclear> <unclear>passions</unclear> within us, in a <unclear>threefold</unclear> way according to what is possible. And the Lord, in <unclear>using</unclear> them in the <unclear>solitude</unclear>, <unclear>adapts</unclear> Himself to the terrible things of men, but in types of knowledge, [it is] the unforming of the parable materially and partially, and also in the <unclear>hypotheses</unclear> of nature. In this soul, there is imagination coupled with sense, and with the mind <term>Nous: In Neoplatonism, this is the "Intellect" or spiritual intuition, higher than discursive reason.</term> things are imagined as <unclear>lacking</unclear> the laws.

->+ How we always participate in God while He is always active +<-

For it is not only that God’s being is Good and beyond-essence <note>original: "hyperousios." A term from Pseudo-Dionysius describing God as being "above" the very category of existence or being.</note> for our sake alongside <unclear>eternity</unclear>; it <unclear>precedes</unclear> all. For just as the sun shines upon the midday regions, it holds all things in their respective places within itself, yet the same things appear [differently] to our turned eyes. In the same way, the Divine gives a likeness and He always provides the light beyond-the-sun <note>original: "hyperelion." ...[to be] harmonious and in no way subject to corruption. And often that which—by His purification, <unclear>having received</unclear> its lot before punishment by divine providence—is not free; it floats upon the calling-back of the body. And hope is the only thing to restore [it], <unclear>partially</unclear> <unclear>because</unclear> <unclear>it</unclear> <unclear>is</unclear> <unclear>mixed</unclear> with them.

->+ Why Evil exists at all +<-

<margin>How evil does not exist essentially, but only in the departure from the good.</margin>
As with darkness, it is a lack of substance and a privation <term>Steresis: The philosophical concept that evil has no independent existence but is merely the absence or "privation" of good.</term> of light. The philosopher Plato in the *Theaetetus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the nature of knowledge.</note> made this especially clear, saying that evil itself is not the perfection of <unclear>spirit</unclear>—as if one were to speak of it as a fate. Listen: if all things in the world were perfectly incorruptible, then corruptible things would never have been handed down. If, therefore, things contrary to nature exist among the corruptible, so too does evil exist in our souls. We bear witness to this among the gods: from this [comes] every perfection. This clearly preserves the world through Providence; but where do we place this Providence to be [found in] evil? Providence makes all things perfectly into the composition of the world.

->+ On how the Divine effects the Return +<-

<margin>Concerning the Providence of God</margin>
Following us from non-being into being, [Providence] is brought forth; to some without form, as in inanimate bodies; to others according to life and of-the-same-essence <note>original: "homoousiotetos." While usually a Trinitarian term, Psellos uses it here to describe the soul's ontological kinship with the divine life.</note>, namely our souls. Each was brought forth by Goodness, and Matter itself provided for the vital [aspect]. That which makes things out of them and from spiritual things: each exists just as it proceeded. Furthermore, the philosopher Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the last great head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens; Psellos frequently synthesized his work with Christianity.</note> clarifies this in his *Theological Chapters*; and before him, the once-holy Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a mysterious 5th-century author whose writings blended Neoplatonism with Christian theology.</note> distinguishes this even more clearly.

<meta>This page transitions from Psellos’s metaphysical discussion of the soul’s knowledge to the physical mechanics of the human body. Having established the soul’s superiority, he now provides a natural-philosophical explanation of how that soul interacts with the world through the senses and how the human form is biologicaly reproduced, following the medical traditions of Galen and Aristotle.</meta>

### And a chapter of things studied with great care for their truth,
### the most accurate account: On the Senses: +

<margin><unclear>... ...</unclear></margin>
Vision occurs when the light emitted from the eyes <note>original: "phos ekpheromenon." This refers to the ancient "extramission theory," where the eyes were thought to send out rays of light to "touch" objects.</note> grows together with the light flowing from physical bodies. We might imagine this as a living thing, though perhaps it is not an actual entity. We hear by means of the void <note>original: "keno." Ancient philosophers believed the ear contained a "built-in" pocket of air that vibrated in sympathy with the outside world.</note>, through the impact of the spirit <note>original: "pneumatos." In this context, it refers to the vital breath or air that carries sound.</note> rushing down through the echo. We smell when the spirit mixes with the air in the head, as the vapor is drawn up from the nose; these we categorize accordingly. Taste occurs through the parts near the root of the tongue and the soft tissues of the palate, as the flavors are carried from the body into the veins. Touch occurs before the spirit is mixed through the body, being distributed not just in part but throughout; we perceive through the contact of bodies because of the arrangement of the particles on the surface of the body, through which we recognize external sensations.

### How conceptions occur:

<margin><unclear>... ...</unclear></margin>
The conception of living beings occurs most successfully when the womb <note>original: "metra." In Byzantine medicine, the health and "rhythm" of the womb were considered central to successful pregnancy.</note> is fit, rhythmic, and well-proportioned for the reception of the seed, and when the menstrual discharge <note>original: "katamenion."</note> is composed of a certain portion of pure blood from the body's mass. Females also contribute to the pregnancy through the moistening of the womb—which is a kind of mixing. This moisture serves as the "matter" <note>original: "hyle." A reference to the Aristotelian idea that the mother provides the material for the body while the father provides the "form" or "impulse."</note> for the sowing of the male impulse. The male seed, being turned toward the extremities of the body, is sufficient for the task; it is contained within the veins and set in motion. The female contributes a similar cause, but toward the more moist

<column-break/>

parts of the body, such as the blood or the humors and both types of bile; rather, it occurs through an exchange from both parents. For the male possesses more of <unclear>these things</unclear> than the female. +

### How males and females are formed:

<margin>16</margin>
Just as with the testicles, where one is on the right and this other on the left, the right is notably warmer while the left is less so. In the same way, the womb has two chambers <note>original: "kolpous." Byzantine anatomical theory often followed the idea that the womb was "double-chambered."</note> for the reception of the male seed: one on the right and one on the left. Since the male is warmer than the female, if the seed should fall into the right chamber, a male is formed because of the heat; if it falls into the left, a female is formed because of the coldness. The locations are the same, yet if a female is formed in the right chamber while the seed was male, or if hair grows in that place, it is because when the seed fell into the left, it was <unclear>perhaps</unclear> deposited there; a female is older <note>This likely refers to the "strength" or "maturity" of the genetic material.</note> than necessary and is found to be softer. Such are the transformations of men and women. +

### Why many women are not able to conceive:

<margin>13</margin>
A woman may not conceive during intercourse for many reasons: when she is warmer than is necessary, or colder, or too moist; or when her internal parts are paralyzed; or when she has calluses or fleshy growths <note>original: "sarkoseis." Likely a reference to fibroids or polyps.</note>. Or, if the menstrual flow is too thin or too sparse, or because of a poor mixture of the male seed, she cannot conceive. It may also be due to an asymmetry of the male member, or an abundance of seed that immediately overwhelms the space, or by...

## Why twins or triplets are born: +

Regarding the birth of twins and triplets, many causes shall be enumerated; both the pouring out of the seed that occurs in the womb <note>original: "hystera."</note>, and the perforated structure of the uterus <note>original: "metras diatreton oikodomian." Medieval medicine often conceptualized the womb as having multiple "cells" or chambers that could catch different portions of seed.</note>. For those who manage the chambers <note>original: "kolpous." Literally "folds" or "pockets."</note> of the seed have already understood this. Twins or triplets, or even more; and the abundance of the seed is a contributing cause of many offspring. For the perfect God <note>Psellos often reconciles natural philosophy with theology, attributing the biological capacity of the organs to divine design.</note> did not only make the belly a certain size, but this part, the womb, has many chambers; and the cavity within you comes from it; the one divided in the middle. At once, the seeds which have been contained are harvested as twins; at the same time, he himself attributes the cause of "super-conceptions" <note>original: "episyllēpseis." Known in modern medicine as superfetation, the rare occurrence of a second conception during an ongoing pregnancy.</note> to the remnants, as is the case with such animals. And one might suggest other causes, which he himself discovers, following the ancients: +

## From where the resemblances to parents are imitated. And why they are like the father: +

The likeness comes from the secreted seed, either from the man or from the woman. That which comes from the man possesses the patterns <note>original: "logous." In this context, the formative "reasons" or "blueprints" that determine shape.</note> of the all-consuming doors; fixed toward the form. And likewise from a part; whenever, therefore, both seeds are mixed together—

<column-break/>

—it is under that <unclear>place</unclear>. It holds for those being imitated a resemblance equal to either the male or the female. Whenever the male dominates the female, the children are completed in the likeness of the father; but whenever the female dominates the male, they are made in the likeness of the mother. But whenever the matter of the seed is dissipated through evaporation, it is modeled after neither of the parents and no resemblance is formed. Many times, I also see that the woman’s act of bearing is influenced by the imagination <note>original: "phantasian." A common ancient and medieval belief held that the mental images a woman had during conception or pregnancy could physically shape the child's appearance.</note> of one or the other; the one being born is made to resemble the thing imagined: +

## Whether the embryo is also a living being and how it is nourished: +

Regarding the question we are participating in, this inquiry:
<margin>*</margin> of Hippocrates, Porphyry, and Galen. Before <note>The text likely means "Aristotle" or "the time of Hippocrates."</note>, everyone was in agreement; they say it is a living being <note>original: "zōon."</note> within the belly and is moved by a soul <note>original: "psyches."</note>, not a matter of great account. But these men say the rational part <note>original: "logikēn."</note> is nourished in the womb through the body. For if it were not this way, some [would say] it is like a sea of things through which it is nourished. But Porphyry, having demonstrated this with many arguments, says it is not a living being, nor is the embryo a living being nor does it participate in a soul; but rather like a plant <note>original: "phyton." This refers to the "vegetative soul" theory, where the fetus has life but not independent sensation or movement until birth.</note>, it has been planted and is moved. It is moved not by a soul, but by nature <note>original: "physeōs."</note>, just as trees and plants are. It is moved from that source, and is nourished not through the mouth but through the place whose name is <unclear>umbilicus</unclear>. But the warm one <note>original: "ho thermos." <meta>This page continues Psellos’s natural-philosophical examination of human biology. Following the previous page’s discussion on the mechanics of the senses and the initial process of conception, he now turns to the causes of dreams, the formation of the flesh, and a transition into celestial "circles" as an analogy for the body or as a broader study of natural order.</meta>

...to parts alone; while others [say they arise] from those things which were separated by the actions of the parts; others say they come from our imaginative power <gloss>from the four humors</gloss> <note>The four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—were believed to dictate health and temperament.</note> to the parts you ask; others [say they are] from the four humors which are transformed; they have the heat and the colors of things seen, together with the humors; others say they are their own memories of the arts and the achievements about which we possess the treatise; the philosophers philosophize about dreams; and the rhetoricians about rhetorical matters; and the geometricians again [about geometry]; there is therefore no single cause of dreams, but many, as has been said. <margin>on the ensouled flesh</margin> <insert>and the flesh</insert> comes from blood; from the <unclear>synthetic</unclear> <gloss>or of the <unclear>seed</unclear></gloss> heat; it cools from the watery <gloss>moisture</gloss> <gloss>or toward the</gloss> while these are divided; <gloss>for even from the other blood</gloss> most of our parts, through the strength of the elements in the body [form] the foundations of the diseases of the body. And through the abundance of food; and through indigestion, or corruption. But order and self-sufficiency [lead to health]; animals are shaped to be super-abundant upon certain stars, from the sixth or the thirtieth day; they are perfected in their parts which are made around the liver; Hippocrates says regarding males, because of being hot, the articulation of limbs happens faster, from the sixth or twentieth day; but for females, they are formed later, because of the roughness and through [lack of] haste. <note>original: "dia to trachy kai to dia spoudes." Psellos repeats the ancient view that male fetuses develop more quickly due to "innate heat," while females are "colder" and "moister."</note>

---

## Into how many circles is the blood divided? <note>This heading likely introduces a microcosm-macrocosm analogy where the "blood" or the body is mapped against the "circles" of the heavens. In Byzantine science, the same laws were thought to govern both the human body and the stars.</note>
The philosophers liken many circles because of the "Whole" in the first principles; blood is the equator, the middle of them cutting

<column-break/>

according to the choice of the northern and <gloss>southern</gloss> parts, and these parts follow, in this way only: the summer circle <note>The Tropic of Cancer.</note>; and on the left, the southern [circle], the arctic; they fashion another circle again, the meridians <gloss>those lying from East to West</gloss> and they are cut with the aforementioned circles at... they divide the morning part and the evening part of this; from the named horizons; <gloss>but the equator</gloss> and through it also the seventh, the zodiac, beginning from the summer <gloss>circle</gloss> cutting the equator and ending in the south. <margin>on the phenomena</margin> named the <term>Milky Way</term> <note>original: "galaxian." From the Greek "gala," meaning milk.</note> circle <term>Milky Way</term>. Whence then a comet <gloss>about the air itself</gloss>, its name is the Milky Way; about the halo; or the same things [apply to] the <term>Milky Way</term> or the comet; and that the Milky Way is in the part of the circle, unless many things from blood somehow—for blood happens, not a circle of it contributes—but according to Sagittarius and Gemini, for fire is moderate, <gloss>or the blood is a garment</gloss> from the commonness of those around the Milky Way and the comet... to these the most divine moon, the body of the purest blood, a solidifying smoky alteration and powerfully underlying <gloss>from the aether</gloss>; the swift-moving in the womb of the kindling from the aether and the breathing of thick parts of matter, for accordingly, it makes a comet; when it passes into a halo, it makes the Milky Way; it appears white; because those who raise the things under the circle of the spirit of the heat, whitish when... <margin>concerning the phenomena in the waters</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the multifaceted causes of dreams and the biological formation of the flesh and fetus according to Hippocratic theory. It then transitions into a cosmological analogy, comparing the "circles" of the body or blood to the celestial equator, zodiac, and the formation of the Milky Way and comets.</summary>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the biological focus of the previous page—which discussed human conception, dreams, and the humors—into the realm of physics and astronomy. Psellos shifts his attention to "meteors" (atmospheric phenomena) and the celestial mechanics of the sun and moon, moving from the "microcosm" of the human body to the "macrocosm" of the heavens.</meta>

...of various fires: of voices...
We have spoken of the kindling-layer <note>original: "ὑπέκκαυμα" (hypékkhauma). In Aristotelian physics, this is the highly flammable upper region of the atmosphere just below the sphere of the moon.</note>, and he said it does not create from the ether a scent or a judgment; those called "goats" <note>Aristotle used the term "goats" to describe flickering, leaping streaks of atmospheric fire that resemble the movement of goats.</note> were named so from the woolly tufts [of light] produced by them... if it produces from them a leaping of light along its length, they are named "torches" because of their shape. Others are called "runners," while Aristotle calls others "stars"—the fire from the ether falling upon some part of the smoky exhalation <note>original: "ἀναθυμιάσεως" (anathymiáseos). These are the dry, hot vapors rising from the earth which were thought to fuel celestial fires.</note>. And by spreading out, it can create a wide, marble-like glow. When the motion becomes slanted, it happens for two reasons: either it is pushed by the cold as it takes shape, or because everything that is thrown upwards also moves naturally. For fire must seek that which is without form. This part occurs because the smoke is carried as it rises. As it moves naturally upward and is simultaneously pressed down, a slanted motion occurs, such as when a spark is struck. Regarding the atmospheric phenomena of the sun, we proceed... <margin>41</margin> The observations of the sun according to the wise Aristarchus <note>Aristarchus of Samos (c. 310–230 BC), the astronomer famous for the first known model placing the sun at the center of the universe.</note> have a greater ratio. For in the... its diameter <term>diameter</term> compared to the diameter of the earth has a greater ratio than 12 to 5. And straightly, the diameter of the earth compared to [the sun], is greater than 18 to 8, and is also triple... it has a greater ratio than 140 to 5. We have set these things out in this way, though they are unproven and incomprehensible to many concerning the sequence of the... but indeed, in the realm of geometry and undeniable proofs... But for you, O most divine Em-

<column-break/>

-peror <note>Psellos is likely addressing his royal pupil, Michael VII Doukas.</note>, regarding the sun, these things are stated without proof. As for the proofs, they are of a higher nature. A solar <term>eclipse</term> is, strictly speaking, an eclipse of the sun; for the solar light itself never fails, but is rather obscured by a shadow. When the moon catches the sun at the ecliptic nodes <note>The points where the moon's orbit intersects the path of the sun.</note>, it guards its position and shadows the solar light from our eyes. This occurs when the body of the moon is within twelve digits <note>Ancient units used to measure the extent of an eclipse.</note>. It does not block the sun's rays from the entire body of the earth at once, but only from smaller parts, wherever the moon is carried. For when it seems to shadow only a small portion, this is because it is lower... For it falls beneath our eyes at the lowest point, and thus blocks the whole sun. An eclipse of the moon occurs when it falls into the shadow of the earth. The shadow of the earth is produced by the sun, but it is not cylindrical <note>original: "κυλινδρική". A shadow that remains the same width throughout.</note>; it would not have the shape of a column, but rather it is conical <note>original: "κονοειδῆ". A cone-shaped shadow.</note>. For a cone <note>OCR reads "typhon" (whirlwind), but context implies "konos" (cone).</note> is that which begins from a wide base and ends in a sharp point. Such is the shadow of the earth. For when a body is illuminated by a divine light (or any body), the shadow becomes cylindrical [if the bodies are equal], but when the light source is larger, the shadow is conical. Since the sun is larger than the body of the earth, it produces a conical shadow. When, therefore, the moon falls into the earth's shadow, it grows dark and does not shine, and thus appears to undergo an eclipse. When it falls into the beginning [the base] of the shadow, it eclipses completely; but when it is near the apex, it appears fainter because the conical shadow is no longer as wide.

<summary>This page discusses atmospheric "fires" such as meteors (described as "goats" and "torches") and the mechanics of solar and lunar eclipses. Psellos explains shadow geometry, noting that because the sun is larger than the earth, the earth casts a conical rather than a cylindrical shadow.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Aristarchus of Samos, meteorology, solar eclipse, lunar eclipse, geometry of shadows, conical shadow, exhalation, Michael VII Doukas</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues Psellos's exploration of "meteors" or atmospheric and celestial phenomena. Having previously discussed the composition of fires in the upper atmosphere, he now moves into astro-meteorology—how the stars signal seasonal changes—and the orbital mechanics of the planetary spheres, concluding with optical phenomena like rainbows and haloes.</meta>

The summit is shorter:

### Concerning the Heraldic Significance of the Stars and How Many Signs of Winter and Summer We Have

<margin><unclear>...</unclear></margin>
Heraldic signs occur whenever a certain rising star rises together with the sun’s heat—such as the so-called Dog Star <note>original: "κύων" (kyon). This refers to Sirius, the brightest star in the sky, whose "heliacal rising" in summer was traditionally associated with the "dog days" of intense heat.</note>, Orion, and Arcturus, or some other of the fixed stars—altering the air and the four seasons of the year. For regarding all these, it was said: "let them be for signs and for seasons and for days and for years" <note>This is a paraphrase of Genesis 1:14, showing how Psellos integrates Biblical creation with Greek natural philosophy.</note>. For the stars provide signs <term>original: "ἐπισημαίνονται" (episémainontai). In Byzantine science, stars were often viewed as "signs" or heralds of weather rather than the physical causes of it.</term> of the conditions occurring in the air, not by their risings themselves creating the fair weather. Regarding summer and winter and the remaining seasons, it should be known that whenever the sun becomes northern to us and sits above, it produces summer; but when it departs toward the south, it creates winter. And when it is aimed along the meridian, it creates the spring equinox. But when it travels through the earth <note>This likely refers to the sun's position in the lower hemisphere or its path through the southern signs during the colder months.</note> toward the snow, it produces the autumnal equinox again.

### What is the Orbital Period of the Planets

<margin><unclear>...</unclear></margin>
For all the planets, according to the proportion of the size of their spheres, these return after fixed times of their departures, according to their motions. Saturn, being higher than the others and traversing a larger circle, completes its own period through thirty years. Jupiter, being immediately after it, completes its period through twelve years. Because of their four motions, Mars, Venus, and Mercury are equal-running <term>original: "ἰσόδρομος" (isodromos). This refers to the observation in geocentric astronomy that Mercury and Venus always stay near the sun, appearing to complete their orbits in the same one-year timeframe.</term> to the sun, and their period is one year. The moon, however, according to my view, is restored every month and a half <note>The Greek text says "μηνὸς πρὸς τὸ ἥμισυ" (a month and a half). This is an unusual calculation, as the lunar month is typically 29.5 days.</note>. If anyone is more precise with me regarding such numbers, or if perhaps my wisdom is wondered at—how all the ratios of harmony are revealed in such things—it is because the entire body is sent through without deception.

<column-break/>

### Concerning the Rainbow

<margin><unclear>...</unclear></margin>
The rainbow—what is called by many a "bow"—is formed by the ray [of light]. It appears in the ether as an image of the sight being seen, the ray from the eyes traveling toward the sun and then being reflected back <note>Psellos is describing the ancient theory of "extramission," where the eyes were thought to send out rays that bounce off objects (or in this case, moist air) and return to the viewer.</note>, thus completing the shape of the bow. To speak simply, it is a blending of the air according to its shape. Hence, it is sometimes blood-colored and more circular—though it may be either smaller or greater than a semicircle. It appears for the most part after the sun; those from the moon [lunar rainbows] occur mostly at night and most often around the horizon. When appearing around the meridian circle, it often occurs in these ways, and indeed I appear to see the rainbows. Every rainbow is colored thus: for it is made of so many colors—the first and second, green; the third coating is like purple. These consist of uneven and non-uniform shapes.

---

### Concerning the Halo

<margin><unclear>...</unclear></margin>
And that which is formed into a sphere, the halo <note>original: "ἀλώνιον" (alōnion). This word literally means "a small threshing floor," used by the Greeks because the circular glow around the sun or moon resembled the round, flat areas used for treading grain.</note>, exists there by appearance <term>original: "ἔμφασιν" (emphasin). An optical reflection or illusion.</term> rather than by substance <term>original: "ὑπόστασιν" (hypostasin). A physical, underlying reality.</term>. For it has the same properties as the rainbow. For the most part, it appears as a perfect circle. But more rarely, it is smaller than a circle, and I think it does not decrease, but appears around the sun. It occurs at night no less than by day, and around the meridian as a circle. For the most part, it occurs near the horizon. It is always monochromatic, or rather white, and it consists of uniform parts.

<summary>This page describes the heraldic nature of stars like Sirius and Arcturus in signaling seasons, details the orbital periods of the seven planets (noting Saturn's 30-year cycle), and explains the optical reflection theories behind rainbows and haloes.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Sirius, Arcturus, planetary periods, Saturn, Jupiter, equinox, rainbow, halo, reflection, Byzantine science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Psellos’s discussion of atmospheric phenomena, moving from the sun’s seasonal path into the optical science of reflections—explaining how haloes and rainbows form—before transitioning into the "harmony of the spheres" and the complex motions of the planets.</meta>

...on uniform parts, the reflection of sight is cast back from them. It is necessary for the medium in front of the reflection to be dense, smooth, and transparent, so that the images <note>original: "εἴδωλα" (eidōla). In ancient optics, these are the visual "images" or "forms" that travel from an object to the eye or are reflected by a surface.</note> may be formed there. These serve as proofs for the circle of the halo and the shape of the rainbow, through the study of measurement and via that fiery-lighted learning which is perceived by the ear: +

### Concerning Rods: +

The phenomena called "rods" <note>original: "ράβδοι" (rabdoi). In Byzantine meteorology, these are vertical streaks of light or "mock suns" (parhelia) caused by the refraction of sunlight through ice crystals.</note> appearing at the edges are not, we say, like the appearance of crystal or the brilliance surrounding a halo. For they appear in parts and only around the sun; specifically near the horizon when the sun is setting. Mathematically and through the cutting of elevations, they are similar in form to the rainbow. For they are prominent, and he <note>Likely a reference to Aristotle, whose *Meteorology* is the primary source for these theories.</note> says that many rods do not appear in a sequence. Most rods appear because while the rainbow is a quadrant of a circle or [more], the rods are positioned at a specific angle relative to the sun. They themselves also contribute to the production of the sight, reflecting from cloud to cloud; and there, the heat is reflected toward the white part. For whether it reaches the same end or that of the rods—therefore, in all things reflected from the sun, since the sun is small, the white part that appears is called by the same logic, most defensive: +

For since it is not un-composite, it follows this same law, just as a unique characteristic creeps in from its own starting point; for it shows the natural state even more, yet it also aids the reflected part of the sense as it withdraws into a waving motion. And regarding the various dances <note>original: "χορείας" (choreias). A common Platonic metaphor describing the rhythmic, circular orbits of the planets as a celestial dance.</note> of the stars around the center of the planets...

---

<page-num>111</page-num>

...it is observed nearby as if using witnesses, they manage the motion of the fixed sphere <note>original: "ἀπλανοῦ" (aplanou). The *Aplanos* is the outermost sphere of the universe containing the fixed stars, which moves as a single unit.</note> through virtue, characterizing the course of the planets as it is driven out from the rotation. Some interpret the bending of those who look back; others interpret it through omens and incantations. They practice the outflow from the more northerly regions as something gentle. But in the progress of the first motion, that of the planet is far extended, which they indeed call **harmonic** <note>original: "ἁρμονικήν" (harmonikēn). This refers to the "Music of the Spheres," the Pythagorean idea that the proportional distances and speeds of the planets create a divine musical harmony.</note>. For while moving forward in the first instance among the things in front, and then withdrawing again, they escape the determination of the winds. Being joined together in these very things, they overshadow the much-celebrated conjunctions <term>original: "συνόδους" (synodous). The alignment of two or more celestial bodies in the same longitude.</term>. It is not only beautiful but also a solution, and I give form to such a shape of memory for those being called from the cause of rekindling the differences of the soul-breathed things concerning their own snubs, at greater length and with pleasure, the reflections of the circles, the breathing-together <note>original: "σύμπνοιαν" (sympnoian). A Stoic and Neoplatonic concept of "cosmic sympathy," where all parts of the universe are interconnected and "breathe" as one organism.</note> into the same of the moved writing, which they first refined. And let there be other parts of the world-making, an orderly hymn for all these things. And let there be a myth not from the motion itself, which conveys to us the entire outflow in that summer; nor should we be misled by what has been demonstrated previously, even the portions and the exceeding power of those who hold the... which will be demonstrated in the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "μετὰ φυσικὰς" (meta physikas). Psellos is likely referencing Aristotle's *Metaphysics* or his own commentaries on the nature of being that transcends physical study.</note>. They call such a theory an interpretation or a harmony with the path of the heavenly bodies.

### Concerning Orbits: +
